\zeta
from
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
, that is,

E_{\zeta}=\bigcup_{\bar{q}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}}\{q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\|q-\bar{q}\|\leq\zeta\}=\bigcup_{\bar{q}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}}\overline{B}_{\zeta}(\bar{q}).

This operation regularizes
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
in a manner that, in some sense, preserves the qualitative features of the boundary, as shown in Figure
1
.

Remark 11
.

Indeed, in the convex analysis literature, the map sending
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
to
E_{\zeta}
provides a means of defining a weak notion of curvature for arbitrary convex sets, see, for instance,
[
51
, Section 2.6]
and the references cited therein. For the purposes of this paper, the idea to have in mind is that, roughly speaking, the curvature of
E_{\zeta}
converges weakly to the curvature of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
as
\zeta\to 0^{+}
. In terms of the norms
(\varphi_{\zeta})_{\zeta>0}
, Section
3.1
already showed that, in some sense, the Finsler infinity Laplace operators also converge in the limit.

Finally, note that this regularization procedure, which amounts to a geometric form of sup-convolution, is well-known in the viscosity solutions literature, notably in Jensen’s classical work
[
34
]
, the literature on level-set PDE, such as
[
28
]
, and the “easy” proof of uniqueness for the (Euclidean) infinity Laplacian in
[
3
]
.

Figure 1.
The shaded regions are the sets
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
for various choices of
\varphi
, while
E_{\zeta}
is the region bounded by the dashed curves.

The next few results describe basic geometric properties of the set
E_{\zeta}
as it relates to
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
. These were already discovered previously in the convex analysis literature (cf.
[
51
, Section 2.6]
); the proofs are presented for the sake of completeness.

Proposition 6
.

For any
\zeta>0
, the set
E_{\zeta}
has the following properties:

(i)

E_{\zeta}
is a closed convex set of class
C^{1,1}
satisfying a uniform interior ball condition with radius
\zeta
.

(ii)

There is a continuous function
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}:\partial E_{\zeta}\to\{\varphi=1\}
such that, for any
q_{*}\in\partial E_{\zeta}
, the point
\bar{q}_{*}=\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)
is the unique point in
\{\varphi=1\}
closest to
q_{*}
, that is,

\{\bar{q}_{*}\}=\{\bar{q}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}\,\mid\,\|\bar{q}-q_{*}\|=\zeta\}.

(iii)

For any
q\in\partial E_{\zeta}
, if
\bar{q}=\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)
as before, then

\varphi^{*}(q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*})^{-1}(q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*})\in\partial\varphi(\bar{q}_{*}),\quad\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},q_{*}\rangle=\sup\left\{\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in E_{\zeta}\right\}.

At this stage, it will be important to note a certain characterization of convex sets with
C^{1,1}
boundary, which will be used repeatedly throughout the paper. To state, it, first, recall the definition of interior and exterior ball conditions: a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to satisfy a
uniform interior ball condition (resp. uniform exterior ball condition) of radius
\zeta>0
if, for each
x\in\partial A
, there is an
e\in S^{d-1}
such that

\overline{B}_{\zeta}(x-\zeta e)\subseteq\overline{A}\quad\text{(resp.}\quad\overline{B}_{\zeta}(x+\zeta e)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\text{int}(A).\text{)}

The next lemma, which is well-known, asserts that a convex set is of class
C^{1,1}
if and only if it satisfies a uniform interior ball condition.

Lemma 5
.

A compact convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with nonempty interior is a submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with boundary of class
C^{1,1}
if and only if there is a
\zeta>0
such that
C
satisfies a uniform interior ball condition of radius
\zeta
.

The lemma follows from a similar characterization of domains of class
C^{1,1}
, made precise, for instance, in
[
40
]
. Hence only a short proof will be given. Nonetheless, here and later in the paper, the notion of supporting hyperplane will be needed. Recall that if
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
e\in S^{d-1}
, then
A
is said to possess a
supporting hyperplane
with normal
e
at a point
x\in\partial A
if the following inclusion holds:

\displaystyle A\subseteq\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle y-x,e\rangle\leq 0\}.

Proof.

The lemma follows from a well-known fact about submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of class
C^{1,1}
(cf.
[
40
]
): in particular, a bounded set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
d
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with boundary of class
C^{1,1}
if and only if there is a
\zeta>0
such that
A
satisfies both an interior and an exterior ball condition of radius
\zeta
. What makes the convex case different is the existence of supporting hyperplanes. Indeed, a supporting hyperplane is, in effect, an exterior ball of radius
r=+\infty
, hence the exterior ball condition is redundant. ∎

Finally, in the analysis of the geometry of
E_{\zeta}
, it will be useful to keep in mind the following elementary observation about convex sets and their supporting hyperplanes:

Lemma 6
.

Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be convex and suppose that
q_{*}\notin\overline{E}
. Given any
\bar{q}_{*}\in\partial E
, the equality
\|q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*}\|=\text{dist}(q_{*},E)
holds if and only if

\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},\bar{q}_{*}\rangle=\sup\left\{\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},\bar{q}\rangle\,\mid\,\bar{q}\in\overline{E}\right\}.

In particular, there is a unique such point
\bar{q}_{*}
.

Proof.

The “if” direction is a consequence of orthogonality. See
[
51
, Lemma 1.3.1]
for the proof of the “only if” direction. ∎

Proof of Proposition
6
.

To see that (i) holds, first, observe that
E_{\zeta}
is convex. Indeed, suppose that
q_{1},q_{2}\in E_{\zeta}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
. By construction, there are points
\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}
and
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\overline{B}_{\zeta}
such that
q_{i}=\bar{q}_{i}+\xi_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. From this, one decomposes the convex combination
(1-\lambda)q_{1}+\lambda q_{2}
as

(1-\lambda)q_{1}+\lambda q_{2}=[(1-\lambda)\bar{q}_{1}+\lambda\bar{q}_{2}]+[(1-\lambda)\xi_{1}+\lambda\xi_{2}].

Since
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
and
\overline{B}_{\zeta}
are both convex, it follows that
(1-\lambda)q_{1}+\lambda q_{2}\in E_{\zeta}
, hence
E_{\zeta}
is convex.

Next, here is the proof that
E_{\zeta}
satisfies a uniform interior ball condition with radius
\zeta
, and, thus, is of class
C^{1,1}
. By definition, if
q_{*}\in\partial E_{\zeta}
, then there is a
\bar{q}_{*}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}
such that
\|q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*}\|=\zeta
. From this, one readily deduces that
\overline{B}_{\zeta}(q_{*}-\zeta e)=\overline{B}_{\zeta}(\bar{q}_{*})\subseteq E_{\zeta}
with
e=\|q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*}\|^{-1}(q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*})
.

Next, turn to (ii). By Lemma
6
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there is a unique
\bar{q}_{x}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}
such that
\|x-\bar{q}_{x}\|=\text{dist}(x,\{\varphi\leq 1\})
. It is also easy to check that
\bar{q}_{x}\in\{\varphi=1\}
if
\varphi(x)>1
. In particular, the function
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}:\partial E_{\zeta}\to\{\varphi=1\}
given by setting
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)=\bar{q}_{q}
is well-defined. Due to the fact that
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is compact, uniqueness readily implies continuity.

Finally, consider (iii). Let
\bar{q}_{*}=\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q_{*})
. A classical argument establishes that
\varphi^{*}(q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*})^{-1}(q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*})\in\partial\varphi(\bar{q}_{*})
. Indeed, were this not the case, it would be possible to invoke (
20
) and (
21
) to find a
\bar{q}_{**}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}
such that

\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},\bar{q}_{**}\rangle>\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},\bar{q}_{*}\rangle.

However, this would contradict Lemma
6
.

It only remains to show that

(36)

\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},q_{*}\rangle=\sup\left\{\langle q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*},q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\partial E_{\zeta}\right\}.

To see this, first, note that if
N_{\partial E_{\zeta}}(q_{*})\in S^{d-1}
is the outward normal vector to
\partial E_{\zeta}
at
q_{*}
, then, by convexity,
N_{\partial E_{\zeta}}(q_{*})
determines a supporting hyperplane:

(37)

\langle N_{\partial E_{\zeta}}(q_{*}),q_{*}\rangle=\sup\left\{\langle N_{\partial E_{\zeta}},q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in E_{\zeta}\right\}.

At the same time, as observed already, there holds
\overline{B}_{\zeta}(\bar{q}_{*})\subseteq E_{\zeta}
and
q_{*}\in\partial B_{\zeta}(\bar{q}_{*})\cap\partial E_{\zeta}
, i.e., the ball
\overline{B}_{\zeta}(\bar{q}_{*})
is tangent to
E_{\zeta}
from the inside. It necessarily follows that
N_{\partial E_{\zeta}}(q_{*})
equals the normal vector to
\partial B_{\zeta}(\bar{q}_{*})
, that is,
N_{\partial E_{\zeta}}(q_{*})=\|q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*}\|^{-1}(q_{*}-\bar{q}_{*})
. Combining this with (
37
) yields (
36
). ∎

In the proof of Theorem
5
, it will be useful to know that the map
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
, which is not invertible in general, nonetheless has an explicit multivalued inverse.

Proposition 7
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
be the map defined in Proposition
6
. Then, for any
\bar{q}\in\{\varphi=1\}
,

\{q\in\partial E_{\zeta}\,\mid\,\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)=\bar{q}\}=\bar{q}+\left\{\frac{\zeta p}{\|p\|}\,\mid\,p\in\partial\varphi(\bar{q})\right\}

Proof.

Suppose that
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}^{-1}(\bar{q})
. By Proposition
6
, (iii), the vector
p=\varphi^{*}(q-\bar{q})^{-1}(q-\bar{q})
is an element of
\partial\varphi(\bar{q})
. At the same time,
\|q-\bar{q}\|=\zeta
. Thus, since
\varphi^{*}(q-\bar{q})>0
, it follows that
q=\bar{q}+\varphi^{*}(q-\bar{q})p=\bar{q}+\zeta\|p\|^{-1}p
. This proves one of the necessary inclusions.

Conversely, given a
p\in\partial\varphi(\bar{q})
, it remains to show that the vector
q=\bar{q}+\zeta\|p\|^{-1}p
is an element of the set
\{\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}=\bar{q}\}
. It is clear that
\|q-\bar{q}\|=\zeta
so
q\in E_{\zeta}
. At the same time,

\displaystyle\langle q-\bar{q},\bar{q}\rangle=\frac{\zeta}{\|p\|}\langle p,\bar{q}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta}{\|p\|}\sup\left\{\langle p,\bar{q}_{1}\rangle\,\mid\,\bar{q}_{1}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\langle q-\bar{q},\bar{q}_{1}\rangle\,\mid\,\bar{q}_{1}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}\right\}.

Thus, by Lemma
6
,
\|q-\bar{q}\|=\inf\left\{\|q-\bar{q}_{1}\|\,\mid\,\bar{q}_{1}\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}\right\}
. This proves
q\in\partial E_{\zeta}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)=\bar{q}
. ∎

3.3.
Construction of
\varphi_{\zeta}

Given
E_{\zeta}
as defined in the previous section, let
\varphi_{\zeta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
be the associated Minkowski gauge, that is, the unique Finsler norm such that
\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}=E_{\zeta}
. Recall that the gauge is determined by the following formula:

(38)

\varphi_{\zeta}(x)=\inf\left\{\alpha>0\,\mid\,\alpha^{-1}x\in E_{\zeta}\right\}.

See, for instance,
[
48
, Chapter 5]
or
[
17
, Section 1.2]
for a proof that (
38
) defines a Finsler norm; uniqueness follows immediately from positive homogeneity.

Remark 12
.

At this stage, it is worth considering what happens when
\varphi(q)=\alpha^{-1}\|q\|
for some constant
\alpha>0
. In this case, the unit ball
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is nothing but the ball
\overline{B}_{\alpha}(0)
, and then it is easy to verify that
E_{\zeta}=\overline{B}_{\alpha+\zeta}(0)
for any
\zeta>0
. In particular, this proves that

\varphi_{\zeta}=\frac{1}{\alpha+\zeta}\|\cdot\|\quad\text{if}\quad\varphi=\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\cdot\|.

The key ingredient in Theorem
5
is the subject of the next proposition:

Proposition 8
.

If
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, then

\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)=\partial\varphi^{*}(p)+\frac{\zeta p}{\|p\|}.

In the proof, it will be useful to recall that
\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}
has a geometric representation:

(39)

\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)=\left\{q\in\partial E_{\zeta}\,\mid\,\langle q,p\rangle=\max_{q^{\prime}\in\partial E_{\zeta}}\langle q^{\prime},p\rangle\right\}.

This is nothing but a recapitulation of (
20
).

Proof.

First, note that if
q\in\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)
, then (
39
) implies that, at the level of the normal vector
N_{\partial E_{\zeta}}(q)
to
\partial E_{\zeta}
at
q
, there holds

\frac{p}{\|p\|}=N_{\partial E_{\zeta}}(q)=\frac{D\varphi_{\zeta}(q)}{\|D\varphi_{\zeta}(q)\|}.

Thus, by Proposition
6
, (iii),

(40)

q-\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)=\frac{\zeta p}{\|p\|},\quad\varphi^{*}(p)^{-1}p\in\partial\varphi(\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)).

By duality, the second identity can be rewritten as
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
. Indeed, by (
21
) and zero-homogeneity,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(q)

\displaystyle\in\partial\varphi^{*}(\varphi^{*}(p)^{-1}p)=\partial\varphi^{*}(p).

Since
q
is an arbitrary element of
\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)
, this yields

\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)\subseteq\partial\varphi^{*}(p)+\frac{\zeta p}{\|p\|}.

Conversely, if
\bar{q}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
, then
\varphi^{*}(p)^{-1}p\in\partial\varphi(\bar{q})
and Proposition
7
implies that

\bar{q}+\frac{\zeta p}{\|p\|}\in\partial E_{\zeta}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}^{-1}(\bar{q}).

By Proposition
6
, (iii) and (
39
), the inclusion
\bar{q}+\frac{\zeta p}{\|p\|}\in\partial\varphi^{*}_{\zeta}(p)
holds. Since
\bar{q}
was arbitrary, this gives the remaining inclusion:

\partial\varphi^{*}(p)+\frac{\zeta p}{\|p\|}\subseteq\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p).

∎

To conclude the proof of Theorem
5
, it remains to verify the remaining properties of
\varphi_{\zeta}
. That is accomplished in the following two propositions.

Proposition 9
.

\varphi_{\zeta}\to\varphi
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
\zeta\to 0^{+}
. Furthermore, there are constants
C(\varphi),\mu(\varphi)>0
such that, for any
\zeta\in(0,1)
,

(41)

\displaystyle\mu(\varphi)\leq\|D\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\|\leq C(\varphi)\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}\subseteq(1+C(\varphi)\zeta)\{\varphi\leq 1\}.

Proof.

First, recall that
\{\varphi\leq 1\}\subseteq\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}
for any
\zeta>0
. This implies
\varphi_{\zeta}\leq\varphi
by positive one-homogeneity.

Define
C(\varphi)=\max\{\varphi(y)\,\mid\,\|y\|=1\}
, and note that

B_{1}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq C(\varphi)\}=C(\varphi)\{\varphi\leq 1\}.

Thus, for any
\zeta>0
, there holds
E_{\zeta}\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}+\overline{B}_{\zeta}(0)\subseteq(1+C(\varphi)\zeta)\{\varphi\leq 1\}
. Since
\varphi_{\zeta}
is positively one-homogeneous, it follows that, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\zeta\in(0,1)
,

(1+C(\varphi)\zeta)^{-1}\min\left\{\varphi(y)\,\mid\,\|y\|=1\right\}\|x\|\leq\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\leq\varphi(x)\leq C(\varphi)\|x\|.

From this and the identity
\varphi_{\zeta}(x)=\langle D\varphi_{\zeta}(x),x\rangle
, one deduces that

(1+C(\varphi)\zeta)^{-1}\min\left\{\varphi(y)\,\mid\,\|y\|=1\right\}\leq\|D\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\|\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}.

Similarly, since
\langle D\varphi_{\zeta}(x),y\rangle\leq\varphi_{\zeta}(y)
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,

\|D\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\|\leq C(\varphi)\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}.

This proves (
41
) holds, and it shows that
(\varphi_{\zeta})_{\zeta>0}
is uniformly Lipschitz.

By the Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem, up to passing to a subsequence, there is a Finsler norm
\tilde{\varphi}
such that
\varphi_{\zeta}\to\tilde{\varphi}
locally uniformly as
\zeta\to 0^{+}
. The previous considerations show that, for any
\zeta\in(0,1)
,

\{\varphi\leq 1\}\subseteq\{\tilde{\varphi}\leq 1\}\subseteq(1+C(\varphi)\zeta)\{\varphi\leq 1\}.

Therefore,
\{\varphi\leq 1\}=\{\tilde{\varphi}\leq 1\}
, which means
\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi
. Since the subsequence was arbitrary, this proves
\varphi_{\zeta}\to\varphi
locally uniformly. ∎

Proposition 10
.

There is a
\kappa(\varphi)>0
such that, for any
\zeta\in(0,1)
,

\varphi^{*}(D\varphi_{\zeta}(x))\geq\kappa(\varphi)\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}.

Proof.

Since
\varphi^{*}
is a Finsler norm, there is a constant
m(\varphi^{*})>0
such that

\varphi^{*}(p)\geq m(\varphi^{*})\|p\|\quad\text{for each}\quad p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Thus, the desired conclusion follows from Proposition
9
.
∎

Finally, Theorem
5
is proved by combining the previous propositions.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

Concerning (i), recall that
E_{\zeta}=\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}
by definition. Thus, this is immediate from Proposition
6
.

Next, statements (ii), (iii), and (iv) were proved in Propositions
8
,
9
, and
10
, respectively.

Finally, concerning (v), it is clear that
0\in\{\varphi<1\}\subseteq\{\varphi_{\zeta}<1\}
, hence there is a
\delta>0
such that
\overline{B}_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}
for all
\zeta>0
. By boundedness, there is an
R>0
such that
\{\varphi\leq 1\}\subseteq\overline{B}_{R}(0)
, hence Proposition
9
implies that
\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}\subseteq B_{CR}(0)
for some
C>0
independent of
\zeta\in(0,1)
. Thus, up to decreasing
\delta
, one has
\overline{B}_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}\subseteq\overline{B}_{\delta^{-1}}(0)
for all
\zeta\in(0,1)
. ∎

4.
Conical Test Functions

This section describes the test functions used in the proofs of
C^{1,1}
cone comparison principles, such as the one encountered in Section
3.1
. The test functions in question are positively one-homogeneous, nonnegative convex functions — hence their domains are cones — so they will be referred to henceforth as
conical test functions
.

4.1.
Faces of Convex Sets

The construction of conical test functions is based on the notion of a
face
of a convex set. That concept will be reviewed here. For more information, see the monographs by Rockafellar
[
50
]
or Schneider
[
51
]
.

Before stating the definition, it is necessary to first define the relative interior and boundary of a convex set. Observe that if
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a convex set, then there is a unique smallest linear subspace
V_{C}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

C\subseteq x+V_{C}\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in C.

The dimension of
C
is then defined to coincide with that of
V_{C}
,
\text{dim}(C):=\text{dim}(V_{C})
.

Definition 5
.

Given a convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the
relative interior

\text{rint}(C)
is the interior of
C
relative to the subspace
V_{C}
, that is,
x\in\text{rint}(C)
if and only if there is an
r>0
such that

B_{r}(x)\cap(x+V_{C})\subseteq C.

The
relative boundary

\text{bdry}(C)
of
C
is the boundary of
C
relative to
V_{C}
:

\text{bdry}(C)=\overline{C}\setminus\text{rint}(C).

Roughly speaking, the faces of a convex set
C
are the convex subsets that are maximal with respect to the operation
\text{rint}(\cdot)
. The definition given next will be useful for us, but is different than that in
[
50
]
; however, see Theorem 18.1 in that reference for the equivalence of the two formulations.

Definition 6
.

Given a convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a convex subset
F\subseteq C
is called a
face
of
C
if it has the following property: for any other convex subset
F^{\prime}\subseteq C
, if
\text{rint}(F^{\prime})\cap F
is nonempty, then
F^{\prime}\subseteq F
.

It is worth noting that the (relative interiors of) faces of a convex set form a partition: that is, if
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is convex, then

(42)

C=\bigcup\left\{\text{rint}(F)\,\mid\,F\,\,\text{face of}\,\,C\right\}

and
\text{rint}(F_{1})\cap\text{rint}(F_{2})=\emptyset
if
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are distinct faces of
C
. See
[
50
, Theorem 18.2]
for more details.

The easiest example of faces are the so-called
exposed faces
. These are the faces determined through linear optimization.

Definition 7
.

Given a closed convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a subset
F\subseteq C
is called an
exposed face
if there is a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

F=\left\{x\in C\,\mid\,\langle x,p\rangle=\max_{y\in C}\,\langle y,p\rangle\right\}.

It is not hard to show that an exposed face is, in fact, a face. In particular, if
\varphi
is a Finsler norm, then, by (
20
), the subdifferential
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
is an exposed face of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Clearly, every exposed face of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
takes this form.

It is important to note that, in general, a face
F
of a convex set
C
need
not
be an exposed face. This is actually very relevant to the approach taken in this paper — non-exposed faces cause difficulties that significantly complicate the proofs, as indicated in Remark
16
. An example of a face that is not exposed is described next.

Remark 13
.

Consider the convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by

C=([0,1]\times[0,1])\cup\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\mid\,1\leq x\leq 2,\,\,0\leq y\leq\sqrt{1-(x-1)^{2}}\right\}.

The segment
[0,1]\times\{1\}
is an exposed face. At the same time, the singleton
\{(1,1)\}
is a face, but it is not exposed (since
\partial C
is differentiable there).

Remark 14
.

More generally, given any Finsler norm
\varphi
, if
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is of class
C^{1}
but not strictly convex — so it contains at least one nontrivial exposed face — then it necessarily has a face that is not exposed. Indeed, for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, the set
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
is a face of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
. If
\#\partial\varphi^{*}(p)>1
, then the differentiability of the boundary
\{\varphi=1\}
implies that every nontrivial exposed face of
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
is a non-exposed face of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
.

4.2.
A Motivating Example

In order to motivate the conical test functions defined below, first, consider the following example of their use.

Specifically, this subsection revisits the case of Finsler norms
\varphi
for which the ball
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is
C^{1,1}
and strictly convex.
18
18

18

Recall that a convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
strictly convex
if
\partial C
contains no line segments.
In this case, the strict convexity of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
implies that
\partial\varphi^{*}
is single-valued away from the origin and, thus,

G_{\varphi}^{*}\equiv G^{\varphi}_{*}\quad\text{in}\,\,(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathcal{S}_{d}

so the
\varphi
-infinity Laplacian is continuous, at least where the gradient is nonzero. While in this setting the comparison principle is well-understood,
19
19

19

See the discussion in
[
42
]
, particularly Proposition 1 therein.
this section presents a different, elementary approach.

Proposition 11
.

If
\varphi
is a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is both
C^{1,1}
and strictly convex, then
\text{Sub}_{\varphi}(U)=CCA_{\varphi}(U)
for any open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

To reiterate, the above result is already known, as discussed, for instance, in
[
10
]
and
[
42
]
. Nonetheless, since
\varphi
is only assumed to be
C^{1,1}
and not
C^{2}
, the known proof involves some form of the maximum principle for semicontinuous functions, see
[
24
, Appendix A]
or
[
42
, Proof of Theorem 6]
. By contrast, the argument detailed next only uses elementary calculus and convex analysis.

As in the last section, the interior ball condition afforded by
C^{1,1}
regularity plays an essential role. In view of Lemma
5
, since the set
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is of class
C^{1,1}
, there is a radius
\zeta>0
such that, given any
q\in\{\varphi=1\}
, if
e\in S^{d-1}
is the outward normal vector to
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
at
q
, then

(43)

\overline{B}_{r}(q-\zeta e)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\},\quad\partial B_{r}(q-\zeta e)\cap\{\varphi\leq 1\}=\{q\}.

Hence although
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
need not be smooth at
q
, it can be touched from the inside at
q
by a convex set that
is
.

At the level of the norm
\varphi
, this implies a similar statement. In particular, there is a function
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
that is smooth in a neighborhood of the ray
\mathcal{C}(\{q\})
through
q
and for which there holds

(44)

\displaystyle\varphi\leq\psi_{e,q,\zeta}\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\text{and}

(45)

\displaystyle\varphi(x)=\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x)\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathcal{C}(\{q\}).

Specifically, let
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
be the Minkowski gauge of the ball
\overline{B}_{\zeta}(q-\zeta e)
, that is, the convex function defined by

\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x)=\inf\left\{\alpha>0\,\mid\,\alpha^{-1}x\in\overline{B}_{\zeta}(q-\zeta e)\right\},

taken with the convention that
\inf\emptyset=+\infty
. See Figure
2
for a depiction of this construction.

0
q
B_{\zeta}(q-\zeta e)
e
0
q
e

Figure 2.
The ball
B_{\zeta}(q-\zeta e)
is shown on the left. The level sets of the Minkowski gauge
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
are shown on the right. Note that
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
is infinite outside of the cone indicated by the dashed lines.

As in the construction of
\varphi_{\zeta}
in Section
3.3
, the function
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
is convex and positively one-homogeneous. Note that (
44
) and (
45
) follow immediately from (
43
), the definition of
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
, and positive one-homogeneity.

While the domain
\{\psi_{e,q,\zeta}<+\infty\}
may be a proper subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the function
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
is nonetheless smooth close to
q
.

Lemma 7
.

The function
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
is smooth in a neighborhood of the ray
\mathcal{C}(\{q\})
.

Proof.

The lemma follows, in effect, from the fact that, at least in a small neighborhood of
q
, the level set
\{\psi_{e,q,\zeta}=1\}
equals a sphere; see Proposition
13
below for a detailed proof of a more general statement. ∎

Since
q
was arbitrary, the lemma implies that at any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, it is always possible to find a nice convex function that touches the norm
\varphi
from above at
x
, a fact that leads to an easy proof of comparison with cones. In other words, what has been done so far already very nearly proves Proposition
2
, which was stated in the introduction and is reproduced here for the reader’s convenience:

See
2

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Define
q=\varphi(x)^{-1}x
so that
q\in\{\varphi=1\}
and
x\in\mathcal{C}(\{q\})
, and let
e=\|D\varphi(q)\|^{-1}D\varphi(q)
. Define
\psi_{x}
by
\psi_{x}(y)=\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(y)
, where
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
is the positively homogeneous convex function defined in the preceding discussion. Condition (i) holds by (
44
) and (
45
), and condition (ii), by Lemma
7
. Therefore, it only remains to check (iii).

Since
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is strictly convex, the proof of (iii) amounts to a bit of a tautology. By the positive one-homogeneity of
\psi_{e,q,\zeta}
, the identity
D^{2}\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(q)q=0
holds, and, thus, again by homogeneity,

D^{2}\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x)q=\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x)^{-1}D^{2}\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(q)q=0.

At the same time, since
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is strictly convex, (
20
) implies that the set function
\partial\varphi^{*}
is single-valued away from the origin. Taken together with the fact that
D\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x)=D\varphi(x)
by (
44
) and (
45
), this yields

\partial\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x))=\{D\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x))\}=\{D\varphi^{*}(D\varphi(x))\}=\{q\}.

Invoking the definition of
G_{\varphi}^{*}
and
G_{*}^{\varphi}
, one concludes

G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x),D^{2}\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x))=G_{*}^{\varphi}(D\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x),D^{2}\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x))=\langle D^{2}\psi_{e,q,\zeta}(x)q,q\rangle=0.

∎

As already indicated in the introduction, Proposition
2
readily leads to an easy proof of cone comparison, as shown next.

Proof of Proposition
11
.

By Lemma
2
, to prove that
\text{Sub}_{\varphi}(U)\subseteq CCA_{\varphi}(U)
, it suffices to show that if
V
is a bounded open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
\epsilon>0
, and
u
is an upper semicontinuous function in
\overline{V}
such that
\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\varphi^{*}(Du)^{2}-G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u)\leq 0
in
V
, then

\max\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\}=\max\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\partial V\}.

The proof proceeds by contradiction: suppose that there is an
x\in V
such that

(46)

u(x)-\varphi(x)=\max\{u(y)-\varphi(y)\,\mid\,y\in\partial V\}.

Since
x\in V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, Proposition
2
implies there is a function
\psi_{x}
, which is smooth in a neighborhood of
x
, such that

(47)

\displaystyle\varphi\leq\psi_{x}\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\varphi(x)=\psi_{x}(x),

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\psi_{x}(x),D^{2}\psi_{x}(x))=G_{*}^{\varphi}(D\psi_{x}(x),D^{2}\psi_{x}(x))=0.

Combining (
46
) and (
47
), one deduces that

\displaystyle u\leq(u(x)-\varphi(x))+\varphi\leq(u(x)-\varphi(x))+\psi_{x}\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{V},

\displaystyle u(x)=(u(x)-\varphi(x))+\varphi(x)=(u(x)-\varphi(x))+\psi_{x}(x).

In particular,
u-\psi_{x}
has a local maximum at
x
. By the subsolution property of
u
, this implies

\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\varphi^{*}(D\varphi(x))=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{x}(x))^{2}-G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\psi_{x}(x),D^{2}\psi_{x}(x))\leq 0.

Yet (
19
) implies that
\varphi^{*}(D\varphi(x))=1
, so this leads to the inequality
0<\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\leq 0
, which is absurd. ∎

Finally, note that Proposition
1
has not been used at all here. Nonetheless, as explained in the next remark, the arguments above should be understood as a stronger version of that proposition.

Remark 15
.

Notice that the test functions
\{\psi_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}
can be used to prove that
-G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\varphi,D^{2}\varphi)\geq 0
holds in the viscosity sense in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Indeed, if
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
\psi
is a smooth test function such that
\varphi-\psi
has a local minimum at
x
, then
\psi_{x}-\psi
also has a local minimum at
x
, hence, by the second derivative test and ellipticity,

-G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\psi(x),D^{2}\psi(x))\geq-G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\psi_{x}(x),D^{2}\psi_{x}(x))=0.

In this sense, Proposition
2
is a stronger version of Proposition
1
in the special case when
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is strictly convex and
C^{1,1}
.

4.3.
Conical Test Functions

Extending the argument of the previous subsection to more general Finsler norms — and, in particular, the discontinuous case where
G_{\varphi}^{*}\not\equiv G_{*}^{\varphi}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathcal{S}_{d}
— will require some work. The first step is to generate more test functions.

As in the last subsection, it is convenient to retain the assumption that
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is of class
C^{1,1}
. Hence, in particular, throughout the remainder of this section, fix a
\zeta>0
and assume that
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
satisfies an interior ball condition of radius
\zeta
.

Given a closed convex set
F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a direction
e\in S^{d-1}
, and a positive number
\nu>0
, define another convex set
F_{(e,\nu)}
by

(48)

F_{(e,\nu)}=\bigcup_{x\in F}\overline{B}_{\nu}(x-\nu e).

Note that
F_{(e,\nu)}
is closed and convex, and it has nonempty interior if
F
is nonempty. Correspondingly, let
\psi_{e,F,\nu}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
be the Minkowski gauge of
F_{(e,\nu)}
, that is,
20
20

20

Here
\inf\emptyset=+\infty
as before.

(49)

\psi_{e,F,\nu}(x)=\inf\left\{\alpha>0\,\mid\,\alpha^{-1}x\in F_{(e,\nu)}\right\}.

This extends the previous definition, where
F
was a singleton.

The utility of these test functions is demonstrated in the two propositions that follow. In the first of the two,
F
is taken to be one of the exposed faces of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
, hence a set of the form
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
for some
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proposition 12
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
satisfies a uniform interior ball condition of radius
\zeta
. For any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and any
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
, if
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
, then

\displaystyle\varphi\leq\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d},

(50)

\displaystyle\varphi(x)=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}(x)\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)).

Further, if
\nu<\zeta
, then the contact set equals
\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
, i.e.,

(51)

\{\varphi=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}\}=\{\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\zeta}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}<+\infty\}=\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)).

The relatively routine proof is deferred to the end of this section.

The next result holds more generally provided
F
is contained in a
(d-1)
-dimensional affine subspace that avoids the origin, which, in view of (
20
), is certainly the case if
F=\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
for some
p\neq 0
. It is stated in this more general form for clarity.

Proposition 13
.

Suppose that
F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a closed convex set,
e\in S^{d-1}
, and
\nu>0
. If there is a constant
c>0
such that

(52)

F\subseteq\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle=c\}

and if
q_{*}\in\text{rint}(F)
, then
\psi_{e,F,\nu}
is smooth in a neighborhood of the ray
\mathcal{C}(\{q_{*}\})
and

D^{2}\psi_{e,F,\nu}(q_{*})q=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F.

Once again, the proof is deferred to the end of the section. (The impatient reader may be relieved to learn that Proposition
13
will not be used in later sections and, in particular, is not needed for the proof of any of the theorems.)

4.4.
Application to Cone Comparison

Here is the application of the previous two propositions to cone comparison, at a first glance. This is only a partial result, which is limited by the lack of (global) smoothness of the test functions. It is nonetheless presented here to motivate the construction so far, setting the scene for the complete treatment in Section
6
.

Proposition 14
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which the unit ball
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is of class
C^{1,1}
. Fix an open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and an
\epsilon>0
, and suppose that
u\in USC(U)
satisfies

\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\varphi^{*}(Du)^{2}-G^{*}_{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u)\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U.

If there is a bounded open set
V\subseteq U\setminus\{0\}
and an
x_{*}\in V
such that

u(x_{*})-\varphi(x_{*})=\max\{u(y)-\varphi(y)\,\mid\,y\in\overline{V}\},

then the point
\varphi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
does not belong to the relative interior of any of the exposed faces of
\varphi
, that is,

(53)

x_{*}\notin\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\text{rint}(\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))).

Proof.

Let
q_{*}=\varphi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
and fix a
\zeta>0
such that
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
satisfies a interior ball condition of radius
\zeta
. Argue by contradiction: assume there is a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
such that

q_{*}\in\text{rint}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)),

which is equivalent to
x_{*}\in\text{rint}(\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)))
.
Setting
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
and applying (
50
), the test function
\psi:=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\zeta}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
x
and
u-\psi
has a local maximum at
x
. Thus, by Proposition
13
, the subsolution property of
u
, and (
19
),

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\varphi^{*}(D\psi(x))^{2}-G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\psi(x),D^{2}\psi(x))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\varphi^{*}(D\varphi(x))^{2}-\max\left\{\langle D^{2}\psi(x)q,q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\right\}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon,

which contradicts the fact that
\epsilon>0
. ∎

4.5.
Extending the Argument

The conclusion (
53
) just slightly misses the mark. If the relative interior
\text{rint}(\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)))
could be replaced by
\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
in Proposition
13
, then the proof of cone comparison for
C^{1,1}
norms would be complete, as (
24
) asserts that this family of cones covers
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Unfortunately, there is no easy way to improve Proposition
13
. If the relative boundary of
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
is nonempty, then
\text{bdry}(\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
is nonempty and, as will become clear shortly,
\psi
is not twice differentiable at any of those points: the second derivative jumps! Hence
\psi
cannot be naïvely treated as a test function.

Remark 16
.

If the relative boundary of
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
is nonempty, then one might be tempted to “iterate:” if the contact point
x_{*}
is such that
q_{*}:=\varphi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}\in\text{bdry}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
, then one is tempted to search for a
p^{\prime}\neq p
such that
q_{*}\in\text{rint}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p^{\prime}))
. If this were possible, then the appropriate thing to do would be to work with the conical test function associated with
\partial\varphi^{*}(p^{\prime})
rather than
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
. However, this is not possible in general. While it is true that there is a unique face
F
of
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
such that
q_{*}\in\text{rint}(F)
, as already observed in Remark
14
, the
C^{1,1}
regularity of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
implies that
F
is
not
an exposed face of
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
, or, in other words,
F\notin\{\partial\varphi^{*}(p^{\prime})\,\mid\,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\}
.

Even though conical test functions are not smooth in general, there is reason for hope. For any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and any
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
, the conical test function
\psi=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\zeta}
has two advantageous properties:

(i)

Even though
\psi
may not be smooth at relative boundary points, the differential inequality
-G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\psi,D^{2}\psi)\geq 0
holds in the viscosity sense at each point of
\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
. More precisely, if
\tilde{\psi}
is a smooth test function touching
\psi
from below at some point
x\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
, then
21
21

21

This can be proved by generalizing the argument of Proposition
1
suitably to arbitrary positively one-homogeneous convex functions. The details are omitted.

-G_{\varphi}^{*}(D\tilde{\psi}(x),D^{2}\tilde{\psi}(x))\geq 0.

This indicates that nothing is lost passing from
\varphi
to the conical test function
\psi
: on the contact set
\{\psi=\varphi\}
, the relevant viscosity-thereotic properties (Proposition
1
) are retained, even at singular points.

(ii)

The “shape” of
\psi
is very simple. More precisely, the jumps of
D^{2}\psi
are relatively well-behaved. Since
\psi
is positively one-homogeneous, this is easiest to comprehend by studying the level set
\{\psi=1\}
of
\psi
. It turns out that it is easy to write the level set as a graph in a very concrete way; this is explained in the next subsection and will be used extensively in Section
6
.

As will be justified below, points (i) and (ii) show that, in passing from an arbitrary
C^{1,1}
Finsler norm to a conical test function, there is a reduction in complexity. At the level of the geometry, one passes from the arbitrary
C^{1,1}
surface
\{\varphi=1\}
to one that can be written explicitly as a graph, while, where the PDE is concerned, the local structure is retained. In effect, the approach of this paper is to treat conical test functions as “flexible building blocks” for building up cone comparison principles.

4.6.
Graphical Representation of
F_{(e,\nu)}

In the previous subsection, it was suggested that the conical test functions, which are not smooth in their domains, nonetheless are quite amenable to geometric arguments. This subsection presents an alternative graphical description of
F_{(e,\nu)}
, which makes the structure of the jumps of the Hessian tractable.

Suppose, as in Proposition
13
, that
F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a convex set for which there is an
e\in S^{d-1}
and a
c>0
such that

F\subseteq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle=c\right\}.

Define
G\subseteq\{e\}^{\perp}
by projecting
F
orthogonally, that is.,

G=\left\{x^{\prime}\in\{e\}^{\perp}\,\mid\,x^{\prime}+ce\in F\right\}=F-ce.

Here and in Section
6
below, to lighten the notation,
\{e\}^{\perp}
will be identified with
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
; the prime notation, such as
x^{\prime}
, will be reserved for points in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.

As before, define
F_{(e,\nu)}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
according to (
48
).

Let
\text{dist}(\cdot,G):\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
denote the distance function to
G
, i.e.,

\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)=\inf\left\{\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|\,\mid\,y^{\prime}\in G\right\}.

Next, for a given
\nu>0
, define
g_{\nu}:\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(54)

g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})=c-\nu+\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)^{2}}.

It is straightforward to check that the epigraph of
g_{\nu}
parametrizes the “upper half” of
F_{(e,\nu)}
in the following sense:

\displaystyle F_{(e,\nu)}\cap\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle\geq c-\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{x^{\prime}+se\,\mid\,c-\nu\leq s\leq g_{\nu}(x^{\prime}),\,\,\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)\leq\nu\right\},

\displaystyle\partial F_{(e,\nu)}\cap\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle\geq c-\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=\{x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e\,\mid\,\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)\leq\nu\},

In particular,

\displaystyle F=\{x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e\,\mid\,x^{\prime}\in G\}=G+ce.

The representation of
F_{(e,\nu)}
in terms of
g_{\nu}
is convenient for a simple reason. It makes the analysis of the singular points of
\partial F_{(e,\nu)}
tractable; see Figure
3
for a plot of the gradient
D\text{dist}(\cdot,G)
of the distance function to
G
and the discontinuities of the Hessian. In particular, when it comes time to improve Proposition
14
, it will become clear that a manageable way to achieve this is through a perturbation argument involving mollification of
g_{\nu}
.

Figure 3.
The filled regions show two possible choices of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The arrows indicate the gradient of
\text{dist}(\cdot,G)
, while the dashed segments delineate jumps of the Hessian.

4.7.
Proofs of Propositions
12
and
13

This section concludes with the two remaining proofs.

Proof of Proposition
12
.

To lighten the notation, let
F=\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
and
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
. By (
20
), the outward normal vector to
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
equals
e
at each point of
F
. Thus, since, by hypothesis,
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
satisfies an interior ball condition of radius
\zeta
, for any
q\in F
, there holds

\overline{B}_{\zeta}(q-\zeta e)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}.

In particular,
F_{(e,\zeta)}\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}
by definition. After a straightforward manipulation of the definition (
49
) of
\psi_{e,F,\zeta}
, this implies

\varphi\leq\psi_{e,F,\zeta}\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Next, if
q\in F
, then
q\in F_{(e,\nu)}
and, thus, the definition of
\psi_{e,F,\zeta}
immediately implies that
\psi_{e,F,\zeta}(q)\leq 1
. On the other hand, since
F\subseteq\{\varphi=1\}
by (
20
), there holds
1=\varphi(q)\geq\psi_{e,F,\zeta}(q)
. Hence
\varphi=\psi_{e,F,\zeta}=1
in
F
, and, by homogeneity,
\varphi=\psi_{e,F,\zeta}
in
\mathcal{C}(F)
.

Finally, in case
\nu<\zeta
, there holds
\varphi\leq\psi_{e,F,\zeta}\leq\psi_{e,F,\nu}
. Further, note that for an arbitrary
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one has

\overline{B}_{\nu}(q-\nu e)\cap\overline{B}_{\zeta}(q-\zeta e)=\{q\}.

From this, it follows that
\partial F_{(e,\nu)}\cap\partial F_{(e,\zeta)}=F
. Thus, given
x\in\{\psi_{e,F,\zeta}<+\infty\}\cap\{\psi_{e,F,\nu}<+\infty\}
, there holds
\psi_{e,F,\zeta}(x)=\psi_{e,F,\nu}(x)
if and only if
x\in F
. ∎

Proof of Proposition
13
.

We will show that there is an
r>0
and a smooth function
\tilde{\psi}:B_{r}(q)\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\tilde{\psi}(x)=\psi_{e,F,\nu}(x)
for each
x\in B_{r}(q)
.

Let
V_{F}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the smallest linear subspace such that
F\subseteq q+V_{F}
(cf. Section
4.1
). Let
\tilde{F}
be the corresponding tube, defined analogously to
F_{(e,\nu)}
, that is,

\tilde{F}=\bigcup_{y\in q+V_{F}}\overline{B}_{\nu}(y-\nu e).

Let
\tilde{\psi}
be the Minkowski gauge of
\tilde{F}
:

\tilde{\psi}(x)=\inf\left\{\alpha>0\,\mid\,\frac{x}{\alpha}\in\tilde{F}\right\}.

Since
q\in\text{rint}(F)
by assumption, it follows that there is an
r_{1}>0
such that
B_{r_{1}}(q)\cap F=B_{r_{1}}(q)\cap(q+V_{F})
and, thus,

B_{r_{1}}(q)\cap F_{(e,\nu)}=B_{r_{1}}(q)\cap\tilde{F}.

This implies
\psi_{e,F,\nu}=\tilde{\psi}
in the cone
\mathcal{C}(B_{r_{1}}(q))
, and, in particular, in
B_{r}(q)
for an appropriate choice of
r
.

It only remains to show that
\tilde{\psi}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
q
. Since
\tilde{\psi}
is a positively homogeneous convex function, the results of the next section, particularly Propositions
16
and
17
(and their proofs), are applicable. Hence to see that
\tilde{\psi}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
q
, it suffices to prove that
\partial\tilde{F}
is a smooth hypersurface in a neighborhood of
q
.

Let
V_{F}^{\perp}
denote the orthogonal complement of
V_{F}
. To see that
\tilde{F}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
q
, it suffices to establish that

(55)

\{x\in\tilde{F}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle\geq\langle q,e\rangle-\nu\}=(q-\nu e)+V_{F}+(\overline{B}_{\nu}(0)\cap H_{e}\cap V_{F}^{\perp}),

where, as before,
H_{e}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle\geq 0\}
.
Indeed, the right-hand side is a cylinder, hence its boundary is a smooth hypersurface; and
q
is an element of the boundary of the left-hand side. Therefore, it only remains to prove (
55
).

As far as (
55
) is concerned, suppose that
x\in\tilde{F}
and
\langle x,e\rangle\geq\langle q,e\rangle-\nu
. Fix a
y\in q+V_{F}
such that
x\in\overline{B}_{\nu}(y-\nu e)
. Write
x=y-\nu e+\xi_{F}+\xi_{F}^{\perp}
with
\xi_{F}\in V_{F}
and
\xi_{F}^{\perp}\in V_{F}^{\perp}
to find

\|\xi_{F}^{\perp}\|\leq\|x-(y-\nu e)\|\leq\nu,\quad\langle\xi_{F}^{\perp},e\rangle=\langle x,e\rangle-\langle y,e\rangle+\nu=\langle x,e\rangle-\langle q,e\rangle+\nu\geq 0

since
y-q\in V_{F}
and
V_{F}\subseteq\langle e\rangle^{\perp}
holds by (
52
). This proves

-(q-\nu e)+\{x\in\tilde{F}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle\geq\langle q,e\rangle-\nu\}\subseteq V_{F}+(\overline{B}_{\nu}(0)\cap H_{e}\cap V_{F}^{\perp}).

Conversely, fix
\xi_{F}\in V_{F}
and
\xi_{F}^{\perp}\in\overline{B}_{\nu}(0)\cap H_{e}\cap V_{F}^{\perp}
. Let
y=q+\xi_{F}
. Observe that if
x=y-\nu e+\xi_{F}^{\perp}
, then
x\in\overline{B}_{\nu}(y-\nu e)\subseteq\tilde{F}
and

\langle x,e\rangle=\langle y,e\rangle-\nu+\langle\xi_{F}^{\perp},e\rangle\geq\langle q,e\rangle-\nu.

This proves the remaining inclusion, and hence (
55
).
∎

Part II
Cone Comparison Principles for
C^{1,1}
Finsler Norms

This part of the paper concerns the main technical work, namely, utilizing the conical test functions to prove generalized cone comparison principles, such as the one encountered in Section
3.1
. The main technique is an approximation procedure via smoothing, which is tailor-made so that the essential differential structure remains intact. At a high level, it is analogous to Evans’ perturbed test function method
[
27
]
.

5.
Properties of Positively One-Homogeneous Convex Functions

The conical test functions introduced in the previous section, like Finsler norms themselves, belong to the class of positively one-homogeneous convex functions. As in the previous part of the paper, properties of such functions — particularly the extent to which their regularity is determined by that of their level sets — will play an important role in what follows.

This section presents a number of elementary (but fundamental) facts about positively homogeneous convex functions, which will be used extensively in what follows. These facts are more-or-less intuitive and not hard to prove, hence the reader may wish to skim this section at a first reading.

Remark 17
.

In what follows, it will be convenient to consider convex functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
. Toward that end, recall that a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is called convex if and only if the set
\{f<+\infty\}
is convex and
f
restricts to a real-valued convex function therein.

Furthermore, in order to include nontrivial functions taking value
+\infty
, it is important to give a precise definition of
postively one-homogeneous
. Specifically, henceforth, a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is positively one-homogeneous if, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\lambda>0
,

f(\lambda x)=\lambda f(x).

(It is necessary to exclude
\lambda=0
in this definition so that the set
\{f=+\infty\}
need not be trivial.)

5.1.
Subdifferential and Regularity

As was already mentioned above in the special case of Finsler norms, the subdifferential of a positively one-homogeneous convex function has a relatively simple geometric representation.

Recall that the subdifferential
\partial f
of a convex function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is the set-valued function on
\{f<+\infty\}
determined by the formula

(56)

\partial f(x)=\bigcap_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,f(y)\geq f(x)+\langle p,y-x\rangle\}.

The next result recalls the classical fact that a positively one-homogeneous convex function has a very simple subdifferential.

Proposition 15
.

If
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
is convex and positively one-homogeneous, then, for any
x_{*}\in\text{int}(\{\psi<+\infty\})\setminus\{0\}
,

(57)

\partial\psi(x_{*})=\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle p,x_{*}\rangle=\psi(x_{*})\}\cap\bigcap_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle p,x\rangle\leq\psi(x)\}.

Proof.

First, suppose that
x_{*}\in\text{int}(\{\psi<+\infty\})\setminus\{0\}
and
p\in\partial\psi(x_{*})
. Since
x_{*}
is in the interior of
\{\psi<+\infty\}
, there is an
\epsilon>0
such that
(1+t)x_{*}\in\{\psi<+\infty\}\setminus\{0\}
for any
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. Setting
x=(1+t)x_{*}
for
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, one invokes (
56
) to obtain

(1+t)\psi(x_{*})=\psi((1+t)x_{*})\geq\psi(x_{*})+t\langle p,x_{*}\rangle.

Sending
t\to 0^{+}
yields
\psi(x_{*})\geq\langle p,x_{*}\rangle
, while sending
t\to 0^{-}
gives
\psi(x_{*})\leq\langle p,x_{*}\rangle
. Thus,
\psi(x_{*})=\langle p,x_{*}\rangle
.

Revisiting (
56
) and substituting
\psi(x_{*})=\langle p,x_{*}\rangle
, one obtains

\psi(x)\geq\langle p,x\rangle\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

This proves one of the inclusions involved in (
57
).

Finally, it remains to prove the opposite inclusion. Suppose that
x_{*}\in\text{int}(\{\psi<+\infty\})\setminus\{0\}
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is such that

\langle p,x\rangle\leq\psi(x)\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\langle p,x_{*}\rangle=\psi(x_{*}).

To see that
p\in\partial\psi(x_{*})
, add and subtract
\langle p,x_{*}\rangle
to find, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\psi(x)\geq\langle p,x\rangle=\langle p,x_{*}\rangle+\langle p,x-x_{*}\rangle=\psi(x_{*})+\langle p,x-x_{*}\rangle.

∎

The next result makes precise the observation that, in the interior of the domain
\{\psi<+\infty\}
, a positively one-homogeneous convex function
\psi
is as regular as the level set
\{\psi=1\}
.

Proposition 16
.

Let
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
be a positively one-homogeneous convex function and suppose that
x_{*}\in\text{int}(\{\psi<+\infty\})\setminus\{0\}
. Then
\psi
is differentiable at
x_{*}
if and only if there is a unique supporting hyperplane to
\{\psi\leq 1\}
through the point
\psi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
.

In particular,
\{\psi=1\}
is a
C^{k}
hypersurface in the interior of
\{\psi<+\infty\}
if and only if
\psi
is a
C^{k}
function in the interior of
\{\psi<+\infty\}
.

Proof.

First, note that if
p\in\partial\psi(x_{*})
, then

\langle p,q\rangle\leq\psi(q)\leq 1=\langle p,\psi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}\rangle\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in\{\psi\leq 1\}.

Thus, each element of
\partial\psi(x_{*})
determines a supporting hyperplane to
\{\psi\leq 1\}
through
\psi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
.

Conversely, suppose that
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
determines such a hyperplane, that is,
\langle p,q-\psi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}\rangle\leq 0
for each
q\in\{\psi\leq 1\}
. Replacing
p
by
\lambda p
for an appropriate choice of
\lambda>0
, there is no loss of generality assuming that
\langle p,x_{*}\rangle=\psi(x_{*})
. Since
\langle p,q\rangle\leq 1
for any
q\in\{\psi\leq 1\}
, one readily deduces by positive-homogeneity that
\langle p,y\rangle\leq\psi(y)
for any
y\in\{\psi<+\infty\}
. Thus, by Proposition
15
,
p\in\partial\psi(x_{*})
.

The previous two paragraphs show that
p\in\partial\psi(x_{*})
holds if and only if
p
determines a supporting hyperplane to
\{\psi\leq 1\}
through the point
\psi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
. Finally, recall that
\psi
is differentiable at
x_{*}
if and only if there is a vector
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\partial\psi(x_{*})=\{p\}
.
22
22

22

See
[
49
, Theorem 9.18]
or
[
51
, Theorem 1.5.15]
.
Therefore,
\psi
is differentiable at
x_{*}
if and only if there is a unique supporting hyperplane to
\{\psi\leq 1\}
through
\psi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
.

Finally, if
\psi
is differentiable at a point
x
, then the positive one-homogeneity of
\psi
implies that
\psi
is differentiable at each point of
\mathcal{C}(\{x\})
and

D\psi(x)=D\psi(\check{x})=\|D\psi(\check{x})\|N(\check{x}),

where
\check{x}=\psi(x)^{-1}x
and
N(\check{x})
is the outward normal vector to
\{\psi\leq 1\}
at
\check{x}
.
At the same time, by Proposition
15
,

1=\psi(\check{x})=\langle D\psi(\check{x}),\check{x}\rangle,

which implies that

\|D\psi(\check{x})\|^{-1}=\langle N(\check{x}),\check{x}\rangle.

Therefore,

(58)

D\psi(x)=\langle N(\check{x}),\check{x}\rangle^{-1}N(\check{x}).

From this, one readily deduces that if
\{\psi\leq 1\}
is a
C^{k}
hypersurface locally in the interior of
\{\psi<+\infty\}
, then
D\psi
is
C^{k-1}
in that set and hence
\psi
is
C^{k}
there. The converse follows immediately from the implicit function theorem. ∎

5.2.
Curvature Bounds

In the next sections, approximation arguments will be introduced that will require somewhat delicate information concerning the Hessians of the test functions involved. Toward this end, it will be convenient to know that curvature bounds on the level sets of a positively homogeneous convex function can be propagated to Hessian bounds.

The next result shows that the Hessian of a positively homogeneous convex function can be expressed in terms of the normal vector and second fundamental form (derivative of the normal vector) of one of its level sets.

Proposition 17
.

Let
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
be a positively one-homogeneous convex function. Assume that
\psi
is locally
C^{1,1}
in the interior of
\{\psi<+\infty\}
, and let
N:\{\psi=1\}\to S^{d-1}
be the (outward) normal vector to
\{\psi\leq 1\}
. Define a map
x\mapsto\check{x}
from
\{\psi<+\infty\}
into
\{\psi=1\}
by

\check{x}=\frac{x}{\psi(x)}.

Then, for every
x\in\{\psi<+\infty\}
at which
D^{2}\psi(x)
exists, there holds

(59)

D^{2}\psi(x)=\psi(x)^{-1}A(x)\cdot B(x),

where
A
and
B
are the matrix fields given by

\displaystyle A(x)

\displaystyle=\langle N(\check{x}),\check{x}\rangle^{-1}DN(\check{x})-\langle N(\check{x}),\check{x}\rangle^{-2}(N(\check{x})\otimes\check{x})DN(\check{x})-\langle N(\check{x}),\check{x}\rangle^{-2}N(\check{x})\otimes N(\check{x}),

\displaystyle B(x)

\displaystyle=\text{Id}-\check{x}\otimes D\psi(x).

Proof.

The formula (
59
) follows directly from (
58
) and the chain rule. ∎

The previous expression for
D^{2}\psi
provides very convenient Hessian bounds in terms of the curvature of the level set
\{\psi=1\}
.

Proposition 18
.

Let
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
be a positively one-homogeneous convex function. Assume that
\psi
is locally
C^{1,1}
in the open set
\text{int}(\{\psi<+\infty\})
, and define the map
x\mapsto\check{x}
as in Proposition
17
. Then

\displaystyle\|D^{2}\psi(x)\|\leq\psi(x)^{-1}\left[2\left(1+\|D\psi(x)\|^{2}\right)\left(1+\|\check{x}\|\right)(1+\|DN(\check{x})\|)\right]\left(1+\|D\psi(x)\|\|\check{x}\|\right)

at any point in
x\in\text{int}(\{\psi<+\infty\})
at which
D^{2}\psi(x)
exists.

Proof.

In view of (
58
) and (
59
), it suffices to observe that the matrix functions
A
and
B
of the previous proposition are controlled by the trivial bounds

\displaystyle\|A(x)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|D\psi(x)\|\|DN(x)\|+\|D\psi(x)\|^{2}\|\check{x}\|\|DN(x)\|+\|D\psi(x)\|^{2},

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\|D\psi(x)\|^{2})(1+\|\check{x}\|)(1+\|DN(x)\|),

\displaystyle\|B(x)\|

\displaystyle\leq 1+\|D\psi(x)\|\|\check{x}\|.

Thus, since
\|D^{2}\psi(x)\|\leq\psi(x)^{-1}\|A(x)\|\|B(x)\|
, the desired inequality follows. ∎

The following trivial consequence of Proposition
18
will be used repeatedly in the arguments that follow:

Proposition 19
.

There is a continuous function
\Gamma:(0,1)\to(0,1)
such that, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, the constant
\Gamma(\delta)>0
has the following property: suppose that
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
is a positively homogeneous convex function and
\psi
is locally
C^{1,1}
in the interior of
\{\psi<+\infty\}
, fix
\zeta\in(0,1)
, and let
A\subseteq\{\psi=1\}\cap\overline{B}_{\delta^{-1}}(0)
. If for any
q\in A
, there holds

\|DN(q)\|\leq\zeta^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\|D\psi(q)\|\leq\delta^{-1},

then
\psi
satisfies the Hessian estimate

\psi(x)D^{2}\psi(x)\leq\Gamma(\delta)\zeta^{-1}\text{Id}\quad\text{for almost every}\quad x\in\mathcal{C}(A).

Proof.

Suppose that
x\in\mathcal{C}(A)\setminus\{0\}
, hence
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
\psi(x)^{-1}x\in\overline{B}_{\delta^{-1}}(0)
. If
D^{2}\psi(x)
exists, then Proposition
18
applies to give, by hypothesis,

\psi(x)D^{2}\psi(x)\leq 2(1+\delta^{-1})(1+\delta^{-1})(1+\zeta^{-1})(1+\delta^{-2}).

Since
\max\{\zeta,\delta\}<1
, this yields the desired estimate with
\Gamma(\delta)=4(1+\delta^{-2})^{3}
. ∎

5.3.
Gradient Bounds

This section concludes with an easy gradient bound that will be particularly convenient when used in conjunction with the curvature bounds of the previous subsection.

Proposition 20
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If there is a
\delta>0
such that

\overline{B}_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\},

then
\{\varphi^{*}\leq 1\}\subseteq\overline{B}_{\delta^{-1}}(0)
.

In particular, if
\varphi
is
C^{1}
away from the origin and
\overline{B}_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}
, then
\|D\varphi(x)\|\leq\delta^{-1}
for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\overline{B}_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}
, and fix
p\in\{\varphi^{*}=1\}
. If
q\in\partial B_{\delta}(0)
, then
q\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}
and, thus, by duality,

\langle q,p\rangle\leq\varphi(q)\varphi^{*}(p)\leq 1.

It follows that
\|p\|\leq\delta^{-1}
. This proves
\{\varphi^{*}=1\}\subseteq\overline{B}_{\delta^{-1}}(0)
, hence also
\{\varphi^{*}\leq 1\}\subseteq\overline{B}_{\delta^{-1}}(0)
by scaling.

Finally, if
\varphi
is
C^{1}
away from the origin and
\overline{B}_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}
, then, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, the variational formula (
19
) implies that

\{D\varphi(x)\}=\partial\varphi(x)\subseteq\{\varphi^{*}=1\}.

Thus,
\|D\varphi(x)\|\leq\delta^{-1}
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. ∎

5.4.
Bounding the Hessians of the Approximations

To motivate the results above, here is a simple application, namely, the
O(\zeta^{-1})
Hessian bound stated earlier. The statement is recalled below for the convenience of the reader:

See
4

Proof.

The point is to combine combine conclusion (i) from the approximation theorem, Theorem
5
, with the key Hessian bound, Proposition
19
.

In particular, recall from Proposition
9
that
\|D\varphi_{\zeta}\|
is uniformly bounded independently of
\zeta\in(0,1)
. Further, as is well known, condition (i) implies that the derivative of the normal vector to
\{\varphi_{\zeta}\leq 1\}
is bounded above by
\zeta^{-1}\text{Id}
; see
[
40
]
. As a result of these two bounds, Proposition
19
implies the desired estimate. ∎

6.
C^{1,1}
Cone Comparison Principles via Perturbation

In this section, cone comparison principles are proved in the special case when the unit ball
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is
C^{1,1}
, or, equivalently,
\varphi\in C^{1,1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
. This will be achieved by utilizing the conical test functions of Section
4
.

As pointed out already above, difficulties arise due to the fact that the conical test functions are not globally smooth. Thus, new ideas are necessary to show that they still yield useful information at contact points. This is the main obstacle that will be overcome in this section.

In order to fully justify the computations in Section
3.1
, it will be necessary to quantify the proofs. Accordingly, the following running assumption will be used throughout this section:

Assumption 1
.

\varphi
is a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that, for some
\zeta,\delta>0
,

(60)

\displaystyle\{\varphi\leq 1\}\quad\text{satisfies an interior ball condition of radius}\,\,\zeta,

(61)

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}\subseteq\overline{B}_{\delta^{-1}}(0).

6.1.
Abstract Cone Comparison Principles

The generalized cone comparison principle that appeared in Section
3.1
follows from a more general result, stated next. Toward that end, given
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, recall the shifted infinity Laplacian
\mathcal{G}^{*,\alpha}_{\varphi}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{*,\alpha}_{\varphi}(p,X)

\displaystyle=\max\left\{Q_{X}(q-\alpha\|p\|^{-1}p)\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\right\}\quad\text{if}\quad(p,X)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathcal{S}_{d},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{*,\alpha}_{\varphi}(0,X)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{0\neq p\to 0}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\varphi}(p,X).

The arguments of Section
3.1
above motivated the interest in these operators.

The main result of this section concerns differential inequalities of the form

(62)

-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}^{*,\alpha}_{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0,

where
\mathcal{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a given function and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
is fixed.

The assumptions on
\mathcal{H}
will be as follows:

(63)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\,\,\text{is upper semicontinuous in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\mathbb{R},

(64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x,r,p,X,a)\leq\mathcal{H}(x,s,p,Y,b)\quad\text{if}\quad r\geq s,\,\,X\leq Y,\,\,\text{and}\,\,a\leq b.

Theorem 6
.

Assume that
\varphi
satisfies Assumption
1
. Let
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be a bounded open set, fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\mathcal{H}
be a function satisfying (
63
) and (
64
).

If
u\in USC(\overline{V})
satisfies

-\mathcal{H}(x,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,V,

and

\max\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\}>\max\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\partial V\},

then there is a triple
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})\in V\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi=1\}
such that

\displaystyle u(x_{*})-\varphi(x_{*})=\max\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\},

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},u(x_{*}),D\varphi(x_{*}),X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{**}-\alpha\|D\varphi(x_{*})\|^{-1}D\varphi(x_{*})))\leq 0,

\displaystyle X_{*}q_{**}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\varphi(x_{*})X_{*}\leq\Gamma(\delta)\zeta^{-1}\text{Id}.

Above
\Gamma(\delta)
is the constant from Proposition
19
.

6.2.
The Touching Argument

Here is the outline of the proof of Theorem
6
. As in the statement of the theorem, suppose that
u\in USC(\overline{V})
and
x_{0}\in V
are such that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,V,

\displaystyle u(x_{0})-\varphi(x_{0})=\max\left\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\right\}>\max\left\{u(y)-\varphi(y)\,\mid\,y\in\partial V\right\}.

Define
M=\max_{\overline{V}}(u-\varphi)=u(x_{0})-\varphi(x_{0})
. Since
\varphi\in C^{1,1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
and
x_{0}\in V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, one can define vectors
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
e\in S^{d-1}
by setting

p=D\varphi(x_{0}),\quad e=\|p\|^{-1}p.

Since the
\zeta
-interior ball condition (
60
) is satisfied, the family of conical test functions
\{\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}\,\mid\,0<\nu\leq\zeta\}
all touch
u
above at
x_{0}
. More precisely, for any
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
, one readily verifies using Proposition
12
that

(65)

\displaystyle u(x_{0})=M+\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}(x_{0}),\quad u\leq M+\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}\quad\text{in}\,\,V,

(66)

\displaystyle\{x\in\overline{V}\,\mid\,u(x)=M+\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}(x)\}\subseteq V.

Since it will be used later, it is worth noting at this stage that (
65
) and (
66
) hold for any
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
if and only if they hold for
\nu=\zeta
. This is a consequence of the ordering
\psi_{\zeta}\leq\psi_{\nu}
and the inclusion
x_{0}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))=\{\psi_{\zeta}=\psi_{\nu}<+\infty\}
.

For convenience, since
p
and
e
will be fixed henceforth, the following shorthand notation will be used throughout this section:

\psi_{\nu}:=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}.

As already mentioned in Section
4
, while
\psi_{\nu}
touches
u
from above at
x_{0}
, unfortunately
\psi_{\nu}
is not a smooth test function in general. Nonetheless, it still has the “right shape,” which suggests a perturbation argument. Toward that end, in this section, a family of smooth perturbations
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
will be introduced, which converge to
\psi_{\nu}
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
while retaining the main geometric properties of
\psi_{\nu}
.

In particular, assume that there is an open set
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, a parameter
\epsilon_{*}\in(0,\nu)
, and nonnegative functions
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
such that

\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\to\psi_{\nu}\quad\text{locally uniformly in}\,\,\mathcal{U}\quad\text{as}\quad\epsilon\to 0^{+}

and, for any
\epsilon<\epsilon_{*}
,

\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{is smooth in}\,\,\mathcal{U}\quad\text{and}\quad\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}<+\infty\}=\mathcal{U}.

Given any
\epsilon\in(0,\nu)
, fix a point
x_{\epsilon}\in\overline{V}
such that

u(x_{\epsilon})-\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})=\max\left\{u(x)-\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\right\}.

Due to (
66
) and the compactness of
\overline{V}
,
x_{\epsilon}\in V\cap\mathcal{U}
provided
\epsilon
is small enough.
Thus, since
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
is smooth in
\mathcal{U}
, the subsolution property of
u
implies that

-\mathcal{H}(x_{\epsilon},D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}),D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}),\mathcal{G}^{*,\alpha}_{\varphi}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{0}),D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{0})))\leq 0.

By definition of
\mathcal{G}^{*,\alpha}_{\varphi}
, there is a point
q_{\epsilon}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}))
such that

(67)

\mathcal{G}^{*,\alpha}_{\varphi}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}),D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}))=Q_{D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})}(q_{\epsilon}-\alpha\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})).

The key now is to show that the perturbation
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{0<\epsilon<\nu}
can be constructed in such a way that, along subsequences,

(x_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon},D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}))\to(x_{*},q_{**},X_{*}),

where the triple
(x_{*},q_{**},X_{*})
has the property

X_{*}q_{**}=0.

Below the perturbation
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
is constructed precisely so that this is the case.

In order to avoid repetition later and to prepare for future results, it is worth observing that the previous discussion amounts to a proof of the following proposition.

Proposition 21
.

Assume that
\varphi
satisfies Assumption (
1
). Let
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be a bounded open set, fix an
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\mathcal{H}
be a function satisfying (
63
) and (
64
).

Fix a
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
and a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
and write
\psi_{\mu}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\mu}
for
\mu\in\{\nu,\zeta\}
. Assume that there is an open set
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a constant
\epsilon_{*}>0
, and a family of nonnegative functions
(\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
such that
\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))\subseteq\mathcal{U}
and

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\to\psi_{\nu}\quad\text{locally uniformly in}\,\,\mathcal{U}\quad\text{as}\quad\epsilon\to 0^{+},

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{is smooth in}\,\,\mathcal{U}\quad\text{and}\quad\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}<+\infty\}=\mathcal{U}\quad\text{for each}\quad\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}).

If
u\in USC(\overline{V})
,
M\in\mathbb{R}
, and
x_{0}\in V\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
are such that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,V,

\displaystyle u\leq M+\psi_{\zeta}\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{V},\quad u(x_{0})=M+\psi_{\zeta}(x_{0}),

\displaystyle\{x\in\overline{V}\,\mid\,u(x)=M+\psi_{\zeta}(x)\}\subseteq V,

then there are sequences
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
and
(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V\cap\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
such that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to+\infty
and, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(68)

\displaystyle u(x_{j})-\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})=\max\left\{u(x)-\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\right\},

(69)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{j},u(x_{j}),D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}),D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}),\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}),D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})))\leq 0.

6.3.
Smoothing the Test Function

Assume, as in the previous discussion, that
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, and let
p=D\varphi(x_{0})
and
e=\|D\varphi(x_{0})\|^{-1}D\varphi(x_{0})
.

The question now — and here is the main technical hurdle of this section — is: how to perturb
\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}
at the contact point so as to obtain “better” contact points, where the viscosity subsolution property of
u
can be invoked? This will be analogous to the classical approach in the proof of Jensen’s Lemma,
23
23

23

See
[
24
, Lemma A.3]
or
[
22
, Lemma 11.2]
.
except that the Alexandrov-Bakelman-Pucci argument will be replaced by completely different techniques involving smoothing the test function.

As suggested in the previous subsection, it will be convenient to consider the entire family
\{\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}\,\mid\,0<\nu\leq\zeta\}
. Again, the following shorthand will be used:

\psi_{\nu}:=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}

To overcome the fact that
\psi_{\nu}
may not be smooth at the contact point
x_{0}
, a family of functions
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
will be constructed in such a way as to exploit the graphical representation of the surface
\{\psi_{\nu}=1\}
introduced in Section
4.6
.

First, following the discussion in that subsection, define the convex set
G_{p}\subseteq\{e\}^{\perp}
; the distance function
\text{dist}(\cdot,G_{p}):\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
; and the function
g_{\nu}
according to the following formulas:

\displaystyle G_{p}

\displaystyle=\{x^{\prime}\in\{e\}^{\perp}\,\mid\,x^{\prime}+\varphi^{*}(e)e\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\}=\partial\varphi^{*}(p)-\varphi^{*}(e)e,

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|\,\mid\,y^{\prime}\in G_{p}\right\},

(70)

\displaystyle g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\varphi^{*}(e)-\nu+\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})^{2}},

where, to reiterate, here
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
is identified with
\{e\}^{\perp}
. Note that
g_{\nu}
is concave since
\text{dist}(\cdot,G_{p})
is convex.

Recall from Section
4.6
that if
F
denotes the face
F=\partial\varphi^{*}(p)=\partial\varphi^{*}(e)
and
F_{(e,\nu)}
is defined as in (
48
), then

(71)

\displaystyle F_{(e,\nu)}\cap\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle\geq\varphi^{*}(e)-\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=\{x^{\prime}+se\,\mid\,\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\nu,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\varphi^{*}(e)-\nu\leq s\leq g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})\,\,\},

(72)

\displaystyle\partial F_{(e,\nu)}\cap\left\{x\in\ \,\mid\,\langle x,e\rangle\geq\varphi^{*}(e)-\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=\{x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e\,\mid\,\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\nu\},

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\{x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e\,\mid\,x^{\prime}\in G_{p}\}.

Recalling that
\psi_{\nu}
is nothing but the Minkowski gauge (
49
) of
F_{(e,\nu)}
, this shows that, at least close to
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
, the hypersurface
\{\psi_{\nu}=1\}
equals the graph of
g_{\nu}
.

More precisely, and in preparation for what is to come, the connection between
\psi_{\nu}
and
g_{\nu}
can be described in the following way: for any small enough
r>0
, there is an open set
\mathcal{U}(r)
such that
\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))\subseteq\mathcal{U}(r)\subseteq\{\psi_{\nu}<+\infty\}
and, for any
x\in\mathcal{U}(r)
,

(73)

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu}(x)=1\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\text{dist}\left(x^{\prime},G_{p}\right)<r\quad\text{and}\quad\langle x,e\rangle=g_{\nu}\left(x^{\prime}\right).

Note that the “only if” follows more-or-less directly from the definition of
\psi_{\nu}
; the interest lies in the “if” direction, which, indeed, requires
r
to be small. Since this statement follows from Theorem
7
stated below, justification is deferred until later.

The connection between
\psi_{\nu}
and
g_{\nu}
encapsulated by (
73
) will be exploited in the construction of the perturbations
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
. In particular,
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
will be a convex positively one-homogeneous function constructed specifically so that a version of (
73
) holds, except with
g_{\nu}
replaced by a smoothed function
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
.

Toward that end, let
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
be a smooth function such that

(74)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\rho(x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}=1,\quad\rho(x^{\prime})=0\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\|x^{\prime}\|\geq 1,

(75)

\displaystyle\rho(x^{\prime})=\rho(y^{\prime})\quad\text{if}\quad\|x^{\prime}\|=\|y^{\prime}\|.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,\nu)
, let
g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
be function obtained by mollifying
g_{\nu}
against
\rho
at scale
\epsilon
:

(76)

g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x^{\prime})=\int_{B_{1}}g_{\nu}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}.

Since
g_{\nu}
is concave, Jensen’s inequality implies that
g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
is concave and it lies below
g_{\nu}
. These facts are recorded below for later use:

(77)

g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\,\,\text{is concave}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\leq g_{\nu}\quad\text{in}\,\,\{\text{dist}(\cdot,G_{p})\leq\nu-\epsilon\}.

Now that
g_{\nu}
has been regularized, the approximation
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
of
\psi_{\nu}
will be constructed so that
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}=1\}
equals the graph of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
close to
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
. To make this precise, and to divorce the details of the construction from the properties needed in this section, here is a definition of the type of approximation that will be needed:

Definition 8
.

Given a conical test function
\psi_{\nu}
as above, a family of convex, positively one-homogeneous functions
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
is said to be an
admissible perturbation of
\psi_{\nu}
if there is an open set
\mathcal{U}_{\nu}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a parameter
\epsilon_{*}>0
, and a mollifier
\rho
satisfying (
74
) and (
75
) such that (i)

\displaystyle(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon},D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})\to(\psi_{\nu},D\psi_{\nu})\quad\text{locally uniformly in}\,\,\mathcal{U}_{\nu}\quad\text{as}\quad\epsilon\to 0^{+};

(ii) for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})
,

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{is smooth in}\,\,\mathcal{U}_{\nu},\quad\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}<+\infty\}=\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\nu}},

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\psi_{\nu}\leq\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{pointwise in}\quad\mathcal{U}_{\nu};

and (iii) there is an
r>0
such that, for any
x\in\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
and any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})
,

(78)

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)=1\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})<r\quad\text{and}\quad\langle x,e\rangle=g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}).

Notice that (
78
) mirrors the claimed property (
73
) of
\psi_{\nu}
.

The above definition spells out the properties of the perturbation that will be used in the arguments that follow. That still leaves the question of existence, but this is somewhat intuitive. Indeed, a natural place to start is by considering the Minkowski gauge of the hypograph of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, which is certainly a convex set since
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
is concave. The main issue — and for this reason the existence problem warrants a careful argument — is the existence of an open set
\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
for which the level surface
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}=1\}
can be characterized as in (
78
).

However, since the proof of the existence of a perturbation satisfying (
78
) is not immediately relevant here, the proof is deferred to Appendix
B
. For now, the next theorem asserts that existence does hold and the graph characterization (
78
) directly extends to
\psi_{\nu}
:

Theorem 7
.

Assume that
\varphi
satisfies Assumption
1
. Let
\psi_{\nu}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}
be the conical test function associated to the parameters
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
, and
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
. Then, for any mollifier
\rho
satisfying (
74
) and (
75
), there is an admissible perturbation
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
of
\psi_{\nu}
.

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
and
r
are as in the definition, then, for any
x\in\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
,

\psi_{\nu}(x)=1\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})<r\quad\text{and}\quad\langle x^{\prime},e\rangle=g_{\nu}(x^{\prime}).

The next result shows that touching
u
above with
\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
in place of
\psi_{\nu}
leads to a useful outcome in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Proposition 22
.

Assume that
\varphi
satisfies Assumption (
1
). Let
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be a bounded open set.

Fix a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and let
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
. For any
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
, let
\psi_{\nu}:=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}
be the associated conical test function.

Suppose there is a
u\in USC(\overline{V})
, an
M\in\mathbb{R}
, and an
x_{0}\in V\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
such that

(79)

\displaystyle u\leq M+\psi_{\zeta}\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{V},\quad u(x_{0})=M+\psi_{\zeta}(x_{0}),

(80)

\displaystyle\{x\in\overline{V}\,\mid\,u(x)=M+\psi_{\zeta}(x)\}\subseteq V.

Fix a
\nu<\zeta
; let
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
and
(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi=1\}
be sequences such that (
68
) holds for each
j
; and let
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
be an admissible perturbation of
\psi_{\nu}
.

If
((X_{j},\tilde{q}_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi=1\}
is a sequence such that, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(81)

X_{j}=D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}),\quad\tilde{q}_{j}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})),

then the following statements hold:

(i)

((x_{j},X_{j},\tilde{q}_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact in
V\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi=1\}
. In particular,

(82)

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\varphi(x_{j})\|X_{j}\|\leq\Gamma(\delta)\nu^{-1}.

(ii)

If
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})
is any accumulation point of
((x_{j},X_{j},\tilde{q}_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, then there is a face
F_{*}\subseteq\partial\varphi^{*}(D\varphi(x_{*}))
such that

(83)

(a)\quad\left\{\varphi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*},q_{**}\right\}\subseteq F_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad(b)\quad X_{*}q=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}.

Since it is somewhat technical, the proof of Proposition
22
is deferred to the next section. Instead, for the moment, here is how the proposition leads to a proof of Theorem
6
.

Proof of Theorem
6
.

To begin the proof, fix an
x_{0}\in V
such that

u(x_{0})-\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{0})=\max\left\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\right\}>\max\left\{u(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid\,x\in\partial V\right\}.

In view of the discussion of Section
6.2
, there is a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
such that if
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
and
\psi_{\zeta}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p),\zeta}
, then (
79
) and (
80
) hold. Given any
\nu\in(0,\zeta)
, Proposition
21
implies there are sequences
