(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
and
(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V
such that (
68
) and (
69
) hold for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Define
(X_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{d}
by setting
X_{j}=D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})
. By (
69
), for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a
\tilde{q}_{j}\in\{\varphi=1\}
such that

(84)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{j},u(x_{j}),D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}),X_{j},Q_{X_{j}}(\tilde{q}_{j}-\alpha\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})))\leq 0,

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\tilde{q}_{j}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})).

By Proposition
22
, there is no loss of generality assuming there is a limit
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})=\lim_{j\to\infty}(x_{j},X_{j},\tilde{q}_{j})
and a face
F_{*}
of
\partial\varphi^{*}(D\varphi(x_{*}))
such that (
83
) holds.

In particular, after defining
q_{*}=\varphi(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
, (
83
) says that

(85)

\{q_{*},q_{**}\}\subseteq F_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{*}q=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}.

Note that
u(x_{*})=\lim_{j\to\infty}u(x_{j})
due to (
68
) and the local uniform convergence of
\psi^{\epsilon_{j}}_{\nu}\to\psi_{\nu}
. Thus, since
\mathcal{H}
is upper semicontinuous and
\psi_{\nu}
is
C^{1}
, sending
j\to+\infty
in (
84
) yields
24
24

24

Here is where one needs that
D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\to D\psi_{\nu}
locally uniformly, as prescribed by Definition
8
.

-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},u(x_{*}),D\psi_{\nu}(x_{*}),X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{**}-\alpha\|D\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})))\leq 0.

Notice that since
x_{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
, Proposition
12
implies that

D\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})=D\varphi(x_{*}).

Thus, the previous inequality can be rewritten in the form

(86)

-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},u(x_{*}),D\varphi(x_{*}),X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{**}-\alpha\|D\varphi(x_{*})\|^{-1}D\varphi(x_{*})))\leq 0.

Since
x_{*}\in V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, the positivity of
\varphi
away from zero implies
\varphi(x_{*})>0
, and, thus, by (
82
),

(87)

0\leq X_{*}\leq\varphi(x_{*})^{-1}\Gamma(\delta)\nu^{-1}.

The combination of (
85
), (
86
), and (
87
) yields the desired conclusion in the limit
\nu\to\zeta^{+}
(i.e., after possibly extracting a limit point of the triples
(x_{*},q_{**},X_{*})
). ∎

7.
The Perturbation Argument

This section presents the proof of Proposition
22
, the main technical ingredient in the perturbation argument presented above. The bulk of the work consists in showing that the derivatives of the perturbed test functions really are well-behaved in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. This will be a consequence of the main technical step in this section, namely, Proposition
23
, stated next.

While the proof of Proposition
23
is geometrically intuitive (in low dimensions), making it rigorous is greatly facilitated through the use of the so-called normal cone of convex analysis. This will become apparent multiple times in the proof. Toward that end, Section
7.3
reviews the definition and relevant properties. The proof of Proposition
23
appears in Section
7.4
, while the proof of Proposition
22
comes earlier in Section
7.2
.

7.1.
Taming the Perturbation

The next result distills the main intermediate step involved in the proof of Proposition
22
. In particular, the time has come to establish that the derivatives of the perturbed test functions
\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
defined in Section
6.3
are well-behaved in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

More precisely, recall what remains to be proved. As in Section
6
, for the remainder of the section, assume that
\varphi
satisfies Assumption
1
. Fix a
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
and a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, and let
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
. The objective is to show that at a contact point
x_{0}\in\mathcal{C}(p)
where
\psi_{\nu}
touches the subsolution
u
from above, if
x_{\epsilon}
is a contact point obtained after replacing
\psi_{\nu}
by the perturbation
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, and if
q_{\epsilon}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}))
, then, up to extraction,

\displaystyle(x_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon},D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon}))\to(x_{*},q_{**},X_{*})\quad\text{as}\quad\epsilon\to 0^{+},

where

\displaystyle X_{*}q_{**}=0,\quad 0\leq\varphi(x_{*})X_{*}\leq\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi)\nu^{-1}.

The next result will be used to prove this.

In the statement, as usual, the representation
x=x^{\prime}+\langle x,e\rangle e
is used so that
x^{\prime}\in\{e\}^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto
\{e\}^{\perp}
for an arbitrary
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proposition 23
.

As in the discussion above, let
(\psi_{\nu},g_{\nu})
be the conical test function together with the function parametrizing its level sets, and let
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon},g_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{0<\epsilon<\nu}
be the perturbed versions.

(a) Fix an arbitrary sequence
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
such that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. If there is a sequence
(q_{j},\tilde{q}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{\varphi\leq 1\}
and a pair
(q_{*},q_{**})\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\times\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
such that

(i)

\lim_{j\to\infty}(q_{j},\tilde{q}_{j})=(q_{*},q_{**})
;

(ii)

for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle q_{j}\in\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}=1\}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{q}_{j}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}));\quad\text{and}

(iii)

the following smallness condition is satisfied:

(88)

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\text{dist}

\displaystyle(q_{j}^{\prime},G_{p})<+\infty,

then there is a subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, a vector
w^{\prime}_{*}\in\{e\}^{\perp}
, and a matrix
A^{\prime}_{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that

(89)

\displaystyle-w_{*}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(q_{j_{k}}^{\prime}),

(90)

\displaystyle A_{*}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}D^{2}g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(q_{j_{k}}^{\prime}).

Furthermore,

(91)

\langle q_{*},w_{*}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle q_{**},w_{*}^{\prime}\rangle=\max\left\{\langle q,w_{*}^{\prime}\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\right\}.

In particular, if
F_{*}
is the face of
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
given by

(92)

F_{*}=\{q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\,\mid\,\langle q,w_{*}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle q_{*},w_{*}^{\prime}\rangle\},

then
\{q_{*},q_{**}\}\subseteq F_{*}
.

(b) If, in addition,
(Y_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{d}
is defined by

Y_{j}=D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}),

then

(93)

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\|Y_{j}\|\leq\Gamma(\delta)\nu^{-1}.

Furthermore, if
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subsequence as in part (a) with corresponding vector
w_{*}^{\prime}
and face
F_{*}
, and if there is a
Y_{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that
Y_{j_{k}}\to Y_{*}
as
k\to+\infty
, then

(94)

Y_{*}q=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}.

The proof of this proposition, which is deferred until Section
7.4
, will begin by establishing part (a), which will then be leveraged to prove part (b). The proof of (a), which identifies the face
F_{*}
, amounts to an analysis of the curvature of the level set of
\psi_{\nu}
near the face
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
. Here, due to the lack of smoothness, curvature is understood in terms of directional derivatives of the normal vector, evaluated along subsequences as in Lemma
10
below. Once the conceptual issues are clear, the explicit definition (
70
) of
g_{\nu}
reduces the task to checking certain conditions via calculus.

The proof of (b), which determines the limiting Hessian
Y_{*}
, is more involved: it becomes necessary to understand the curvature of the level set
\{\psi_{\nu}=1\}
interpreted in terms of the structure of the Hessian
D^{2}\psi_{\nu}
, hence Lemma
10
no longer suffices. In order to make rigorous the intuitive sense that the Hessians of the perturbed test functions should curve “in the right directions,” it turns out that arguing in terms of faces and normal cones clarifies the picture considerably. Once these notions are brought in, the work once again reduces to straightforward calculations.

7.2.
Application to Cone Comparison

The stage is almost set for the proof of Proposition
22
, which is an application of Proposition
23
. Still, one key condition needs to be checked, namely, the smallness condition imposed as a hypothesis in Proposition
23
.

The next result verifies this condition. This is where the strict inequality
\nu<\zeta
is important.

Proposition 24
.

Let
\varphi
satisfy Assumption
1
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be open and bounded.

Fix a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, set
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
, and let
(\psi_{\nu})_{0<\nu\leq\zeta}=\{\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\zeta}\,\mid\,0<\nu\leq\zeta\}
be the conical test functions of Section
4
.

Fix a
\nu\in(0,\zeta)
and let
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{0<\epsilon<\nu}
be an admissible perturbation of
\psi_{\nu}
.

Suppose that there is a
u\in USC(\overline{V})
, an
M\in\mathbb{R}
, and an
x_{0}\in V\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
such that

(95)

\displaystyle u\leq M+\psi_{\zeta}\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{V},\quad u(x_{0})=M+\psi_{\zeta}(x_{0}),

(96)

\displaystyle\{x\in\overline{V}\,\mid\,u(x)=M+\psi_{\zeta}(x)\}\subseteq V.

If
(x_{\epsilon})_{0<\epsilon<\nu}\subseteq\overline{V}
and
(q_{\epsilon})_{0<\epsilon<\nu}
satisfy, for each
\epsilon\in(0,\nu)
,

u(x_{\epsilon})-\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})=\max\left\{u(y)-\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(y)\,\mid\,y\in\overline{V}\right\},\quad q_{\epsilon}=\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})^{-1}x_{\epsilon},

then

(97)

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon}^{\prime},G_{p})<+\infty.

(Here, as throughout this section,
q\mapsto q^{\prime}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto
\{e\}^{\perp}
.)

The somewhat technical proof of Proposition
24
is presented in Appendix
B
alongside the construction of admissible perturbations. Still, at this stage, it may be worthwhile to consider why the proposition is true. Here is an analogous, but much simpler situation: suppose that
u:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is upper semicontinuous and

u(x)\leq\frac{x^{2}}{2\zeta}\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in[-1,1],\quad u(0)=0,

i.e., the quadratic
x\mapsto\frac{x^{2}}{2\zeta}
touches
u
from above at
0
.
Fix a
\nu<\zeta
and, for any
\epsilon>0
, let
v_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
be the function obtained by mollifying the quadratic
x\mapsto\frac{x^{2}}{2\nu}
at scale
\epsilon
, i.e.,
v_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2}\rho(x+\epsilon y)\,dy
for some mollifying kernel
\rho
. Fix points
(x_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
with

u(x_{\epsilon})-v_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})=\max\left\{u(y)-v_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(y)\,\mid\,y\in[-1,1]\right\}\quad\text{for each}\quad\epsilon>0.

In view of the fact that
\frac{x^{2}}{2\nu}-\frac{x^{2}}{2\zeta}=C(\nu,\zeta)x^{2}
for some
C(\nu,\zeta)>0
, it is not hard to show that

|x_{\epsilon}|\leq C\epsilon,

where the constant depends only on
\nu
,
\zeta
, and the mollifier
\rho
. That is, after the mollification, the new contact points cannot stray too far from the original one.

The above analogy is slightly too simple, yet Proposition
24
is essentially the same situation, except with the quadratic
\frac{x^{2}}{2\nu}
replaced by
\psi_{\nu}
and
|\cdot|
(the distance to the origin) replaced by
\text{dist}(\cdot,G_{p})
(the distance to the set
G_{p}=\{g_{\zeta}=g_{\nu}\}
).

Taking Propositions
23
and
24
for granted for now, here is the proof of Proposition
22
, which boils down to concatenating the previous string of propositions.

Proof of Proposition
22
.

Begin with the proof of (i), that is, the claim that the sequence
((x_{j},X_{j},\tilde{q}_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact in
V\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi=1\}
and (
82
) holds. Toward that end, since
\overline{V}
and
\{\varphi=1\}
is compact, it suffices to show that (i) if
x_{*}
is any limit point of
(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, then
x_{*}\in V
; and (ii) the matrices
(X_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
are bounded.

Let
x_{*}
be an accumulation point of
(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. Without loss of generality (passing to a subsequence if necessary), assume that
x_{*}=\lim_{j\to\infty}x_{j}
. Observe that (
68
) implies

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}[u(x_{\epsilon})-\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})]\geq\max\left\{u(y)-\psi_{\nu}(y)\,\mid\,y\in\overline{V}\right\}.

Thus, by upper semicontinuity of
u
and the local uniform convergence
\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}\to\psi_{\nu}
,

(98)

u(x_{*})-\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})=\max\left\{u(y)-\psi_{\nu}(y)\,\mid\,y\in\overline{V}\right\},

and then (
80
) implies that
x_{*}\in V
.

In fact, by Proposition
12
and the strict inequality
\nu<\zeta
, (
98
) and (
80
) together imply that
x_{*}\in V\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
, and then
x_{*}\neq 0
since
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. It follows that one can define
(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
by setting
q_{j}=\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})^{-1}x_{j}
for all
j
sufficiently large. Defining
q_{*}=\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
, the local uniform convergence
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}\to\psi_{\nu}
implies
q_{*}=\lim_{j\to\infty}q_{j}
. Further, by Proposition
24
, if
q_{j}^{\prime}
denotes the component of
q_{j}
orthogonal to
e
, then

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{j}^{\prime},G_{p})<+\infty.

Thus, the sequence
((q_{j},\tilde{q}_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the assumptions (i)-(iii) of Proposition
23
. Invoking that proposition, one deduces that if
(Y_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is given by

Y_{j}=D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}),

then
(Y_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies (
93
). At the same time, observe that, by the definition (
81
) of
X_{j}
and homogeneity, one can write

X_{j}=\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})^{-1}Y_{j}\quad\text{for each}\quad j\in\mathbb{N}.

Accordingly, since
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})\to\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})
as
j\to\infty
, it follows that
(X_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded, and the bound (
93
) can be converted directly into the bound (
82
). This completes the proof of (i).

It remains to prove (ii). Toward that end, suppose that
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})
is any accumulation point of the sequence
((x_{j},X_{j},\tilde{q}_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. Up to passing to a subsequence, there is no loss of generality assuming that
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})=\lim_{j\to\infty}(x_{j},X_{j},\tilde{q}_{j})
.

As in the previous step of the proof, it is expedient to define
(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that, for all
j
sufficiently large,

q_{j}=\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})^{-1}x_{j},\quad Y_{j}=D^{2}\psi_{\nu}(q_{j}).

Let
q_{*}=\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
so that
q_{*}=\lim_{j\to\infty}q_{j}
. Let
Y_{*}=\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})X_{*}
. Since
X_{j}\to X_{*}
, it follows that
Y_{j}\to Y_{*}
as
j\to+\infty
.

As before, one can invoke Proposition
23
: this leads to the conclusion that there is a face
F_{*}\subseteq\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
such that

\{q_{*},q_{**}\}\subseteq F_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{*}q=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}.

Since
X_{*}=\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})^{-1}Y_{*}
, it follows that
X_{*}q=0
for each
q\in F_{*}
. ∎

7.3.
Normal Cones

With the exception of Proposition
24
, the proof of which is presented in Appendix
B
, the only loose end remaining in this section is the proof of Proposition
23
. The notion of normal cones will be used repeatedly in the proof. Therefore, before diving into the analysis, it will be necessary to review the definition and some relevant properties, which will significantly clarify the geometric arguments to come. Much of this information can be found in
[
51
]
or
[
49
]
.

First, let
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a convex set in some arbitrary dimension
m
and suppose
x_{*}\in C
. Recall that a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
determines a supporting hyperplane to
C
at a point
x_{*}
if

C\subseteq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\,\mid\,\langle x-x_{*},v\rangle\leq 0\right\},

or, equivalently, if

\langle v,x_{*}\rangle=\max\left\{\langle v,x\rangle\,\mid\,x\in C\right\}.

Thus, up to rescaling, the supporting hyperplanes to
C
at
x_{*}
are in one-to-one correspondence with such vectors
v
. The normal cone is nothing but the set of all such supporting hyperplanes with this extra multiplicity.

Definition 9
.

Given a convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
in dimension
m
, the normal cone
\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)
to
C
at a point
x\in C
is the convex cone defined by

\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)=\bigcap_{y\in C}\left\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\,\mid\,\langle v,x\rangle\geq\langle v,y\rangle\right\}.

The normal cone plays the same role for a convex set that the subdifferential does for a convex function.
25
25

25

Indeed, the normal cone equals the subdifferential of the indicator function
I_{C}^{\infty}
defined by
I^{\infty}_{C}(x)=0
if
x\in C
and
I^{\infty}_{C}(x)=+\infty
, otherwise.
In particular,
\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)
is nonempty at each boundary point
x\in\partial C
.

Remark 18
.

The ambient dimension
m
is included in the notation
\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}
to avoid the slight ambiguity that will arise later, when certain convex sets will be treated both as subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Note, for instance, that the normal cone to the square
[0,1]\times[0,1]\times\{0\}
changes according to whether one restricts the normal cone to vectors
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
, i.e., identifying
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, or considers
\mathcal{N}^{3}_{[0,1]\times[0,1]\times\{0\}}
as literally defined above.

The most basic example of the normal cone occurs when the set
C
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
of class
C^{1}
. In that case, if
N_{\partial C}
is the outward normal to
C
, then, for any
x\in\partial C
,

\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)=\{\lambda N_{\partial C}(x)\,\mid\,\lambda\geq 0\}.

A more interesting example, which will be needed shortly, occurs when
C=\{\varphi\leq 1\}
for some Finsler norm
\varphi
. The next lemma shows that the normal cone is nothing but the cone generated by the subdifferential in this case.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
q\in\{\varphi=1\}
, then

\mathcal{N}^{d}_{\{\varphi\leq 1\}}(q)=\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi(q)).

Proof.

This follows more-or-less immediately from (
19
) and the definition of the normal cone. ∎

There is another analogy with the subdifferential of a convex function. Even if the convex set in question fails to be
C^{1}
, the normal cone is always upper semicontinuous in a sense that can be made precise. This will be useful later so it is recorded now.

Lemma 9
.

Let
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a convex set in dimension
m
. The normal cone
\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}
is upper semicontinuous in the following sense: for any
x\in C
, if the limit superior of
\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}
at
x
is defined by

\limsup_{y\to x}\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(y)=\overline{\bigcap_{\epsilon>0}\bigcup_{y\in B_{\epsilon}(x)}\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(y)},

then

\limsup_{y\to x}\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(y)\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in C.

Proof.

If
v\in\limsup_{y\to x}\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(y)
, then there is a sequence
((x_{n},v_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
(x_{n},v_{n})\to(x,v)
as
n\to+\infty
and
v_{N}\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x_{N})
for all
N
. If
y\in C
, then the definition of the normal cone implies that

\langle v_{n},x_{n}\rangle\geq\langle v_{n},y\rangle\quad\text{for each}\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

which implies that
\langle v,x\rangle\geq\langle v,y\rangle
. Since this is true for any
y\in C
, this proves
v\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)
by definition. ∎

For the purposes of this paper, the main utility of the normal cone lies in the next lemma.

Lemma 10
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a convex set. Suppose that
x\in C
and
p\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)
. If there is a sequence
((x_{n},p_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{p\})
and a point
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that (i)
(x_{n},p_{n})\to(x,p)
as
n\to+\infty
; (ii)
p_{N}\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x_{N})
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
; and

\text{(iii)}\quad w^{\prime}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{p_{n}-p}{\|p_{n}-p\|},

then

\langle w^{\prime},x\rangle=\max\left\{\langle w^{\prime},y\rangle\,\mid\,y\in C\,\,\text{such that}\,\,p\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(y)\right\}.

See Figure
4
for a pictorial proof.

p
x_{n}
p_{n}
x
w^{\prime}
C

Figure 4.
The vector
w^{\prime}
describes the direction from which the sequence of support vectors
(p_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
approach the limit
p
. Notice that the point
x
has the largest
w^{\prime}
component among all points on the corresponding face of
C
.

The proof leans on a monotonicity formula, which is worth highlighting in its own right: if
C
is any convex set, then

(99)

\langle p_{2}-p_{1},x_{2}-x_{1}\rangle\geq 0\quad\text{for each}\quad x_{1},x_{2}\in C,\,\,(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x_{1})\times\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x_{2}).

This monotonicity formula captures the intuition that a convex set curves only ‘‘inwards,” and is a weak expression of the fact that, in the smooth setting, the principal curvatures are all nonnegative.
26
26

26

As is well known, the subdifferential of a convex function has a similar monotonicity property.
27
27

27

To see that the monotonicity formula holds, observe that if
x_{1},x_{2}\in C
and
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x_{1})\times\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x_{2})
, then the definition of the normal cone directly implies that

\langle p_{2}-p_{1},x_{2}-x_{1}\rangle=\langle p_{2},x_{2}-x_{1}\rangle+\langle p_{1},x_{1}-x_{2}\rangle\geq 0.

Proof of Lemma
10
.

Suppose that
y\in C
and
p\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(y)
. By the monotonicity formula (
99
),

\langle p_{n}-p,x_{n}-y\rangle\geq 0.

Dividing by
\|p_{n}-p\|
is harmless here, hence, after doing so and sending
n\to+\infty
, one finds

\langle w^{\prime},x-y\rangle\geq 0.

Since
y
was arbitrary and
p\in\mathcal{N}^{m}_{C}(x)
, the desired conclusion is now immediate. ∎

7.4.
Proof of Proposition
23

Fnally, the section concludes with the proof that the derivatives of the perturbed test functions are well-behaved in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. The proof of Proposition
23
will be presented in two parts, corresponding to parts (a) and (b) in the statement.

In the proof of part (a), the optimality condition (
91
) in the definition of the face
F_{*}
will arise from an application of Lemma
10
. In particular, upon setting
\hat{p}_{j}=\|D\psi^{\epsilon_{j}}_{\nu}(q_{j})\|^{-1}D\psi^{\epsilon_{j}}_{\nu}(q_{j})
, the proof of (
91
) proceeds by establishing that there is a
w_{*}^{\prime}\in\{e\}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
such that (
89
) holds and
28
28

28

Actually, it will also be necessary to consider the possibility that
w_{*}^{\prime}=0
, but this presents no additional difficulties.

(100)

\frac{w_{*}^{\prime}}{\|w_{*}^{\prime}\|}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\hat{p}_{j}-e}{\|\hat{p}_{j}-e\|}.

Once
w_{*}^{\prime}
is identified, the relation (
91
) will follow after an application of Lemma
10
.

To prove that it is possible to find
w_{*}^{\prime}
satisfying both (
89
) and (
100
), it is useful to write the derivatives of
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}
somewhat explicitly, or, more precisely, to write those of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}
explicitly. Recall that
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
is given by (
76
) and
g_{\nu}
, by (
70
). Thus, the first two derivatives of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
are explicitly determined by

(101)

\displaystyle Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\int_{B_{1}}\frac{\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})}{\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})^{2}}}D\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})\,\rho(y)\,dy,

(102)

\displaystyle D^{2}g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\int_{B_{1}}\frac{\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})}{\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})^{2}}}D^{2}\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})\,\rho(y)dy

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{B_{1}}\frac{\nu^{2}}{(\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})^{2})^{3/2}}D\text{dist}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})^{\otimes 2}\,\rho(y)dy.

Since
G_{p}
is convex, the distance function
\text{dist}(\cdot,G_{p})
is itself convex; hence there are enough (weak) derivatives available to justify these formulas.

To appreciate the role played by the derivatives of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
here, recall that since the level set
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}=1\}
is contained in the graph of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
by (
78
), it follows that the outward normal vector to this surface is given, for any
q\in\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}=1\}
, by the formula

(103)

\displaystyle\frac{D\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(q)}{\|D\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(q)\|}=-\frac{Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\|^{2}}}+\frac{e}{\sqrt{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\|^{2}}}

and the second fundamental form (matrix of principal curvatures) has a related expression

(104)

\displaystyle\frac{(\text{Id}-\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)\|^{-2}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)^{\otimes 2})D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)(\text{Id}-\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)\|^{-2}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)^{\otimes 2})}{\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)\|}=

\displaystyle\qquad\left(\text{Id}-\frac{Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}}{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\|^{2}}\right)D^{2}g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\left(\text{Id}-\frac{Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}}{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\|^{2}}\right).

As before, here
q^{\prime}
denotes the orthogonal projection of
q
onto
\{e\}^{\perp}
.

From the representations above, a straightforward computation shows that (
89
) implies (
100
) provided
w_{*}^{\prime}\neq 0
.

As will be shown shortly, the preceding discussion more-or-less amounts to a proof of part (a) of the proposition. Before proceeding to the proof of part (b), though, it will be necessary to extract some information concerning the “shapes” of possible accumulation points of the family
(g_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{0<\epsilon<\nu}
. That is the purpose of the next lemma.

Lemma 11
.

Let
G\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
be a convex set, fix a
\nu>0
, and define
g:\{\text{dist}(\cdot,G)\leq\nu\}\to[0,+\infty)
and
g^{\epsilon}:\{\text{dist}(\cdot,G)\leq\nu-\epsilon\}\to[0,+\infty)
by

g(x)=\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)^{2}},\quad g^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}g(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}.

If
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
is a sequence converging to zero and
(q_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
satisfies

(105)

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{j}^{\prime},G)<+\infty,

then the blow-up sequence
(d_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
defined by

d_{j}(y^{\prime})=\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\text{dist}(q^{\prime}_{j}+\epsilon_{j}y^{\prime},G)=\text{dist}(y^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{-1}(G-q^{\prime}_{j}))

has a subsequence
(d_{j_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
that converges locally uniformly to a convex function
d_{*}:\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
, which is
C^{1}
in the set
\{d_{*}>0\}
. Furthermore, the following formulas hold:

(106)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}Dg^{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(q_{j_{k}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\nu^{-1}\int_{B_{1}}d_{*}(y^{\prime})Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\,\rho(y^{\prime})dy^{\prime},

(107)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}D^{2}g^{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(q_{j_{k}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\nu^{-1}\int_{B_{1}}\left(d_{*}(y^{\prime})D^{2}d_{*}(y^{\prime})+Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\otimes Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\right)\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}.

In addition, if the subsequence
(q^{\prime}_{j_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to some point
q^{\prime}_{*}\in G
, then

(108)

Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G}(q^{\prime}_{*})\quad\text{for each}\quad y^{\prime}\in\{d_{*}>0\}.

Remark 19
.

Actually, if
q_{j_{k}}^{\prime}\to q_{*}^{\prime}
as in the final statement of the lemma, then one can further say that there is a shift
v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that
d_{*}
is given by

d_{*}(y^{\prime})=\text{dist}(y^{\prime},v^{\prime}+\mathcal{T}_{G}(q_{*}^{\prime}))=\text{dist}(y^{\prime}-v^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{G}(q_{*}^{\prime}),

where
\mathcal{T}_{G}(q_{*}^{\prime})
is the tangent cone to
G
at
q_{*}^{\prime}
.
29
29

29

See
[
49
]
for the definition of tangent cones.
Though it helps in understanding the geometric meaning of the lemma, this observation will not be needed in what follows.

Proof.

Begin with a priori estimates: it is well-known that distance functions are uniformly Lipschitz with constant one, that is,

\{d_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq W^{1,\infty}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1}),\quad\|Dd_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}=1\quad\text{for each}\quad j\in\mathbb{N}.

At the same time, by hypothesis,

\limsup_{j\to\infty}d_{j}(0)=\limsup_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{j}^{\prime},G)<+\infty.

This establishes that
\{d_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is locally bounded in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.

It is convenient to obtain slightly more information about
\{d_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. First, observe that it is possible to write

\frac{1}{2}d_{j}(y^{\prime})^{2}=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2}\|y^{\prime}-\tilde{y}^{\prime}\|^{2}\,\mid\,\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\epsilon_{j}^{-1}(G-x_{j}^{\prime})\right\}.

Thus, since this last expression has the form of an inf-convolution,
30
30

30

See
[
22
, Section 11]
or
[
24
, Appendix]
.

-D^{2}\left\{2^{-1}d_{j}^{2}\right\}\geq-\text{Id}\quad\text{in the viscosity sense in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d-1}.

As already mentioned in the proof of Proposition
4
above, such a viscosity inequality is equivalent to its distributional version, hence
D^{2}\{2^{-1}d_{j}^{2}\}\leq\text{Id}
holds distributionally. At the same time, since
G
is convex,
d_{j}
is convex for any
j
. In particular,
2^{-1}d_{j}^{2}
is also convex for all
j
. It follows that

0\leq D^{2}\left\{2^{-1}d_{j}^{2}\right\}\leq\text{Id}\quad\text{in the sense of distributions in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d-1}.

Thus,
2^{-1}d_{j}^{2}\in W^{2,\infty}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
for any
j
and the second derivative, which is
a priori
just a Radon measure, is represented by a function in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
. In particular, there is no abuse of notation writing it as

D^{2}\{2^{-1}d_{j}^{2}\}=d_{j}D^{2}d_{j}+Dd_{j}\otimes Dd_{j}

since this formula holds almost everywhere; and the positivity of
Dd_{j}\otimes Dd_{j}
implies

0\leq d_{j}D^{2}d_{j}\leq\text{Id}\quad\text{almost everywhere in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d-1}.

At last, the previous arguments show that there is a subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and a nonnegative convex function
d_{*}
such that

\displaystyle d_{j_{k}}\to d_{*}\quad\text{locally uniformly in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d-1},

\displaystyle Dd_{j_{k}}\to Dd_{*}\quad\text{locally uniformly in}\,\,\{d_{*}>0\},

\displaystyle d_{j_{k}}D^{2}d_{j_{k}}\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}d_{*}D^{2}d_{*}\quad\text{weakly in}\,\,L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1}).

In view of (
101
) and (
102
), these convergences imply (
106
) and (
107
).

It remains to prove that if
q_{j_{k}}^{\prime}\to q_{*}^{\prime}
as
k\to+\infty
, then
Dd_{*}
takes values in
\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G}(q^{\prime}_{*})
. Recall that if
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\setminus\overline{G}
, then
D\text{dist}(q^{\prime},G)=\|q^{\prime}-[q^{\prime}]\|^{-1}(q^{\prime}-[q^{\prime}])
, where
[q^{\prime}]
is the (unique) closest point in
G
to
x^{\prime}
.
31
31

31

See
[
51
, Section 1.2]
and
[
49
, Example 2.25]
.
In particular,
[q^{\prime}]\in\partial G
and
\|q^{\prime}-[q^{\prime}]\|^{-1}(q^{\prime}-[q^{\prime}])
is the normal vector to a supporting hyperplane to
G
at
[q^{\prime}]
. Thus,

D\text{dist}(q^{\prime},G)=\|q^{\prime}-[q^{\prime}]\|^{-1}(q^{\prime}-[q^{\prime}])\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G}([q^{\prime}]).

After rescaling, this becomes

Dd_{j}(y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G}([q_{j}^{\prime}+\epsilon_{j}y^{\prime}]).

Clearly,
[q_{j}^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime}]\to[q_{*}^{\prime}]=q_{*}^{\prime}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Therefore, by the upper semicontinuity of
\mathcal{N}_{G}^{d-1}
(i.e., Lemma
9
), for any
y^{\prime}\in\{d_{*}>0\}
,

Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})=\lim_{j\to\infty}Dd_{j_{k}}(y^{\prime})\in\limsup_{q^{\prime}\to q^{\prime}_{*}}\mathcal{N}_{G}^{d-1}(q^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G}(q_{*}^{\prime}).

This proves that
Dd_{*}
maps
\{d_{*}>0\}
into
\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G}(q^{\prime}_{*})
. ∎

At this point, the stage is set for the proof of the first part of Proposition
23
.

Proof of Proposition
23
, (a).

Apply Lemma
11
with
G
equal to
G_{p}=\partial\varphi^{*}(p)-\varphi^{*}(e)e
(using the identification of
\{e\}^{\perp}
with
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
to regard this as a convex set in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
) and the sequence
(q_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
determined as usual by projecting
(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
to
\{e\}^{\perp}
, see (
(ii)
). The lemma asserts that there is a convex function
d_{*}:\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
such that

(109)

Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})\quad\text{for each}\quad y^{\prime}\in\{d_{*}>0\}.

Further, note that the function
g
in the statement of the lemma coincides with
g_{\nu}
up to a constant, hence the conclusions concerning its derivatives imply the desired conclusions (
89
) and (
90
). In particular, there is a subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that

(110)

-\lim_{k\to\infty}\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(q_{j_{k}}^{\prime})=\nu^{-1}\int_{B_{1}}d_{*}(y^{\prime})Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\,\rho(y^{\prime})dy^{\prime}

As in the discussion preceding this proof, see (
89
), denote this vector by
w_{*}^{\prime}
.

It remains to show that

\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q_{*}\rangle=\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q_{**}\rangle=\max\left\{\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\right\}.

This is trivial if
w_{*}^{\prime}=0
; hence, in the rest of the proof, assume that
w_{*}^{\prime}\neq 0
.

Start with
q_{**}
, the easier of the two: this part is a straightforward application of Lemma
10
. Consider the convex set
\{\varphi\leq 1\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, one knows that
\tilde{q}_{j}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}))
by hypothesis and, thus, by inversion (
21
),

\varphi^{*}(D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}))^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})\in\partial\varphi(\tilde{q}_{j}).

From this and Lemma
8
, it follows that if one defines the sequence
(\hat{p}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
by letting
\hat{p}_{j}=\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})
, then

\hat{p}_{j}\in\mathcal{N}^{d}_{\{\varphi\leq 1\}}(\tilde{q}_{j})\quad\text{for each}\quad j\in\mathbb{N}.

Furthermore, since
q_{j}\to q_{*}
as
j\to+\infty
and
q_{*}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
, Proposition
12
says that
\varphi
touches
\psi_{\nu}
from below at
x_{*}
. Thus,

\lim_{j\to\infty}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})=D\psi_{\nu}(q_{*})=D\varphi(q_{*})\overset{\eqref{E: subdifferential basic identity}}{=}\varphi^{*}(p)^{-1}p,

It follows that
\hat{p}_{j}\to e
as
j\to+\infty
. At the same time, by the definition (
89
) of
w_{*}^{\prime}
and the representation formula (
103
),

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\hat{p}_{j_{k}}-e}{\|\hat{p}_{j_{k}}-e\|}=\frac{w_{*}^{\prime}}{\|w_{*}^{\prime}\|}.

At last, by Lemma
10
applied to
(\tilde{q}_{j_{k}},\hat{p}_{j_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, plus another application of Lemma
8
,

\displaystyle\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q_{**}\rangle

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\{\varphi\leq 1\}\,\,\text{such that}\,\,e\in\mathcal{N}^{d}_{\{\varphi\leq 1\}}(q)\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(e)\right\}\overset{\eqref{E: zero hom}}{=}\max\left\{\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\right\}.

It remains to consider
q_{*}
. This boils down to yet another application of Lemma
10
, except for the (nontrivial) complication that the convex set in question varies with
j
.

For any
j
, consider the convex set
C_{j}=\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}\leq 1\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By assumption,
q_{j}\in\partial C_{j}
for any
j
and
q_{j}\to q_{*}
as
j\to+\infty
. By the discussion preceding Lemma
8
,

\hat{p}_{j}=\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})\in\mathcal{N}^{d}_{C_{j}}(q_{j})\quad\text{for each}\quad j\in\mathbb{N}.

At the same time, if the set
[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}
is defined for
\mu>0
by

[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}=\left\{q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\,\mid\,\text{dist}(q,\text{bdry}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)))\geq\mu\right\},

then it is not difficult to show that, for any
\epsilon<\mu
,

\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)=e\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)};

see Proposition
38
in Appendix
B.3
for the proof. In particular, for any
\mu>0
, there is a
J(\mu)\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
j\geq J(\mu)
, then

e\in\mathcal{N}^{d}_{C_{j}}(q)\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}.

Thus, by the monotonicity formula (
99
), for any
j\geq J(\mu)
,

\langle\hat{p}_{j}-e,q_{j}-q\rangle\geq 0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}.

Dividing by
\|\hat{p}_{j}-e\|
and sending
j\to+\infty
, this becomes

\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q_{*}-q\rangle\geq 0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}.

In the limit
\mu\to 0^{+}
, this yields the remaining half of (
91
). ∎

To complete the proof of Proposition
23
, it only remains to prove part (b) concerning the asymptotic behavior of the Hessians. Here is where the explicit representations (
89
) and (
90
) and the gradient constraint (
108
) are used.

To appreciate the significance of the matrix
A_{*}^{\prime}
obtained in the limit (
90
), first, notice that it can be expressed in the form

\displaystyle A_{*}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}D^{2}g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\nu^{-1}\int_{B_{1}}\left(d_{*}(y^{\prime})D^{2}d_{*}(y^{\prime})+Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\otimes Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\right)\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}.

Further, the formula (
104
) implies that

\displaystyle(\text{Id}-e\otimes e)Y_{*}(\text{Id}-e\otimes e)=\lim_{j\to\infty}(\text{Id}-\hat{p}_{j}^{\otimes 2})Y_{j}(\text{Id}-\hat{p}_{j}^{\otimes 2})

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{j\to\infty}\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})\|\left(\text{Id}-\frac{Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}}{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}^{\prime})\|^{2}}\right)D^{2}g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}^{\prime})\left(\text{Id}-\frac{Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}}{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}^{\prime})\|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\|D\psi_{\nu}(q_{*})\|A_{*}^{\prime}

since
Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}^{\prime})\to Dg_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})=0
as
j\to+\infty
. Thus, the constraints (
94
) on
Y_{*}
will follow from an analysis of the integral formula for
A_{*}
, which amounts to studying the “shape” of the limiting distance function
d_{*}
.

Finally, before entering into the proof of part (b), there is still one more convex analytic consideration that will facilitate the proof, basically an elementary lemma.

Lemma 12
.

Given any dimension
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any closed convex cone
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
, let
\mu
be a finite positive Borel measure on
\mathcal{N}
and let
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
be a face of
\mathcal{N}
. If
\int_{\mathcal{N}}\|v\|\,\mu(dv)<+\infty
and the mean of
\mu
is in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, that is,

\int_{\mathcal{N}}v\,\mu(dv)\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime},

then
\mu
is supported on
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
(i.e.,
\mu(\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime})=0
).

For the reader’s convenience, a proof of the lemma can be found in Appendix
A
.

Proof of Proposition
23
, (b).

The Hessian bounds (
93
) follow from the estimates in Section
5
. Indeed, at the limit point
q_{*}
, one knows that
D\psi_{\nu}(q_{*})=D\varphi(q_{*})
, and control over
D\varphi
is furnished by the
\delta
-nondegeneracy condition (
61
). Similarly, control over the curvature of the level sets of the sequence
\{\psi^{\epsilon_{j}}_{\nu}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
can be gleaned from the explicit formula for the second derivative of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}
. For the full details, the reader is referred to Lemma
18
in Appendix
A
.

That only leaves the more interesting statement, namely, the Hessian constraint (
94
). Toward that end, it suffices to establish that

(111)

A_{*}^{\prime}(q-q_{*})=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}.

To see why, assume for the moment that (
111
) holds. Note that
Y_{*}q_{*}=0
since, by positive homogeneity,

\displaystyle Y_{*}q_{*}=\lim_{k\to\infty}D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(q_{j_{k}})q_{j_{k}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}(0)=0.

Therefore, for any
q\in F_{*}
,

\displaystyle\langle Y_{*}q,q\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Y_{*}(q-q_{*}),q-q_{*}\rangle.

At the same time, since
F_{*}\subseteq\partial\varphi^{*}(p)=\partial\varphi^{*}(e)
, the representation (
20
) implies that
q-q_{*}=(\text{Id}-e\otimes e)(q-q_{*})
for any
q\in F_{*}
. Thus, by the discussion preceding Lemma
12
,

\displaystyle\langle Y_{*}q,q\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\text{Id}-e\otimes e)Y_{*}(\text{Id}-e\otimes e)(q-q_{*}),q-q_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\|D\psi_{\nu}(q_{*})\|\langle A_{*}^{\prime}(q-q_{*}),q-q_{*}\rangle=0.

Since
Y_{*}\geq 0
by convexity, the identity
\langle Y_{*}q,q\rangle=0
actually implies
Y_{*}q=0
. Therefore, (
111
) implies (
94
) as claimed.

It only remains to prove (
111
). For the rest of the proof,
G_{p}
is treated as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
so that
\mathcal{N}_{G_{p}}^{d-1}
makes sense. In what follows, given a vector
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
, let
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w^{\prime})
denote the face of
\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
such that

w^{\prime}\in\text{rint}(\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w^{\prime})).

By (
42
), there is a unique such face, hence the map
w^{\prime}\mapsto\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w^{\prime})
is well-defined.

To ease the exposition, the proof will be presented arguing by cases.

Case:
w_{*}^{\prime}=0
and
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)=\{0\}
.

This case is particularly simple. By (
106
),

\int_{B_{1}\cap\{d_{*}>0\}}d_{*}(y^{\prime})Dd(y^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy=\int_{B_{1}}d_{*}(y^{\prime})Dd(y^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy=w_{*}^{\prime}=0.

At the same time, since
Dd_{*}\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
pointwise by (
109
), this last integral has the form described in Lemma
12
. Applying that lemma with
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=\{0\}
, and
\mu
the measure on
\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
given by

\int_{\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})}f(v)\,\mu(dv)=\int_{B_{1}\cap\{d_{*}>0\}}f(d_{*}(y^{\prime})Dd_{*}(y^{\prime}))\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime},

one finds

(112)

Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})=0\quad\text{for each}\quad y^{\prime}\in\{d_{*}>0\}\cap B_{1}.

It follows that
D^{2}d_{*}(y^{\prime})=0
for a.e.
y^{\prime}\in B_{1}
, and, thus,
A_{*}^{\prime}=0
by (
107
). Hence (
111
) holds trivially in this case.

Case:
w_{*}^{\prime}=0
and
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)\neq\{0\}
.

First, it is useful to note that
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)
has a special form: specifically,
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)
is what is referred to in
[
50
]
as the
lineality
of
\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
, that is, the maximal linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
contained in
\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
. At the same time, by the definition of normal cone, this is equivalent to saying that
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)
is the maximal subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that

(113)

G_{p}-q_{*}^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)^{\perp}.

Next, applying Lemma
12
as in the last case, observe that the inclusion
w_{*}^{\prime}=0\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)
implies that

Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)\quad\text{for each}\quad y^{\prime}\in B_{1}\cap\{d_{*}>0\}.

Since
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)
is a linear space, the last inclusion can be differentiated to find

D^{2}d_{*}(y^{\prime})(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)\quad\text{for a.e.}\quad y^{\prime}\in B_{1}\cap\{d_{*}>0\}.

Integrating over
B_{1}\cap\{d_{*}>0\}
, one finds from (
107
) that
A_{*}^{\prime}
has the same property, that is, the range of
A_{*}^{\prime}
is contained in
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)
. Hence, since
A_{*}^{\prime}
is a symmetric matrix,

(114)

\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(0)^{\perp}\subseteq\text{Ker}(A_{*}^{\prime}),

where
\text{Ker}(A_{*}^{\prime})
denotes the kernel of
A_{*}^{\prime}
.

Combining (
113
) and (
114
), one deduces that, for any
q^{\prime}\in G_{p}
,

A_{*}^{\prime}(q^{\prime}-q_{*}^{\prime})=0.

Since
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)=G_{p}+\varphi^{*}(e)e
, this implies that

A_{*}^{\prime}(q-q_{*})=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p),

which is nothing but (
111
).

Case:
w_{*}^{\prime}\neq 0
.

This case is very similar to the previous one in so far as the use of the map
w^{\prime}\mapsto\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w^{\prime})
is concerned. By definition of
F_{*}
(i.e., (
92
)), one knows that

(115)

\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q\rangle=\langle w_{*}^{\prime},q_{*}\rangle\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}.

In particular,

F_{*}-q_{*}\subseteq\{w_{*}^{\prime}\}^{\perp}.

In fact, this can be upgraded to

(116)

F_{*}-q_{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime})^{\perp}.

Indeed, to see that (
116
) holds, suppose that
q\in F_{*}
and let
q^{\prime}
denote its orthogonal projection to
\{e\}^{\perp}
as usual. By (
115
),

w_{*}^{\prime}\in\{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})\,\mid\,\langle w^{\prime},q\rangle=\langle w^{\prime},q_{*}\rangle\}=:\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(q).

At the same time,
\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(q)
is an exposed face of
\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime})
since, by the definition of normal cone and the fact that
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
is being identified with
\{e\}^{\perp}
,

\langle w^{\prime},q\rangle=\langle w^{\prime},q^{\prime}\rangle\leq\langle w^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{*}\rangle=\langle w^{\prime},q_{*}\rangle\quad\text{for each}\quad w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{d-1}_{G_{p}}(q_{*}^{\prime}).

Therefore, since
w_{*}^{\prime}\in\text{rint}(\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime}))\cap\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(q)
, the defining property of faces (Definition
6
) implies

\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(q).

Since
q
was arbitrary, this leads to the inclusion
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime})\subseteq\bigcap_{q\in F_{*}}\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}(q)
, which is equivalent to (
116
).

Finally, once again, Lemma
12
implies that

Dd_{*}(y^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime})\quad\text{for each}\quad y^{\prime}\in B_{1}\cap\{d_{*}>0\},

and hence

D^{2}d_{*}(y^{\prime})(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})\subseteq\text{span}(\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime}))\quad\text{for a.e.}\quad y^{\prime}\in B_{1}\cap\{d_{*}>0\},

where
\text{span}(\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime}))
denotes the linear span of
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime})
.
In particular, combining this last observation with (
116
), one concludes as in the last step that

F_{*}-q_{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{N}}(w_{*}^{\prime})^{\perp}\subseteq\text{Ker}(A_{*}^{\prime}),

which is precisely (
111
). The proof of (
94
) is complete. ∎

Part III
The General Case and Applications

In this final part of the paper, the cone-type comparison results proved above are used to establish general comparison principles. This is achieved by doubling variables. The applications mentioned already in the introduction are treated in the final section, Section
10
.

8.
General Cone Comparison Principles

Before delving into comparison proofs involving variable-doubling arguments, the next proposition recapitulates the
C^{1,1}
results proved above in the context of a general Finsler norm
\varphi
. In effect, it shows that
(\varphi_{\zeta})_{\zeta>0}
provides a good class of test functions for elliptic PDE involving the operators
G_{\varphi}^{*}
and
G_{*}^{\varphi}
.

Proposition 25
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
(\varphi_{\zeta})_{\zeta>0}
be the regularized norms of Theorem
5
. Let
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be a bounded open set, and let
\mathcal{H}
be a function satisfying (
63
) and (
64
).

If
u\in USC(\overline{V})
satisfies

-\mathcal{H}(x,Du,D^{2}u,G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,V,

and if there is a
\zeta\in(0,1)
such that

\max\{u(x)-\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\}>\max\{u(x)-\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\,\mid\,x\in\partial V\},

then there is a triple
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})\in V\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
such that

\displaystyle u(x_{*})-\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})=\max\{u(x)-\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\},

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}),X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{**}-\zeta\|D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})\|^{-1}D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})))\leq 0,

\displaystyle X_{*}q_{**}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})X_{*}\leq\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi)\zeta^{-1}\text{Id},

where
\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi)>0
depends only on
\varphi
.

Proof.

This is an application of Theorem
6
. Indeed, reasoning as in Section
3.1
, one observes that

-\mathcal{H}(x,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi_{\zeta}}^{\zeta}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,V.

Furthermore,
\varphi_{\zeta}
satisfies (
60
) with radius
\zeta
and (
61
) with constant
\delta=\delta(\varphi)
by Theorem
5
. Taken together with the other hypotheses, these observations establish that Theorem
6
applies, and the theorem directly yields the desired conclusion with
\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi):=\Gamma(\delta(\varphi))
.
∎

8.1.
Curved Cones

The cone comparison principle proved so far only treats
\varphi
itself as a test function. In various contexts, it useful to know that these results extend to “curved” cones of the form
h(\varphi)
for some increasing function
h
.

Proposition 26
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
(\varphi_{\zeta})_{\zeta>0}
be the regularized norms of Theorem
5
. Let
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be a bounded open set, and let
\mathcal{H}
be a function satisfying (
63
) and (
64
). Suppose that
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth and increasing.

If
u
is an upper semicontinuous function in
\overline{V}
satisfying

-\mathcal{H}(x,Du,D^{2}u,G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,V,

and if there is a point
x_{0}\in V
and a
\zeta\in(0,1)
such that

u(x_{0})-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{0}))=\max\{u(x)-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x))\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\}>\max\{u(x)-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x))\,\mid\,x\in\partial V\},

then there is a triple
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})\in V\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
such that

(117)

\displaystyle u(x_{*})-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}))=\max\{u(x)-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x))\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\},

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},h^{\prime}(u(x_{*}))D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}),X_{*}^{h},Q_{X^{h}_{*}}(q_{**}-\zeta\|D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})\|^{-1}D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})))\leq 0,

\displaystyle X_{*}q_{**}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})X_{*}\leq c(\varphi)\zeta^{-1}\text{Id},

where
X^{h}_{*}
is derived from
h
,
X_{*}
, and
x_{*}
via the rule

X^{h}_{*}=h^{\prime}(u(x_{*}))X_{*}+h^{\prime\prime}(u(x_{*}))D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})\otimes D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}).

The proof of the proposition amounts to writing down the differential inequality governing the function
v=h^{-1}(u)
, as explained next.

Proof.

By hypothesis, there is an
r>0
such that if
\overline{B}_{r}(\partial V)=\bigcup_{y\in\partial V}\overline{B}_{r}(y)
, then

\max\{u(x)-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x))\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\}>\max\{u(x)-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x))\,\mid\,x\in\overline{B}_{r}(\partial V)\}.

To begin with, there is no loss of generality assuming that
h^{\prime}
is positive and
h(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}
; otherwise, one can perturb
h
by setting
h_{\epsilon}(u)=h(u)+\epsilon u
, and then recover the desired result in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Up to subtracting a constant from
u
, which does not change the equation, one can assume that

\max\left\{u(x)-h(\varphi_{\zeta}(x))\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\right\}=0,

hence
u\leq h(\varphi_{\zeta})
holds in
\overline{V}
with equality at some point in
V
.

In particular, if
v=h^{-1}(u)
, then

\max\left\{v(x)-\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\right\}=0>\max\left\{v(x)-\varphi_{\zeta}(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{B}_{r}(\partial V)\right\}.

A straightforward computation shows that
v
satisfies

-\mathcal{H}(x,h^{\prime}(u(x))Dv,\mathcal{X}(v,Dv,D^{2}v),G_{\varphi}^{*}(h^{\prime}(u(x))Dv,\mathcal{X}(u(x),Dv,D^{2}v)))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,V,

where
\mathcal{X}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\to\mathcal{S}_{d}
is the map

\mathcal{X}(u,p,X)=h^{\prime}(u)X+h^{\prime\prime}(u)p\otimes p.

This differential inequality fits into the framework of Proposition
25
. Therefore, by that proposition, there is a triple
(x_{*},X_{*},q_{**})\in V\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi=1\}
such that

(118)

\displaystyle v(x_{*})-\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})=0=\max\left\{v(x)-\varphi(x)\,\mid x\in\overline{V}\right\},

(119)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},h^{\prime}(u(x_{*}))D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}),X^{h}_{*},Q_{X^{h}_{*}}(q_{**}-\zeta\|D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})\|^{-1}D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})))\leq 0,

(120)

\displaystyle X_{*}q^{\epsilon}_{**}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*})X_{*}\leq c(\varphi)\zeta^{-1}\text{Id}.

where
X^{h}_{*}=\mathcal{X}(u(x_{*}),D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}),X_{*})
.
Note that (
118
) implies (
117
) since
h
is monotone. Therefore, the proof is complete. ∎

9.
Comparison Results in Bounded Domains

In this section, general comparison principles are proved by combining the cone comparison results of the previous sections with the standard uniqueness machinery from the theory of viscosity solutions.

9.1.
The Dirichlet Problem

Consider the following generalization of (
1
), namely,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0&\text{in}\,\,U,\\
-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{*}^{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u))\geq 0&\text{in}\,\,U,\\
u=g&\text{on}\,\,\partial U,\end{array}\right.

where the function
\mathcal{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function satisfying the following two assumptions. First, assume there is a constant
\lambda=\lambda(\mathcal{H})>0
such that, for any
(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
s,r,a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, and
X,Y\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
,

(124)

\displaystyle\lambda|s-r|\leq\mathcal{H}(x,s,p,Y,b)-\mathcal{H}(x,r,p,X,a)\quad\text{if}\,\,s\leq r,\,\,Y\geq X,\,\,b\geq a;

Next, assume there is a
\zeta=\zeta(\mathcal{H})\in(0,1)
such that if
X,Y\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
satisfy, for some constants
C,T>0
,

\displaystyle-CT\left(\begin{array}[]{r l}\text{Id}&0\\
0&\text{Id}\end{array}\right)\leq\left(\begin{array}[]{r l}X&0\\
0&-Y\end{array}\right)\leq CT\left(\begin{array}[]{r l}\text{Id}&-\text{Id}\\
-\text{Id}&\text{Id}\end{array}\right),

then there is a continuous function
\omega_{C}:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
depending only on
C
such that
\omega_{C}(0)=0
and, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
r\in\mathbb{R}
, and
q\in B_{C}(0)
,
32
32

32

Here
D(\varphi_{\zeta}^{2})
denotes the derivative of the function
x\mapsto\varphi_{\zeta}(x)^{2}
. In particular, if
\varphi_{\zeta}=\|\cdot\|
, then
D(\varphi_{\zeta}^{2})(x-y)=2(x-y)
.

(125)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x,r,2^{-1}TD(\varphi_{\zeta}^{2})(x-y),X,Q_{X}(q))-\mathcal{H}(y,r,2^{-1}TD(\varphi_{\zeta}^{2})(x-y),X,Q_{X}(q))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\omega_{C}(\|x-y\|+T\|x-y\|^{2}).

For examples of functions
\mathcal{H}
for which this last condition holds, see Remarks
21
and
22
below.

The comparison principle for (
9.1
) reads as follows:

Theorem 8
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set, and
\mathcal{H}
be a continuous function such that (
124
) and (
125
) both hold for some fixed parameters
\lambda(\mathcal{H})>0
and
\zeta(\mathcal{H})\in(0,1)
. If
u\in USC(\overline{U})
and
v\in LSC(\overline{U})
are such that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U,

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,v,Dv,D^{2}v,\mathcal{G}_{*}^{\varphi}(Dv,D^{2}v))\geq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U,

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\max\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\partial U\right\}\leq 0,

then

\max\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\right\}\leq 0.

The proof proceeds using the variable-doubling technique. In particular, given
\beta>0
, consider the function

\Phi(x,y)=u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta},

where
\varphi_{\zeta}
is the regularized version of
\varphi
from Theorem
5
and
\zeta=\zeta(\mathcal{H})
is the parameter from (
125
). The next result asserts that, compared to the cone comparison results proved earlier (i.e., Propositions
25
and
26
), the addition of a second variable causes no additional difficulties.

Proposition 27
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm and
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set. Given a parameter
\zeta\in(0,1)
, let
\varphi_{\zeta}
be the regularized version of
\varphi
from Theorem
5
. Let
\mathcal{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function satisfying assumptions (
63
) and (
64
).

Suppose that
u\in USC(\overline{U})
and
v\in LSC(\overline{U})
are such that

(126)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u))

\displaystyle\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U,

(127)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Dv,D^{2}v,G_{*}^{\varphi}(Dv,D^{2}v))

\displaystyle\geq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U.

If for some
\beta>0
one has that

(128)

\displaystyle\sup

\displaystyle\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in U\times U\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad>\max\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in\partial(U\times U)\right\},

then there are triples
(x_{*},X_{*},q^{u}_{**}),(y_{*},Y_{*},q^{v}_{**})\in U\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
and a constant
C(\varphi)>0
depending only on
\varphi
such that, after defining
T_{*}\geq 0
and
\mu_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

T_{*}=\beta^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{2},\quad\mu_{*}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\beta^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{3}D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*}),&\text{if}\,\,x_{*}\neq y_{*},\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

one has

(129)

\displaystyle u(x_{*})-v(y_{*})-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{4}}{4\beta}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\max\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U}\right\},

(130)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},u(x_{*}),\mu_{*},X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{**}^{u}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*}))\leq 0\quad\text{if}\,\,x_{*}\neq y_{*},

(131)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},u(x_{*}),0,0,0)\leq 0\quad\text{if}\,\,x_{*}=y_{*},,

(132)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(y_{*},u(y_{*}),\mu_{*},Y_{*},Q_{Y_{*}}(q^{v}_{**}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*}))\geq 0\quad\text{if}\,\,x_{*}\neq y_{*},

(133)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(y_{*},u(y_{*}),0,0,0)\geq 0\quad\text{if}\,\,x_{*}=y_{*},

(134)

\displaystyle X_{*}(q^{u}_{**}-q^{v}_{**})=Y_{*}(q^{u}_{**}-q^{v}_{**})=0,,

\displaystyle-C(\varphi)T_{*}\zeta^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}\text{Id}&0\\
0&\text{Id}\end{array}\right)\leq\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}X_{*}&0\\
0&-Y_{*}\end{array}\right)\leq C(\varphi)T_{*}\zeta^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}\text{Id}&-\text{Id}\\
-\text{Id}&\text{Id}\end{array}\right),

Remark 20
.

Notice that if, as in the previous proposition, there is a matrix
Y_{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
and vectors
q_{**}^{u},q_{**}^{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
Y_{*}q_{**}^{u}=Y_{*}q_{**}^{v}
, then, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

Q_{Y_{*}}(q^{v}_{**}+\xi)=Q_{Y_{*}}(q_{**}^{u}+\xi).

In particular, in this scenario, (
132
) is equivalent to

-\mathcal{H}(y_{*},u(y_{*}),\mu_{*},Y_{*},Q_{Y_{*}}(q^{u}_{**}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*})\leq 0.

As will become clear in the proof of Theorem
8
below, this means the discontinuity of
G_{\varphi}^{*}
and
G_{*}^{\varphi}
has effectively been circumvented, and the rest of the variable-doubling argument can be conducted as in the case of equations with continuous coefficients.

Before going into the proof of Proposition
27
, here are some remarks on the regularity condition (
125
):

Remark 21
.

Note that (
125
) is a generalization of the standard assumption guaranteeing comparison in the viscosity theory for second-order elliptic operators, see, for instance,
[
24
, Section 3]
. Indeed, (
125
) becomes the assumption in that reference if
\varphi_{\zeta}=\alpha^{-1}\|\cdot\|
for some
\alpha>0
(cf. Remark
12
).

In particular, as usual, if
\mathcal{H}
has the following “separable” form

\mathcal{H}(x,s,p,X,a)=\mathcal{H}_{0}(s,p,X,a)-f(x),

where
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is monotone increasing in the third and fourth arguments and
f
is uniformly continuous, then (
125
) holds.

Remark 22
.

The computation of
[
24
, Example 3.6]
applies just as well in this setting. Thus, for instance, if
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
is uniformly Lipschitz, that is, if there is a
K>0
such that, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|\sigma(x)-\sigma(y)|\leq K\|x-y\|,

then the operator
\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}
given by

\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}(x,r,p,X,a)=-u+\sigma(x)^{2}a

satisfies (
125
). Hence, setting
c(x)=\sigma(x)^{2}
, Theorem
8
applies to the problem (
2
) presented in the introduction.

Taking Proposition
27
for granted for the moment, here is the proof of Theorem
8
.

Proof of Theorem
8
.

Argue by contradiction, that is, assume that

(141)

\max\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\right\}>0\geq\max\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\partial U\right\}.

Let
\zeta=\zeta(\mathcal{H})\in(0,1)
be as in the hypotheses and let
\varphi_{\zeta}
be the regularized norm of Theorem
5
.

Let
\beta>0
. Assume that there is a
\beta_{*}\in(0,1)
such that, for any
\beta\in(0,\beta_{*})
,

(142)

\displaystyle\sup

\displaystyle\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in U\times U\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad>\max\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in\partial(U\times U)\right\}.

The existence of
\beta_{*}
follows from (
141
), as will be verified at the end of this proof.

Taking
\beta<\beta_{*}
and applying Proposition
27
, one obtains two triples

(x_{*},X_{*},q^{u}_{**}),(y_{*},Y_{*},q^{v}_{**})\in U\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}

such that the conditions (
129
)-(
27
) all hold. As in the statement of the proposition, it is convenient to let
T_{*}=\beta^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{2}
. The crux of the proof lies in establishing the following inequality

(143)

\lambda(u(x_{*})-v(y_{*}))\leq\omega_{C}(\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|+T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2}),

where
\lambda=\lambda(\mathcal{H})
is as in the hypotheses and
\omega_{C}
is as in (
125
) for some well-chosen
C
.

If
x_{*}=y_{*}
, then this follows immediately from (
131
), (
133
), and (
124
). Indeed, here in the application of (
124
), one needs to observe that, by (
141
) and the choice of the point
(x_{*},y_{*})
,

(144)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<\sup\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,x,y\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U}\right\}\leq u(x_{*})-v(y_{*}).

Thus, if
x_{*}=y_{*}
, one invokes (
124
) and (
125
) (with an arbitrary
C
) to find

\displaystyle\lambda(u(x_{*})-v(y_{*}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}(x_{*},v(y_{*}),0,0,0)-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},u(x_{*}),0,0,0)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}(x_{*},v(y_{*}),0,0,0)-\mathcal{H}(y_{*},v(y_{*}),0,0,0)

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{C}(\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|+T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2}).

Hence, in what follows, assume
x_{*}\neq y_{*}
.

As explained in Remark
20
, when
x_{*}\neq y_{*}
, (
132
) combines with (
134
) to yield the following inequality

(145)

-\mathcal{H}(y_{*},v(y_{*}),\mu_{*},Y_{*},Q_{Y_{*}}(q^{u}_{**}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*})\geq 0,

where
\mu_{*}=\beta^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{3}D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})
. At this stage, the goal is to apply (
124
) and (
125
) to obtain (
143
).

Toward that end, note that, by (
27
),
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
satisfy the following inequality

-C(\varphi)T_{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}\text{Id}&0\\
0&\text{Id}\end{array}\right)\leq\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}X_{*}&0\\
0&-Y_{*}\end{array}\right)\leq C(\varphi)T_{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}\text{Id}&-\text{Id}\\
-\text{Id}&\text{Id}\end{array}\right).

Furthermore, observe that since
\{q_{**}^{u},q_{**}^{v}\}\subseteq\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
, there holds

\left\|q_{**}^{u}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*}\right\|,\left\|q_{**}^{v}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*}\right\|\leq\max\left\{\|q\|\,\mid\,\varphi_{\zeta}(q)=1\right\}+1.

Thus, if
C=C(\varphi)+\max\{\|q\|\,\mid\,\varphi_{\zeta}(q)=1\}+1
, then one can invoke (
125
) to find

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x_{*},v(y_{*}),\mu_{*},X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{u}^{**}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu))-\mathcal{H}(y_{*},v(y_{*}),\mu_{*},Y_{*},Q_{Y_{*}}(q^{u}_{**}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\omega_{C}(\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|+T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2}).

Adding this last expression to (
130
) and invoking (
145
) and (
124
), one obtains

\displaystyle\lambda(u(x_{*})-v(y_{*}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}(x_{*},v(y_{*}),\mu_{*},X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{**}^{u}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*}))

\displaystyle\quad-\mathcal{H}(x_{*},u(x_{*}),\mu_{*},X_{*},Q_{X_{*}}(q_{**}^{u}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*}))

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{C}(\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|+T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{H}(y_{*},v(y_{*}),\mu_{*},Y_{*},Q_{Y_{*}}(q_{**}^{u}-\zeta\|\mu_{*}\|^{-1}\mu_{*}))

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{C}(\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|+T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2}).

Thus, (
143
) holds, as claimed.

Here is the conclusion of the proof: by (
143
) and (
144
), one deduces that

\displaystyle\sup\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq u(x_{*})-v(y_{*})\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\omega_{C}(\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|+T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2}).

In view of the running assumption (
141
), this leads to a contradiction provided

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\left(\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|+T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2}\right)=0.

Toward that end, by definition of
T_{*}
, it suffices to establish that

(146)

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{4}}{4\beta}=0.

Indeed, notice that, by definition of
T_{*}
,

T_{*}\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|^{2}\leq\max\left\{\|q\|^{2}\,\mid\,\varphi_{\zeta}(q)=1\right\}\cdot\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{4}}{\beta}.

Thus, the proof is complete as soon as (
142
) and (
146
) are established. Both are fairly standard claims; nonetheless, the details are provided next for completeness.

In order to avoid confusion in what follows, make the dependence on
\beta
explicit by setting
(x_{*}^{\beta},y_{*}^{\beta})=(x_{*},y_{*})
. Since
\overline{U}
is compact, there is no great loss of generality in assuming that the limits
x_{\infty}=\lim_{\beta\to 0^{+}}x_{*}^{\beta}
and
y_{\infty}=\lim_{\beta\to 0^{+}}y_{*}^{\beta}
exist; otherwise, pass to a subsequence. In view of the computation (
144
),

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}^{\beta}-y_{*}^{\beta})^{4}}{4\beta}

\displaystyle\leq u(x_{*}^{\beta})-v(y_{*}^{\beta})

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{u(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\right\}-\inf\left\{v(y)\,\mid\,y\in\overline{U}\right\}<+\infty.

It follows that
x_{\infty}=y_{\infty}
.

Since
u
is upper semicontinuous and
v
, lower semicontinuous, one finds

\displaystyle u(x_{\infty})-v(x_{\infty})

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{\beta\to 0^{+}}u(x_{*}^{\beta})-\liminf_{\beta\to 0^{+}}v(y_{*}^{\beta})

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\left[u(x_{*}^{\beta})-v(y_{*}^{\beta})-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}^{\beta}-y_{*}^{\beta})^{4}}{4\beta}\right].

At the same time,

u(x_{\infty})-v(x_{\infty})\leq\sup\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in U\}\leq\liminf_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\left[u(x_{*}^{\beta})-v(y_{*}^{\beta})-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}^{\beta}-y_{*}^{\beta})^{4}}{4\beta}\right].

This proves
u(x_{\infty})=\limsup_{\beta\to 0^{+}}u(x_{*}^{\beta})
and
v(x_{\infty})=\liminf_{\beta\to 0^{+}}v(y_{*}^{\beta})
. Therefore, after revisiting the last inequality, one finds

\displaystyle u(x_{\infty})-v(x_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\beta\to 0^{+}}u(x_{*}^{\beta})-\liminf_{\beta\to 0^{+}}v(x_{*}^{\beta})-\limsup_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}^{\beta}-y_{*}^{\beta})^{4}}{4\beta}

\displaystyle=u(x_{\infty})-v(x_{\infty})-\limsup_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}^{\beta})-y_{*}^{\beta})^{4}}{4\beta}.

This proves
(4\beta)^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}^{\beta}-y_{*}^{\beta})^{4}\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, as claimed. Furthermore, the argument showed that

\lim_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\sup\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,x,y\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U}\right\}=\sup\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\}.

Finally, to establish (
142
), one repeats the previous arguments to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to 0^{+}}\sup\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,x,y\in\partial(U\times U)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sup\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\partial U\right\}.

Combining this with the previous limit, one invokes (
141
) to deduce that (
142
) holds. ∎

Before entering into the proof of Proposition
27
, here is a final remark concerning one of its first implications.

Remark 23
.

As is readily verified, Proposition
27
can be combined with the classical result of
[
12
]
to give an alternative proof of the comparison principle for
\varphi
-infinity harmonic functions, or (
10
).

9.2.
Proof of Proposition
27

The proof of Proposition
27
proceeds by combining the ingredients developed in the previous sections with the uniqueness machinery from the theory of viscosity solutions. In particular, a very useful lemma from
[
32
]
will be used to control the Hessian.

Specifically, the following version of the maximum principle for semicontinuous functions (or the Jensen-Ishii Lemma)
33
33

33

See
[
24
, Theorem 3.2]
,
[
22
, Theorem on Sums]
, or
[
31
, Lemmas 3.23 and 3.30]
.
will be used:

Proposition 28

(Lemma 1 in
[
32
]
)
.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set, and suppose that
u\in USC(\overline{U})
and
v\in LSC(\overline{U})
satisfy

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(Du,D^{2}u))\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U,

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{*,\alpha}^{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u))\geq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U.

Let
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and suppose that
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is a convex function which is finite and smooth in a neighborhood of
\xi_{0}
. If there are points
(x_{0},y_{0})\in U
such that
\xi_{0}=x_{0}-y_{0}
and

\sup\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\psi(x-y)\,\mid\,(x,y)\in U\times U\right\}=u(x_{0})-v(y_{0})-\psi(x_{0}-y_{0}),

then there are matrices
X,Y\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{0},u(x_{0}),D\psi(\xi_{0}),X,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(D\psi(\xi_{0}),X))

\displaystyle\leq 0,

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(y_{0},v(y_{0}),D\psi(\xi_{0}),Y,\mathcal{G}_{*,\alpha}^{\varphi}(D\psi(\xi_{0}),Y))

\displaystyle\geq 0,

and

-3\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}D^{2}\psi(\xi_{0})&0\\
0&D^{2}\psi(\xi_{0})\end{array}\right)\leq\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}X&0\\
0&-Y\end{array}\right)\leq 3\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}D^{2}\psi(\xi_{0})&-D^{2}\psi(\xi_{0})\\
-D^{2}\psi(\xi_{0})&D^{2}\psi(\xi_{0})\end{array}\right).

The idea of the proof of Proposition
27
is now more-or-less straightforward. It starts by replacing
\varphi_{\zeta}
by
\psi_{\zeta}
, an appropriately chosen conical test function. Next, the perturbation argument is used, replacing
\psi_{\zeta}
by
\psi_{\zeta}^{\epsilon}
. Applying the maximum principle for semicontinuous functions to
\psi_{\zeta}^{\epsilon}
yields matrices
(X_{\epsilon},Y_{\epsilon})
with good properties, which, in particular, lead to the key constraint (
134
) in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

As in Section
6
, it is convenient to first consider the case when the unit ball
\{\varphi\leq 1\}
is
C^{1,1}
. First, here is a variable-doubled analogue of Proposition
21
:

Proposition 29
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying (
60
) and (
61
) for some constants
\zeta,\delta>0
; let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set; and fix a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Define
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
.

Fix a
\beta>0
and a
\nu\in(0,\zeta)
. For
\mu\in\{\nu,\zeta\}
, let
\psi_{\mu}
be the conical test function
\psi_{\mu}:=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\mu}
of Section
4
, and let
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{0<\epsilon<\nu}
be an admissible perturbation of
\psi_{\nu}
.

If
u\in\text{USC}(\overline{U})
and
v\in\text{LSC}(\overline{U})
are such that

(147)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(Du,D^{2}u))

\displaystyle\leq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U,

(148)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x,u,Du,D^{2}u,\mathcal{G}_{*,\alpha}^{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u))

\displaystyle\geq 0\quad\text{in}\,\,U,

and

(149)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:=\max\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\psi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad>\max\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\psi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in\partial(U\times U)\right\},

(150)

\displaystyle\{(x,y)

\displaystyle\,\mid\,u(x)-v(y)=M+\frac{\psi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\{(x,y)\in U\times U\,\,\mid\,x-y\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)),\,\,x\neq y\},

then, for any
\nu\in(0,\zeta)
, there are sequences
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
,
((x_{j},y_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq U\times U
, and
((p_{j},X_{j},Y_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that

(151)

\displaystyle u(x_{j})-v(y_{j})-\frac{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{4}}{4\beta}=\max\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U}\right\},

(152)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{j},u(x_{j}),\mu_{j},X_{j},\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}^{*,\alpha}(\mu_{j},X_{j}))\leq 0,\quad-\mathcal{H}(y_{j},v(y_{j}),\mu_{j},Y_{j},\mathcal{G}_{*,\alpha}^{\varphi}(\mu_{j},Y_{j}))\geq 0,

(153)

\displaystyle\mu_{j}=\beta^{-1}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{3}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j}),

\displaystyle-3\left(\begin{array}[]{r l}\bar{A}_{j}(x_{j}-y_{j})&0\\
0&\bar{A}_{j}(x_{j}-y_{j})\end{array}\right)\leq\left(\begin{array}[]{r l}X_{j}&0\\
0&-Y_{j}\end{array}\right)\leq 3\left(\begin{array}[]{r l}\bar{A}_{j}(x_{j}-y_{j})&-\bar{A}_{j}(x_{j}-y_{j})\\
-\bar{A}_{j}(x_{j}-y_{j})&\bar{A}_{j}(x_{j}-y_{j})\end{array}\right),

where
\bar{A}
is the matrix function

\displaystyle\bar{A}_{j}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\beta^{-1}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(\xi)^{3}D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(\xi)+3\beta^{-1}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(\xi)^{2}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(\xi)^{\otimes 2},&\text{if}\,\,\xi\neq 0,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Argue as in Proposition
21
to obtain sequences
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
((x_{j},y_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that (
151
) holds. Then apply Proposition
28
to obtain a sequence
((X_{j},Y_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that (
152
) and (
29
) hold. ∎

Finally, here is the proof of Proposition
27
.

Proof of Proposition
27
.

The proof boils down to an application of Propositions
22
and
29
. In order to apply Proposition
29
directly, though, it would be necessary to know that the maximum of
u(x)-v(y)-(4\beta)^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}
occurs away from the diagonal. Of course, this may not be true in general. Thus, the proof begins with the (ultimately trivial) case when the maximum is on the diagonal before proceeding to the more interesting situation.

Case: maximum on the diagonal.

Suppose that there is a point
x_{0}\in U
such that

u(x_{0})-v(x_{0})=\sup\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in U\times U\right\}.

This implies that

\displaystyle u(x_{0})-v(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\max\left\{u(x)-v(x_{0})-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-x_{0})^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,x\in U\right\},

\displaystyle u(x_{0})-v(y)

\displaystyle=\max\left\{u(x_{0})-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{0}-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,y\in U\right\}.

At the same time, since
\varphi_{\zeta}
sastisifes (
17
),

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(\xi)^{4}}{4\beta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\xi\|^{4}}{4\beta\delta}\quad\text{with equality if}\quad\xi=0,

Therefore,

\displaystyle u(x_{0})-v(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{u(x)-v(x_{0})-\frac{\|x-x_{0}\|^{4}}{4\beta\delta}\,\mid\,x\in U\right\},

\displaystyle u(x_{0})-v(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{u(x_{0})-v(y)-\frac{\|x_{0}-y\|^{4}}{4\beta\delta}\,\mid\,y\in U\right\},

and, thus, since
\|\cdot\|^{4}
is smooth and vanishing to second order at zero,

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{0},u(x_{0}),0,0,0)\leq 0,\quad-\mathcal{H}(y_{0},u(y_{0}),0,0,0)\geq 0.

This completes the proof with
(x_{*},y_{*})=(x_{0},y_{0})
,
(X_{*},Y_{*})=(0,0)
, and
q^{u}_{**},q^{v}_{**}
any arbitrary points in
\{\varphi=1\}
, their values being completely irrelevant in this case.

Case: all maxima off the diagonal.

To complete the proof, it suffices to proceed under the assumption that there is an
r>0
such that

(162)

\left\{(x,y)\,\mid\,u(x)-v(y)=M+\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\right\}\subseteq\{(x,y)\in U\times U\,\mid\,\|x-y\|>r\},

where
M
is the maximum

M=\max\left\{u(x)-v(y)-\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,(x,y)\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U}\right\}.

Due to (
128
) and (
162
), there are points
(x_{0},y_{0})\in U\times U
such that

u(x_{0})-v(y_{0})=M+\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{0}-y_{0})^{4}}{4\beta},\quad\|x_{0}-y_{0}\|>r.

Let
\xi_{0}=x_{0}-y_{0}
. Since
\|\xi_{0}\|>r
, the vector
p=D\varphi_{\zeta}(\xi_{0})
is well-defined, as is the direction
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
.

For
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
, let
\psi_{\nu}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p),\nu}
be the conical test function associated with the triple
(e,\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p),\nu)
. By Proposition
12
, the following statements all hold:

\displaystyle u(x_{0})-v(y_{0})=M+\frac{\psi_{\nu}(x_{0}-y_{0})^{4}}{4\beta},

\displaystyle u(x)-v(y)\leq M+\frac{\psi_{\nu}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\quad\text{for each}\quad(x,y)\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U},

(163)

\displaystyle\left\{(x,y)\,\mid\,u(x)-v(y)=M+\frac{\psi_{\nu}(x-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\right\}\subseteq\{(x,y)\in U\times U\,\mid\,x-y\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}_{\nu}(p)),\,\,x\neq y\}.

In particular, the hypotheses of Proposition
29
are satisfied.

Fix a
\nu\in(0,\zeta)
; for instance,
\nu=2^{-1}\zeta
, although the exact value is immaterial to the proof. By Proposition
29
, there are sequences

(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty),\quad((x_{j},y_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq U\times U,\quad((\mu_{j},X_{j},Y_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}\times\mathcal{S}_{d}

such that (
151
)-(
29
) all hold with this value of
\nu
.

In what follows, it will be useful to define
p_{j}=D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})
and
A_{j}=\bar{A}_{j}(x_{j}-y_{j})
, where
\bar{A}_{j}
is the matrix function defined in (
29
). Notice that if
(H_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is the sequence
H_{j}=D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})
, then

A_{j}=\beta^{-1}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{3}H_{j}+3\beta^{-1}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{2}p_{j}\otimes p_{j}.

By compactness (up to passing to a subsequence), there are points
