(x_{*},y_{*})\in\overline{U}\times\overline{U}
such that
(x_{j},y_{j})\to(x_{*},y_{*})
. Note that, by upper semicontinuity and Proposition
12
,

(164)

u(x_{*})-v(y_{*})=M+\frac{\psi_{\nu}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{4}}{4\beta}=M+\frac{\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{4}}{4\beta}

and, thus,
\|x_{*}-y_{*}\|>r
by assumption (
162
). Since
x_{*}\neq y_{*}
, (
164
) implies
\psi_{\nu}(x_{*}-y_{*})=\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})<+\infty
and, thus,
p_{j}\to D\psi_{\nu}(x_{*}-y_{*})=D\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})=p
as
j\to+\infty
by (
163
) and Proposition
12
.
Additionally, by Proposition
22
,

(165)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})\limsup_{j\to\infty}\|H_{j}\|=\limsup_{j\to\infty}\psi_{\nu}(x_{j}-y_{j})\|H_{j}\|\leq\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi)\zeta^{-1}.

In particular, up to passing to yet another subsequence, there is an
H_{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that
H_{j}\to H_{*}
as
j\to+\infty
. Note that this implies
A_{j}\to A_{*}
, where

(166)

A_{*}=\beta^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{3}H_{*}+3\beta^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{2}p\otimes p.

Thus, by (
165
), Proposition
20
, and the assumption
\zeta<1
, upon writing
T_{*}=\beta^{-1}\varphi_{\zeta}(x_{*}-y_{*})^{2}
, one finds

(167)

\displaystyle 0\leq A_{*}\leq\left(\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi)\zeta^{-1}T_{*}+3T_{*}\delta(\varphi)^{-2}\right)\text{Id}\leq\left(\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi)+3\delta(\varphi)^{-2}\right)\zeta^{-1}T_{*}\text{Id}.

Next, fix the
y
variable: observe that, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle u(x_{j})-v(y_{j})-\frac{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{4}}{4\beta}=\sup\left\{u(x)-v(y_{j})-\frac{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x-y_{j})^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\right\},

hence there is a sequence
(q^{u}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
such that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(x_{j},u(x_{j}),\mu_{j},A_{j},Q_{A_{j}}(q^{u}_{j}-\zeta\|\mu_{j}\|^{-1}\mu_{j}))\leq 0,\quad q_{j}^{u}\in\partial\varphi^{*}_{\zeta}(\mu_{j})=\partial\varphi^{*}_{\zeta}(p_{j}).

Since
\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
is compact, there is no loss of generality assuming there is a
q^{u}_{**}\in\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
such that
q^{u}_{j}\to q^{u}_{**}
as
j\to+\infty
. By Proposition
22
, it follows that there is a face
F_{*}^{u}\subseteq\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)
such that

(168)

q_{**}^{u}\in F_{*}^{u}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{*}q=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}^{u}.

Similarly, fix the
x
variable: observe that, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle u(x_{j})-v(y_{j})-\frac{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{4}}{4\beta}=\sup\left\{u(x_{j})-v(y)-\frac{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j}-y)^{4}}{4\beta}\,\mid\,y\in\overline{U}\right\},

hence there is a sequence
(q^{v}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{\varphi_{\zeta}=1\}
such that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{H}(y_{j},v(y_{j}),p_{j},-A_{j},Q_{-A_{j}}(q_{j}^{v}-\zeta\|\mu_{j}\|^{-1}\mu_{j}))\geq 0,\quad q_{j}^{v}\in\partial\varphi^{*}_{\zeta}(p_{j}).

Once again, after passing to a subsequence, there is a limit
q_{**}^{v}=\lim_{j\to\infty}q_{j}^{v}
. Further, by Proposition
22
(applied to supersolutions; cf. Lemma
3
), there is a face
F_{*}^{v}\subseteq\partial\varphi^{*}_{\zeta}(p)
such that

(169)

q_{**}^{v}\in F_{*}^{v}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{*}q=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in F_{*}^{v}.

Finally, notice that (
29
) implies that
-3A_{j}\leq X_{j}\leq Y_{j}\leq 3A_{j}
. Thus, the sequence
((X_{j},Y_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is pre-compact, and there is no loss of generality in assuming that there are matrices
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
such that
(X_{j},Y_{j})\to(X_{*},Y_{*})
as
j\to+\infty
. By (
29
) and (
167
), the inequality (
27
) holds with
C(\varphi)=3(\tilde{\Gamma}(\varphi)+3\delta(\varphi)^{-2})
. To prove (
134
), first, observe that, on the one hand, since
q_{**}^{u},q_{**}^{v}\in\partial\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)
, the variational formula (
20
) implies that

(p\otimes p)(q_{**}^{u}-q_{**}^{v})=\left[\langle p,q_{**}^{u}\rangle-\langle p,q_{**}^{v}\rangle\right]p=[\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)-\varphi_{\zeta}^{*}(p)]p=0.

In view of (
166
), (
168
), and (
169
), this proves
A_{*}(q_{**}^{u}-q_{**}^{v})=0
. On the other hand, in the limit
j\to+\infty
, (
29
) implies
-3A_{*}\leq X_{*}\leq Y_{*}\leq 3A_{*}
. Accordingly, it is an exercise in linear algebra to convince oneself that also
34
34

34

Cf. the proof of
[
42
, Lemma 1]
.

X_{*}(q_{**}^{u}-q_{**}^{v})=Y_{*}(q_{**}^{u}-q_{**}^{v})=0.

∎

10.
Applications

This section treats the applications described in the introduction, namely, Theorem
1
on the Dirichlet problem (
2
), Theorem
3
on the infinity eigenvalue problem (
11
), and Theorem
4
on tug-of-war games.

10.1.
Existence via Perron’s Method

This section establishes an existence result for the Dirichlet problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\lambda u-c(x)G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u)\leq f(x)&\text{in}\,\,U,\\
\lambda u-c(x)G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u)\geq f(x)&\text{in}\,\,U,\\
u=g&\text{on}\,\,\partial U.\end{array}\right.

The argument exploits the fact that
\varphi
is convex and satisfies
\varphi^{*}(D\varphi)\geq 1
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, hence it can be used to build explicit barriers when
g
is regular enough.

Proposition 30
.

For any Finsler norm
\varphi
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any bounded open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if
\sqrt{c}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
is uniformly Lipschitz continuous, then there is a viscosity solution
u\in C(\overline{U})
of (
10.1
) for any
g\in C(\partial U)
.

The proof proceeds first by constructing solutions when the boundary datum
g
is uniformly Hölder continuous on
\partial U
, then establishes the general case by approximation. Toward that end, it will be useful to know that there are explicit conical barriers.

Lemma 13
.

For any Finsler norm
\varphi
, any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and any
R>0
, there are constants
C_{+,\alpha}(R),C_{-,\alpha}(R)>0
such that the functions
u_{+,\alpha},u_{-,\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
given by

u_{+,\alpha}(x)=\varphi(x)^{\alpha},\quad u_{-,\alpha}(x)=-\varphi(-x)^{\alpha},

satisfy the differential inequalities

\displaystyle-G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du_{+,\alpha},D^{2}u_{+,\alpha})

\displaystyle\geq C_{+,\alpha}(R)\quad\text{in}\,\,B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\},

\displaystyle-G^{*}_{\varphi}(Du_{-,\alpha},D^{2}u_{-,\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq-C_{-,\alpha}(R)\quad\text{in}\,\,B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\}.

Proof.

In a manner similar to Lemma
3
above, the arguments for
u_{+,\alpha}
and
u_{-,\alpha}
are symmetrical. Therefore, only those for
u_{+,\alpha}
will be presented here.

In view of the fact that
-G_{*}^{\varphi}(D\varphi,D^{2}\varphi)\geq 0
holds by Proposition
1
, the following inequalities also hold

\displaystyle-G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du_{+,\alpha},D^{2}u_{+,\alpha})

\displaystyle\geq-\alpha(\alpha-1)\varphi(x)^{\alpha-2}\varphi^{*}(D\varphi)^{2}.

At the same time, since
\varphi^{*}(D\varphi)\geq 1
in the viscosity sense, this implies

\displaystyle-G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du_{+,\alpha},D^{2}u_{+,\alpha})

\displaystyle\geq\alpha(1-\alpha)\varphi(x)^{\alpha-2}.

Finally, since
\alpha<2
,

\displaystyle C_{+,\alpha}(R):=\min\left\{\alpha(1-\alpha)\varphi(x)^{\alpha-2}\,\mid\,x\in B_{R}(0)\setminus\{0\}\right\}>0.

∎

With the barriers constructed in the previous lemma, the proof of existence for (
10.1
) is now routine. Here is the proof:

Proof of Proposition
30
.

As advertised, the proof begins with the case when
g
is more reuglar, then extends to the general case by approximation and monotonicity.

Step one: uniformly Hölder continuous boundary data.

First, assume that there is an
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
g
is uniformly Hölder continuous on
\partial U
that is, there is a
K>0
such that

|g(x)-g(y)|\leq K\|x-y\|^{\alpha}\quad\text{for each}\quad x,y\in\partial U.

Notice that, by equivalence of norns, this implies there are constants
K_{+}
and
K_{-}
such that, for any
x,y\in\partial U
,

|g(x)-g(y)|\leq\max\{K_{+}\varphi(x-y)^{\alpha},K_{-}\varphi(y-x)^{\alpha}\}.

In particular, if
x_{0}\in\partial U
is fixed, then, for any
x\in\partial U
,

g(x_{0})-K_{-}\varphi(x_{0}-x)^{\alpha}\leq g(x)\leq g(x_{0})+K_{+}\varphi(x-x_{0})^{\alpha}=:u^{-}_{x_{0}}(x).

Let
C>0
be a large constant to be determined. Define functions
u^{+}_{x_{0}}
and
u^{-}_{x_{0}}
by

\displaystyle u^{\pm}_{x_{0}}(y)=g(x_{0})\pm CK_{\pm}\varphi(\pm[x-x_{0}])^{\alpha}.

Due to Lemma
13
, a routine argument shows that, at least if
C>1
is large enough,

\displaystyle\lambda u^{+}_{x_{0}}-G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du^{+}_{x_{0}},D^{2}u^{+}_{x_{0}})

\displaystyle\geq f(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,U,

\displaystyle\lambda u^{-}_{x_{0}}-G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du^{-}_{x_{0}},D^{2}u^{-}_{x_{0}})

\displaystyle\leq f(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,U.

Note that
u^{+}_{x_{0}}\geq g\geq u^{-}_{x_{0}}
also holds. Fix such a
C
henceforth.

It only remains to apply Perron’s Method. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of all subsolutions, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\left\{v\in USC(\overline{U})\,\mid\,\lambda v-G_{\varphi}^{*}(Dv,D^{2}v)\leq f(x)\,\,\text{in}\,\,U,\,\,v\leq g\,\,\text{on}\,\,\partial U\right\}.

We showed that
\mathcal{S}
is nonempty. Further, by the comparison principle (Theorem
8
), for any
v\in\mathcal{S}
and any
x_{0}\in\partial U
,

\displaystyle v\leq u_{x_{0}}^{+}\quad\text{pointwise in}\,\,\overline{U}.

This shows
\mathcal{S}
is pointwise bounded. In particular, let
u:\overline{U}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function

u(x)=\max\left\{v(x)\,\mid\,v\in\mathcal{S}\right\}.

A standard computation shows that the upper semicontinuous envelope of
u
belongs to
\mathcal{S}
, from which it follows that
u\in USC(\overline{U})
and
u\in\mathcal{S}
. Similarly, a standard construction shows that
u
is a supersolution in
U
, that is,

\lambda u-c(x)G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u)\geq f(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,U.

See
[
24
, Section 4]
for detailed proofs of both of these claims.

At the same time, for any
x_{0}\in\partial U
,
u_{x_{0}}^{-}\leq u\leq u_{x_{0}}^{+}
holds pointwise in
\overline{U}
, and, therefore,
u=g
on
\partial U
.

Step two: the general case.

It remains to prove existence even when
g
need not be uniformly Hölder continuous. To that end, observe that there is a sequence of functions
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with constants
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
such that, for any
n
,

g\leq g_{n+1}\leq g_{n}\quad\text{pointwise in}\,\,\partial U,

and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|g_{n}-g\|_{C(\partial U)}=0,

\displaystyle|g_{n}(x)-g_{n}(y)|\leq K_{n}\|x-y\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{for each}\quad x,y\in\partial U.

Further, it is possible to fix a
h\in C(\partial U)
such that
h\leq g
pointwise on
\partial U
and
|h(x)-h(y)|\leq K_{h}\|x-y\|^{\frac{1}{2}}
for any
x,y\in\partial U
.

For any
n
, let
u_{n}
denote the solution of (
10.1
) with
u_{n}=g_{n}
on
\partial U
. Similarly, let
u_{h}
be the solution with
u_{h}=h
on
\partial U
. By the comparison principle (Theorem
8
),
u_{h}\leq u_{n+1}\leq u_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, if
u:\overline{U}\to\mathbb{R}
is the function given by
u(x)=\min\{u_{n}(x)\,\mid\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, then Dini’s Theorem implies that
u\in C(\overline{U})
, and the choice of
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
implies that
u=g
on
\partial U
. Finally, by stability of viscosity solutions,
u
is the viscosity solution of (
10.1
). ∎

Finally, the barriers of Lemma
13
also imply that the solution
u
of (
10.1
) is locally Hölder continuous.

Proposition 31
.

Let
\varphi
,
U
, and
c
be as in Proposition
30
. Given any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and any
V\subset\subset U
, there is a
C(V,\alpha)>0
such that if
u
is the solution of (
10.1
), then

[u]_{\alpha,V}\leq C(V,\alpha)\max\left\{|g(x)|\,\mid\,x\in\partial U\right\},

where
[u]_{\alpha,V}
is the Hölder seminorm

[u]_{\alpha,V}=\inf\left\{K>0\,\mid\,|u(x)-u(y)|\leq K\|x-y\|^{\alpha}\,\,\text{for each}\,\,x,y\in V\right\}.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
13
by a well-known argument (cf.
[
35
, Corollary 3.8]
). ∎

The above propositions combine with Theorem
8
to prove Theorem
1
.

10.2.
Applications to Tug-of-War Games

This section describes applications of the comparison results above to the asymptotics of tug-of-war games involving Finsler norms. In this setting, the Finsler infinity Laplacian is replaced by the finite-difference operator
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}
defined by
35
35

35

Note that the inversion in the minimum is not a typo. As in the definition of
CCA_{\varphi}
and
CCB_{\varphi}
,
\varphi
is replaced by the “inverted” norm
x\mapsto\varphi(-x)
when passing from the maximum to the minimum.

(173)

\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}u(x)=\frac{1}{2}\sup\left\{u(y)\,\mid\,\varphi(y-x)<\epsilon\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\inf\left\{u(y)\,\mid\,\varphi(x-y)<\epsilon\right\}.

In order to prove results that were previously out of reach, instead of the standard tug-of-war game, which can be treated entirely using the cone comparison results from
[
3
]
and
[
2
]
, this section treats a version of the game with discount rate and running cost, the treatment of which requires Theorem
8
; see Remark
24
for further explanation on this point.

Analytically, this section considers the
\epsilon
-scale finite-difference version of the problem considered in the previous subsection:

(174)

\displaystyle u^{\epsilon}-\epsilon^{-2}c(x)(u^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}u^{\epsilon})=f(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,U_{\epsilon},\quad u^{\epsilon}=G\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{U}\setminus U_{\epsilon},

where
G\in C(\overline{U})
and
U_{\epsilon}
is the subdomain

U_{\epsilon}=\{x\in U\,\mid\,\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,\varphi(y-x)<\epsilon\,\,\text{or}\,\,\varphi(x-y)<\epsilon\}\subseteq U\}.

The aim is to prove Theorem
4
, which asserts that this problem has solutions for
\epsilon>0
that converge to the solution of the corresponding PDE in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Probabilistically, the above equation characterizes the value function of a two-player stochastic game. Specifically, fix a discount rate
\mu>0
, a running cost
f\in C(\overline{U})
, and a terminal payout
G\in C(\overline{U})
. The game consists of two players, Player I and Player II. Their counter begins at some initial point
x_{0}\in U_{1}
. If at time
n
the current position of the counter is
x_{n}\in U_{1}
, a random (unbiased) coin is flipped. If the outcome is heads, Player I chooses a new position
x_{n+1}
such that
\varphi(x_{n+1}-x_{n})<1
; if it is tails, Player II chooses a position
x_{n+1}
such that
\varphi(x_{n}-x_{n+1})<1
.
36
36

36

Hence Player I moves the counter “forward,” while Player II moves it “backwards.”
If
x_{n+1}\notin U_{1}
, the game ends and the payout is

(1+\mu)\sum_{m=0}^{n}(1+\mu)^{-m}f(x_{m})+G(x_{n+1}).

Otherwise, if
x_{n+1}\in U_{1}
, the game continues as before. Player I seeks to maximize the expected payout, while Player II seeks to minimize it.

It turns out that the value functions of the two players, that is, the expected payout suitably optimized over the choice of strategies, coincide. This function
u
is characterized as the solution of the difference equation

(175)

\mu u+u-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}u=f(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,U_{\epsilon},\quad u=G\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{U}\setminus U_{\epsilon}.

See the original paper
[
47
]
or either of the introductory texts
[
39
,
46
]
for derivations to this effect. Under the diffusive rescaling
u^{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon^{2}u(\epsilon^{-1}x)
, this equation becomes (
174
), hence the
\epsilon
-scale equation captures the behavior of the game in a large spatial domain and over long time scales.
37
37

37

The diffusivity
c
appears in a continuous-time version of the game, where jumps occur at random Poisson times occurring with variable rate equal to
c(x_{t})
.

While the probabilistic sketch above can be made rigorous to establish the existence of solutions of (
175
), analytical arguments very similar to those presented for the continuum limit (
10.1
) also apply. The next two lemmas are the main ingredients necessary to see this:

Lemma 14
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and fix a bounded open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given any
\epsilon,\mu>0
, if
u,v:\overline{U}\to\mathbb{R}
are bounded functions such that

\mu u+c(x)(u-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}u)\leq\mu v+c(x)(v-\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}v)\quad\text{in}\,\,U_{\epsilon},

then

\sup\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\right\}=\sup\left\{u(x)-v(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{U}\setminus U_{\epsilon}\right\}.

Proof.

Let
B(\overline{U})
denote the space of all bounded functions in
\overline{U}
and let
T:B(\overline{U})\to B(\overline{U})
denote the map

Tu(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\frac{c(x)}{\mu+c(x)}\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}u(x),&\text{if}\,\,x\in U_{\epsilon},\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Notice that
T
is monotone nondecreasing in the sense that if
u_{1}\leq u_{2}
pointwise in
\overline{U}
, then
Tu_{1}\leq Tu_{2}
pointwise in
\overline{U}
. Furthermore, since
\mu>0
and
c
is bounded above in
\overline{U}
,
T
is a strict contraction with respect to the supremum norm. It follows that
u-Tu\leq v-Tv
holds pointwise in
\overline{U}
if and only if
u\leq v
pointwise in
\overline{U}
. ∎

Lemma 15
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and fix a bounded open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
v\notin U
, then, for any
\epsilon>0
, the function
u(x)=\varphi(x-v)
satisfies

u-\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}u=0\quad\text{in}\,\,U_{\epsilon}.

Proof.

This is one place where the need for asymmetry in the definitinon of
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}
becomes apparent. Up to translation, one can assume
v=0
. Observe then that, for any
x\in U_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\max\left\{\varphi(y)\,\mid\,\varphi(y-x)\leq\epsilon\right\}

\displaystyle=\varphi(x)+\epsilon,

\displaystyle\min\left\{\varphi(y)\,\mid\,\varphi(x-y)\leq\epsilon\right\}

\displaystyle=\varphi(x)-\epsilon.

This proves
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}u(x)=u(x)
. ∎

The previous two lemmas imply existence and uniqueness of the solution of (
174
) by analogy with what was done in the previous subsection. Where existence is concerned, one can once again argue by Perron’s Method, as in the next lemma.

Lemma 16
.

For any bounded open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, any bounded functions
c:U\to[0,+\infty)
,
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
, and
G:\overline{U}\to\mathbb{R}
, and any
\epsilon>0
, there is a bounded function
u:\overline{U}\to\mathbb{R}
such that (
175
) holds.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
14
, let
B(\overline{U})
denote the space of all bounded functions in
\overline{U}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the space of all subsolutions, meaning

\mathcal{S}=\left\{u\in B(\overline{U})\,\mid\,\mu u+\epsilon^{-2}c(x)(u-\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}u)\leq F(x)\,\,\text{in}\,\,U_{\epsilon},\,\,u\leq G(x)\,\,\text{in}\,\,\overline{U}\setminus U_{\epsilon}\right\}.

Note that the constant function
u(x)=\min\{\mu^{-1}\inf F,\inf G\}
is in
\mathcal{S}
, hence
\mathcal{S}
is nonempty. Further, the function
v(x)=\max\{\mu^{-1}\sup F,\sup G\}
is a supersolution, which, in view of Lemma
14
, implies that any element of
\mathcal{S}
lies below it.

Defining
u^{\epsilon}(x)=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}}u(x)
, one readily deduces that
u^{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
. Furthermore, by maximality,
u^{\epsilon}
is also a supersolution. (If
u^{\epsilon}
fails to be a supersolution at some point
x\in\overline{U}
, one directly shows that the function
u^{\epsilon}+\zeta 1_{\{x\}}
is a subsolution provided
\zeta>0
is small enough, which would contradict the maximality of
u^{\epsilon}
.) ∎

Finally, it remains to show that the solution
u^{\epsilon}
converges to the solution of the PDE (
10.1
) in the limit
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. Here the classical technique of Barles and Souganidis
[
13
]
is applicable. Toward that end, the following lemma is needed, which shows, in the terminology used in that reference, that the approximation (
173
) is a consistent scheme for the PDE. This computation is by now quite well-known.
38
38

38

Cf.
[
19
, Lemma 5.10]
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given any
C^{2}
function
f
defined in some open set
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, any
x_{0}\in V
, and any family
(x_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}\subseteq V
such that
x_{\epsilon}\to x_{0}
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle-G_{\varphi}^{*}(Df(x_{0}),D^{2}f(x_{0}))\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{-2}\left[f(x_{\epsilon})-\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}f(x_{\epsilon})\right],

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{-2}\left[f(x_{\epsilon})-\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}f(x_{\epsilon})\right]\leq-G_{*}^{\varphi}(Df(x_{0}),D^{2}f(x_{0})).

For the sake of completeness, and to provide further evidence that
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}
is defined correctly in (
173
), a proof of this fundamental lemma is provided in Appendix
A
.

With the above lemmas in hand, the proof of convergence of
u^{\epsilon}
to the solution of (
10.1
) follows by the Barles-Souganidis approach, as sketched in the next proof:

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Let
u_{*}
and
u^{*}
be the upper and lower half-relaxed limits of
(u^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
as defined in
[
14
, Section 6]
or
[
19
, Proof of Theorem 3.1]
. Since the barrier arguments above are
\epsilon
-independent and
G
is an extension of
g
, it follows that
u_{*}=u^{*}=G=g
on
\partial U
. Further, using Lemma
17
, one readily deduces that

\lambda u^{*}-G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u)\leq f(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,U,\quad\lambda u_{*}-G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u)\geq f(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,U.

Therefore, by Theorem
8
,
u^{*}\leq u_{*}
in
\overline{U}
. Yet the definitions imply
u^{*}\geq u_{*}
. Thus, equality holds
u^{*}\equiv u_{*}
, and
u^{\epsilon}\to u
uniformly in
\overline{U}
.∎

Remark 24
.

It is also true that the solution
u^{\epsilon}
of the simpler problem

u^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}u^{\epsilon}=0\quad\text{in}\,\,U_{\epsilon},\quad u^{\epsilon}=G\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{U}\setminus U_{\epsilon},

converges to the
\varphi
-infinity harmonic function in
U
with boundary value
G
. However, this follows already from the cone comparison results of
[
3
]
and
[
2
]
, the reason being that one can use Lemmas
14
and
15
to prove that the upper (resp. lower) half-relaxed limit of
(u^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
is in
CCA_{\varphi}(U)
(resp.
CCB_{\varphi}(U)
) and then invoke the comparison theorems in those papers to conclude that the upper and lower half-relaxed limits coincide.
39
39

39

The limiting behavior is less straightforward when there is a right-hand side, see
[
4
]
.

The focus here is on (
174
) since it seems to be out of reach of previous work. Specifically, unlike the Finsler infinity Laplace equation, the solutions of which are characterized by comparison with cones, the PDE (
10.1
) considered here does not seem to possess a comparison-with-cones-type characterization.

Remark 25
.

The problem (
173
) is just one of many tug-of-war games whose value functions can be shown to converge using the comparison results of this paper. For another simple example, consider tug-of-war with noise,
40
40

40

The game is the same, except for a third “drunk” player who chooses the next position
x_{n+1}
uniformly at random from the ball
B_{1}(x_{n})
; see
[
39
,
46
]
.
in which the value function is the solution of the following equation

u^{\epsilon}+\epsilon^{-2}(u^{\epsilon}-p\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\varphi}u^{\epsilon}-(1-p)\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}u^{\epsilon})=f(x).

Above
p\in[0,1]
and
\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
is the following averaging operator:

(\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}u)(x)=\fint_{B_{\epsilon}(x)}u(y)\,dy.

Since
\text{Id}-\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
approximates
-\Delta
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, limits of the above equation are determined by the following differential inequalities:

\lambda u-p\Delta u-(1-p)G_{\varphi}^{*}(Du,D^{2}u)\leq f(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda u-p\Delta u-(1-p)G_{*}^{\varphi}(Du,D^{2}u)\geq f(x).

Part IV
Appendices

Appendix A
Technical Lemmata

A.1.
Integrals over Cones

Recall that the perturbative argument in the proof of the cone comparison principle involved a property of integration over cones. The lemma in question is proved here.

The reader may wish to compare this result to its better-known analogue: if
\mu
is a probability measure on a compact convex set
C
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\int_{C}x\,\mu(dx)
equals an extreme point of
C
, then
\mu
is a Dirac mass. The lemma that follows more-or-less shows (albeit in the setting of cones) that this observation can be generalized to the case where “extreme point” is replaced by “face.”

Proof of Lemma
12
.

Let
\text{sppt}(\mu)
denote the support of
\mu
, that is,
v\in\text{sppt}(\mu)
if and only if

\mu(B_{\epsilon}(v))>0\quad\text{for each}\quad\epsilon>0.

Recall that
\mu(\mathcal{N}\setminus\text{sppt}(\mu))=0
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{0}
denote the convex cone generated by
\text{sppt}(\mu)
, that is,

\mathcal{N}_{0}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}v_{i}\,\mid\,N\in\mathbb{N},\,\,v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\in\text{sppt}(\mu),\,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N}\geq 0\right\}.

The closure
\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
is also a convex cone, and
\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
since
\mu
is a measure on
\mathcal{N}
. To prove that
\mu(\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{N}^{\prime})=0
, it suffices to show that
\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Toward that end, since
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
is a face of
\mathcal{N}
, it enough to show that the mean
m_{\mu}:=\int_{\mathcal{N}}v\,\mu(dv)
is an element of the relative interior of
\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
.

It is clear that
m_{\mu}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
since
\mu
is a positive measure (e.g., by simple approximation). Let us argue by contradiction: suppose that
m_{\mu}\in\text{bdry}(\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}})
. It follows that there is a vector
w\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

\displaystyle\langle w,m_{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\langle w,v\rangle\,\mid\,v\in\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\right\}.

Note that if
v\in\text{rint}(\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}})
, then
\langle w,v\rangle<\langle w,m_{\mu}\rangle
necessarily holds. At the same time, since
\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
is a cone, the maximum can only be zero:
\langle w,m_{\mu}\rangle=0
.

Fix a
v_{0}\in\text{rint}(\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}})
. Since
\langle w,v_{0}\rangle<0
, the definition of
\mathcal{N}_{0}
implies there is a
\tilde{v}\in\text{sppt}(\mu)
such that
\langle w,\tilde{v}\rangle<0
. In particular, there is an
\epsilon>0
such that

\langle w,v\rangle<0\quad\text{for each}\quad v\in B_{\epsilon}(\tilde{v}).

Thus,
\mu(\{\langle w,\cdot\rangle<0\})\geq\mu(B_{\epsilon}(\tilde{v}))>0
.
On the other hand, since
\langle w,v\rangle\leq 0
for all
v\in\overline{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
, this leads to the observation that

\displaystyle 0=\langle w,m_{\mu}\rangle=\int_{\text{sppt}(\mu)}\langle w,v\rangle\,\mu(dv)=\int_{\{\langle w,v\rangle<0\}}\langle w,v\rangle\,\mu(dv)<0,

a patent contradiction. ∎

A.2.
Hessian Bounds for Conical Test Functions

This appendix proves the Hessian bound that was used in the analysis of conical test functions in Section
6
.

Lemma 18
.

Let
\varphi
satisfying Assumption
1
. Fix a
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, and let
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
. Let
\psi_{\nu}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}
be the associated conical test function, and let
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{0<\nu<\epsilon}
be an admissible perturbation.

If there are sequences
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,\nu)
and
(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a point
q_{*}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
such that
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})=1
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and

\lim_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{j\to\infty}q_{j}=q_{*},

then

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\|D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{*})\|\leq\Gamma(\delta)\nu^{-1},

where
\Gamma(\delta)
is the constant from Proposition
19
.

Proof.

This is a relatively routine application of the results from Section
5
, most notably Proposition
19
. To begin, fix an arbitrary
\delta_{*}\in(0,\delta)
.

First, note that since
q_{*}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\subseteq\{\varphi\leq 1\}
, the nondegeneracy condition (
61
) implies that
\|q_{*}\|\leq\delta^{-1}
. Since
\delta_{*}<\delta
, one can fix an
R>0
such that

\overline{B}_{R}(q_{*})\subseteq\overline{B}_{\delta_{*}^{-1}}(0).

Next, note that, at the limit point
q_{*}
, one has that
D\psi_{\nu}(q_{*})=D\varphi(q_{*})
by Proposition
12
and, thus, by the nondegeneracy assumption (
61
) and Proposition
20
,

\|D\psi_{\nu}(q_{*})\|=\|D\varphi(q_{*})\|\leq\delta^{-1}.

Since
D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}\to D\psi_{\nu}
locally uniformly as
j\to+\infty
, up to making
R
smaller if necessary, one can fix a
J\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for any
j\geq J
,

\max\left\{\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q)\|\,\mid\,q\in\overline{B}_{R}(q_{*})\right\}\leq\delta_{*}^{-1}.

Thus, the functions
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy a gradient bound, as required in Proposition
19
.

It remains to check that the level set
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}=1\}
satisfies a curvature bound near
q_{*}
. Toward that end, it will be convenient to fix a
\rho\in(0,1)
. First, recall that the proof of Lemma
11
establishes that

0\leq\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})D^{2}\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})+D\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})\otimes D\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\text{Id}.

Combining this with the explicit formula (
102
), one deduces that, for every
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that
\text{dist}(q^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\rho\nu-\epsilon_{j}
,

\|D^{2}g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q^{\prime})\|\leq(1-\rho)^{-1}\nu^{-1}\max\left\{1,\frac{1}{1-\rho}\right\}=(1-\rho)^{-2}\nu^{-1}.

At the same time, recall that the derivative of the outward normal vector
N_{j}
to the graph of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}
is given, at points
q=q^{\prime}+g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q^{\prime})e
, by the formula

DN_{j}(q)=\left(\text{Id}-\frac{Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}}{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q^{\prime})\|^{2}}\right)D^{2}g_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q^{\prime})\left(\text{Id}-\frac{Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}}{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q^{\prime})\|^{2}}\right).

Thus, up to making
R
smaller and
J
larger above, there is no loss of generality assuming that

\max\left\{\|N_{j}(q)\|\,\mid\,q\in\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}=1\}\cap\overline{B}_{R}(q_{*})\right\}\leq(1-\rho)^{-2}\nu^{-1}.

This proves that
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy a uniform curvature estimate close to
q
, verifying the remaining hypothesis of Proposition
19
.

At last, apply Proposition
19
to conclude that, for any
j\geq J
,

\max\left\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q)\|D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q)\|\,\mid\,q\in\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}=1\}\cap\overline{B}_{R}(q_{*})\right\}\leq\Gamma(\delta_{*})(1-\rho)^{-2}\nu^{-1}.

In particular, since
q_{j}\to q_{*}
as
j\to+\infty
and
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})=1
for all
j
, this implies

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\|Y_{j}\|=\limsup_{j\to\infty}\|D^{2}\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j})\|\leq\Gamma(\delta_{*})(1-\rho)^{-2}\nu^{-1}.

Finally, since
\Gamma
is a continuous function, the desired estimate (
93
) follows upon sending
\rho\to 0
and
\delta_{*}\to\delta
. ∎

A.3.
Finite-Difference Approximation

Proof of Lemma
17
.

Only the details for the bound involving
G_{\varphi}^{*}
will be presented since the bound for
G_{*}^{\varphi}
follows via a transformation as in Lemma
3
.

Recall that
f
is a smooth function defined in some neighborhood of a point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
(x_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
are points such that
x_{\epsilon}\to x_{0}
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, the goal being to establish that

-G_{\varphi}^{*}(Df(x_{0}),D^{2}f(x_{0}))\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{-2}\left[f(x_{\epsilon})-(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}f)(x_{\epsilon})\right].

Given
\epsilon>0
, define
y^{+}_{\epsilon}
and
y_{\epsilon}^{-}
such that
\varphi(y^{+}_{\epsilon}-x)\leq\epsilon
,
\varphi(x_{\epsilon}-y^{-}_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon
, and

\displaystyle f(y^{+}_{\epsilon})=\max\left\{f(y)\,\mid\,\varphi(y-x_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon\right\},\quad f(y^{-}_{\epsilon})=\min\left\{f(y)\,\mid\,\varphi(x_{\epsilon}-y)\leq\epsilon\right\}.

By definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}
,

(176)

f(x_{\epsilon})-(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}f)(x_{\epsilon})=\frac{1}{2}(f(x_{\epsilon})-f(y^{+}_{\epsilon}))+\frac{1}{2}(f(x_{\epsilon})-f(y^{-}_{\epsilon})).

At this stage, it is convenient to rescale: consider the function
F_{\epsilon}
given by

F_{\epsilon}(\xi)=\epsilon^{-1}(f(x_{\epsilon}+\epsilon\xi)-f(x_{\epsilon})).

Notice that
F_{\epsilon}
converges locally uniformly to the linear function
\xi\mapsto\langle Df(x_{0}),\xi\rangle
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\max\left\{F_{\epsilon}(\xi)\,\mid\,\varphi(\xi)\leq 1\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\langle Df(x_{0}),\xi\rangle\,\mid\,\varphi(\xi)\leq 1\right\}=\varphi^{*}(Df(x_{0}))

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\min\left\{F_{\epsilon}(\xi)\,\mid\,\varphi(-\xi)\leq 1\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\langle Df(x_{0}),\xi\rangle\,\mid\,\varphi(-\xi)\leq 1\right\}

\displaystyle=-\max\left\{\langle Df(x_{0}),\xi\rangle\,\mid\,\varphi(\xi)\leq 1\right\}=-\varphi^{*}(Df(x_{0})).

Note that optimizers of
F_{\epsilon}
converge to optimizers of the limit, hence, by (
20
),

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\min\left\{\|\pm\epsilon^{-1}(y^{\pm}_{\epsilon}-x_{\epsilon})-q\|\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(Df(x_{0}))\right\}=0.

After Taylor expanding the two terms in the right-hand side of (
176
), one concludes

\displaystyle\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{-2}\left[f(x_{\epsilon})-(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{\epsilon}f)(x_{\epsilon})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\varphi^{*}(Df(x_{0}))-\varphi^{*}(Df(x_{0}))

\displaystyle\quad+2\min\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\langle D^{2}f(x_{0})q,q\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(Df(x_{0}))\right\}

\displaystyle=-G_{\varphi}^{*}(Df(x_{0}),D^{2}f(x_{0})).

∎

Appendix B
Construction of the Perturbed Test Functions

Throughout this appendix, let
\varphi
be a Finsler norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that satisfies the
\zeta
-interior ball condition (
60
) for some
\zeta>0
. Fix a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and let
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
. As in Section
6
, let
\psi_{\nu}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}
be the conical test function associated with
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
.

Towards the construction of the perturbed test functions
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
, let
g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
be the mollified function defined as in Section
6.3
. Define the hypograph
\mathcal{H}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
by

(177)

\mathcal{H}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}=\{x^{\prime}+se\,\mid\,\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\nu-\epsilon,\,\,0\leq s\leq g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})\}.

\mathcal{H}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
is convex since
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
is concave and nonnegative. Accordingly, if
\underline{\psi}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
is the Minkowski gauge of this set

(178)

\underline{\psi}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)=\inf\left\{\alpha>0\,\mid\,\alpha^{-1}x\in\mathcal{H}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}\right\},

then
\underline{\psi}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
is a convex function and it is not hard to imagine that
\{\underline{\psi}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}=1\}
coincides with the graph of
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
close to the flat part
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
.

To construct an admissible perturbation as in Definition
8
, the trick is to identify a nice open cone
\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
containing
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
such that the functions
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
defined by

\displaystyle\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(q)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\underline{\psi}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(q),&\text{if}\,\,q\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\nu}},\\
+\infty,&\text{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

have the desired properties. That is the approach taken below.

B.1.
Construction of an Admissible Perturbation

For any
r\in(0,\nu]
, define open sets
U_{p}(r)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the hypersurface
\mathcal{S}_{p}(r)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\displaystyle U_{p}(r)=\{q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,\mid\,\text{dist}(q^{\prime},G_{p})<r\},\quad\mathcal{S}_{p}(r)=\{q^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})e\,\mid\,q^{\prime}\in U_{p}(r)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)=\{tq\,\mid\,t>0,\,\,q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r)\}.

Note that these sets decrease in
r
with respect to set inclusion.

Proposition 32
.

There are parameters
r_{*}\in(0,2^{-1}\nu)
and
\epsilon_{*}\in(0,\nu)
such that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})
,

(i)

\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\nu}}\subseteq\{\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}<+\infty\}
.

(ii)

If
x\in\mathcal{U}_{p}(r_{*})
, then

\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)=1\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\text{dist}\left(x^{\prime},G_{p}\right)<2r_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle x,e\rangle=g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}\left(x^{\prime}\right).

Furthermore,
\overline{\mathcal{U}_{p}(r_{*})}
is convex.

Taking the proposition for granted for now, here is the proof of Theorem
7
on the existence of admissible perturbations:

Proof of Theorem
7
.

Let
\epsilon_{*}
and
r_{*}
be the parameters of Proposition
32
above, and let
\mathcal{U}_{\nu}=\mathcal{U}_{p}(r_{*})
and
r=2r_{*}
.

For
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})
, define
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
as in (
B
). Otherwise, if
\epsilon\geq\epsilon_{*}
, simply set
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)=\|x\|
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\nu}}
is convex and
(\underline{\psi}^{\epsilon}_{\nu})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
are convex functions, the functions so defined are convex as well. Similarly, they are positively one-homogeneous.

Proposition
32
shows that condition (iii) in the definition of admissible perturbation (Definition
8
) is satisfied with the parameters
\epsilon_{*}
and
r
.

Trivially,
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}<+\infty\}=\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\nu}}
for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})
and these functions are smooth in
\mathcal{U}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
since
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}=1\}
is a smooth graph for any
\epsilon<\epsilon_{*}
.

To conclude the proof of the existence of an admissible perturbation, it remains to show that
\psi_{\nu}\leq\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})
and that
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\to\psi_{\nu}
in
C^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathcal{U}_{\nu})
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. The first follows from the fact that
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\leq g_{\nu}
pointwise for any
\epsilon
, while the second follows readily from the fact that
(g_{\nu}^{\epsilon},Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon})\to(g_{\nu},Dg_{\nu})
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Finally, since
\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
is open and
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\to\psi_{\nu}
locally uniformly as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, the characterization of
\{\psi_{\nu}=1\}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\nu}
in the final statement of the theorem follows directly from that for
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})}
. ∎

B.2.
Proof of Proposition
32

To begin with, it will be useful to recall that
g_{\nu}
and
g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
are defined by

(181)

g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})=\varphi^{*}(e)-\nu+\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(y^{\prime},G_{p})^{2}},\quad g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})=\int_{B_{1}}g_{\nu}(x^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime},

where above
\rho
is a smooth nonnegative function supported in
B_{1}
with integral equal to one. Inspection of the proof of Lemma
11
shows that the following result holds:

Proposition 33
.

g_{\nu}
is
C^{1}
in the open set
\{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,\mid\,\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})<\nu\}
. In particular,
Dg_{\nu}(x^{\prime})=0
if
x^{\prime}\in G_{p}
.

Recall that if
x^{\prime}\in G_{p}
, then
x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
and

\left\langle x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e,e\right\rangle=\varphi^{*}(e).

Thus, by continuity and Proposition
33
, there are constants
r_{*}^{(1)},\epsilon_{*}^{(1)}>0
such that, for each
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(1)})
and each
x^{\prime}\in U_{p}(r_{*}^{(1)})
,

(182)

\min\left\{g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}),\left\langle x^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e,\frac{e-Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})\|^{2}}}\right\rangle\right\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{*}(e)>0.

In the proof of the proposition, it will be useful to study the properties of the maps
\Phi
and
(\Phi^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi:(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(\nu)\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\Phi(t,x^{\prime})=t[x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(x^{\prime})e],

\displaystyle\Phi^{\epsilon}:(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(\nu-\epsilon)\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\Phi^{\epsilon}(t,x^{\prime})=t[x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})e].

The next result shows that these maps are homeomorphisms onto their respective ranges.

Proposition 34
.

If
r\in(0,r_{*}^{(1)})
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(1)})
, then the maps
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\epsilon}
restrict to homeomorphisms of
(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(r)
.

Proof.

For convenience, set
\Phi^{0}=\Phi
and
g_{\nu}^{0}=g_{\nu}
so there is no need to treat
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\epsilon}
separately. Suppose that
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{*}^{(1)})
and
r\in(0,r_{*}^{(1)})
.

It is clear that
\Phi^{\epsilon}
is continuous. Further, the assumption (
182
) implies that, for each
(t,x^{\prime})\in U_{p}(r)
, the vector
x^{\prime}+g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})e
crosses the surface
\mathcal{S}_{p}(r)
transversely. It follows that
\partial_{t}\Phi^{\epsilon}(t,x^{\prime})
is not in the span of
\{\partial_{x_{1}^{\prime}}\Phi^{\epsilon}(t,x^{\prime}),\dots,\partial_{x_{d-1}^{\prime}}\Phi^{\epsilon}(t,x^{\prime})\}
. Since these last vectors are readily shown to be linearly independent, this proves
D\Phi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(t,x^{\prime})
has full rank, hence
\Phi^{\epsilon}
maps open sets to open sets by the inverse function theorem. To prove that it is a homeomorphism, it thus only remains to show that
\Phi^{\epsilon}
restricts to an injective function in
(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(r)
.

To see this, suppose that
\Phi(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})=\Phi(t_{2},x_{2}^{\prime})
for some pair of points
(t_{1},x_{1}),(t_{2},x_{2})\in(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(r)
. By definition, this implies

(183)

t_{1}[x^{\prime}_{1}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{1}^{\prime})e]=t_{2}[x_{2}^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{2}^{\prime})e].

For convenience, denote the outward normal vectors to the graph of
g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
at
x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}^{\prime}
respectively by
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
, i.e.,

n_{i}=\frac{e-Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{i}^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1+\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{i}^{\prime})\|^{2}}}\quad\text{for}\quad i\in\{1,2\}.

At the level of the normal vectors, (
183
) leads to

(184)

t_{1}\langle x_{1}^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{1}^{\prime})e,n_{i}\rangle=t_{2}\langle x_{2}^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{2}^{\prime})e,n_{i}\rangle\quad\text{for each}\quad i\in\{1,2\}.

At the same time, since
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
is concave, its hypograph is convex. Thus, by convexity, for each
i,j\in\{1,2\}
,

\langle x_{i}^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{i}^{\prime})e,n_{i}\rangle\geq\langle x_{j}^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{j}^{\prime})e,n_{i}\rangle.

Combining this with (
182
) and (
184
), one deduces that
t_{j}-t_{i}\geq 0
for any
i,j
, or, in other words,
t_{1}=t_{2}
.

Since
t_{1}=t_{2}
, the identity (
183
) becomes

x_{1}^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{1}^{\prime})e=x_{2}^{\prime}+g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x_{2}^{\prime})e.

Projecting to
\{e\}^{\perp}
, this yields
x_{1}^{\prime}=x_{2}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})=(t_{2},x_{2}^{\prime})
, proving
\Phi^{\epsilon}
restricts to an injective map on
(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(r)
. ∎

The fact that
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)
is an open set follows immediately from the previous result.

Proposition 35
.

If
r\in(0,r_{*}^{(1)})
, then
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)
is an open set.

Proof.

By the previous result, the map
\Phi
maps
(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(r)
homeomorphically onto
\Phi((0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(r))
. At the same time, by definition,
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)=\Phi((0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(r))
. Therefore,
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)
is open. ∎

The next result is the key ingredient in the proof of Proposition
32
.

Proposition 36
.

There are parameters
r_{*}^{(2)}\in(0,2^{-1}r_{*}^{(1)})
,
\epsilon_{*}^{(2)}\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(1)})
, and
a_{*}\in(0,1)
such that, given any
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
and any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(2)})
, if
f^{\epsilon}_{q}:[1-a_{*},1+a_{*}]\to\mathbb{R}
is the function defined by

f^{\epsilon}_{q}(\alpha)=\frac{\langle q,e\rangle}{\alpha}-g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right),

then
f^{\epsilon}_{q}
is strictly decreasing in
[1-a_{*},1+a_{*}]
and there is a unique
\alpha_{*}\in[1-a_{*},1+a_{*}]
such that
f_{q}^{\epsilon}(\alpha_{*})=0
.

Proof.

Define the constant
D\geq 0
by

D=\sup\left\{\|q^{\prime}\|\,\mid\,q^{\prime}\in U_{p}(\nu)\right\}.

By Proposition
33
, there is an
\epsilon_{A}\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(1)})
and an
r_{*}^{(2)}\in(0,2^{-1}r_{*}^{(1)})
such that

(185)

\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})\|\leq\frac{\varphi^{*}(e)}{4(D+1)}\quad\text{if}\quad x^{\prime}\in U_{p}(2r_{*}^{(2)})\,\,\text{and}\,\,\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{A}).

Further, since
\langle q,e\rangle=\varphi^{*}(e)
for each
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(0)=\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
, up to shrinking
r_{*}^{(2)}
further, there is no loss of generality in assuming that

(186)

\langle q,e\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{*}(e)\quad\text{if}\quad q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)}).

Next, let
a_{*}=(D+2r_{*}^{(2)})^{-1}r_{*}^{(2)}
. Observe that if
x^{\prime}\in U_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
and
\alpha\in[1-a_{*},1+a_{*}]
, then

\text{dist}(\alpha^{-1}x^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})+|1-\alpha^{-1}|D\leq 2r_{*}^{(2)}<r_{*}^{(1)}.

Finally, fix
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
. By definition of
\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
, it is possible to write
q=q^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})e
for some
q^{\prime}\in U_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
. Thus, given any
\alpha\in[1-a_{*},1+a_{*}]
, the previous paragraph shows that the point
\alpha^{-1}q^{\prime}
is in
U_{p}(2r_{*}^{(2)})\subseteq U_{p}(\nu)
. In particular, the number
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(\alpha^{-1}q^{\prime})
is well-defined for any such
\alpha
. Differentiating with respect to
\alpha
and invoking (
185
) and (
186
), one finds

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\alpha}\left\{f_{q}^{\epsilon}(\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\cdot\left[\langle q,e\rangle-\left\langle Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right),q^{\prime}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{*}(e)-\left\|Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right)\right\|\cdot D\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}\varphi^{*}(e)\right)<0.

This proves
f_{q}
is strictly decreasing in the interval
[1-a_{*},1+a_{*}]
.

Finally, observe that the
C^{1}
property of
g_{\nu}
implies there is a constant
C>0
depending on
r_{*}^{(2)}
and
\nu
such that if
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(2)})
, then

f_{q}^{\epsilon}(1)=\langle q,e\rangle-g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})+[g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})-g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})]=g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})-g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\leq C\epsilon.

Thus, if
\epsilon_{B},\epsilon_{*}^{(2)}\in(0,\nu)
are given by

\epsilon_{B}=\frac{\varphi^{*}(e)a_{*}}{4(1+a_{*})^{2}C},\quad\epsilon_{*}^{(2)}=\min\{\epsilon_{A},\epsilon_{B}\},

then one finds, for each
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
and each
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(2)})
,

f_{q}^{\epsilon}(1+a_{*})=f_{q}^{\epsilon}(1)+\int_{1}^{1+a_{*}}\frac{d}{d\alpha}\{f_{q}^{\epsilon}\}(\alpha)\,d\alpha\leq C\epsilon-\frac{\varphi^{*}(e)a_{*}}{4(1+a_{*})^{2}}<0.

At the same time, by (
77
),

f_{q}^{\epsilon}(1)=g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})-g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\geq 0.

Therefore, the intermediate value theorem implies there is an
\alpha_{*}\in[1,1+a_{*}]
such that
f_{q}^{\epsilon}(\alpha_{*})=0
. Since
f_{q}^{\epsilon}
is strictly decreasing,
\alpha_{*}
is unique. ∎

The stage is set for the proof of Proposition
32
. The next lemma, which will be used in the proof, follows readily from what has been proved so far.

Lemma 19
.

Let
r_{*}^{(2)}
and
\epsilon_{*}^{(2)}
be the constants from Proposition
36
. If
x\in\mathcal{U}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(2)})
, then there is a unique
t>0
such that

\text{dist}(t^{-1}x,G_{p})<2r_{*}^{(2)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\langle x,e\rangle}{t}=g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{t}\right).

Proof.

If
x\in\mathcal{U}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
, then there is a
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
and an
a>0
such that
x=aq
. Therefore, it suffices to prove the result when
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
.

Suppose that
q\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(r_{*}^{(2)})
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*}^{(2)})
. By Proposition
36
, there is an
\alpha>0
such that

\text{dist}(\alpha^{-1}q^{\prime},G_{p})<2r_{*}^{(2)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\langle q,e\rangle}{\alpha}=g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right).

It remains to show that
\alpha
is unique.

To establish uniqueness, suppose that
\beta>0
is another candidate:

\text{dist}(\beta^{-1}q^{\prime},G_{p})<2r_{*}^{(2)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\langle q,e\rangle}{\beta}=g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{\beta}\right).

Define
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime}),(t_{2},x_{2}^{\prime})\in(0,+\infty)\times U_{p}(2r_{*}^{(2)})
by

x_{1}^{\prime}=\alpha^{-1}q^{\prime},\quad x_{2}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}q^{\prime},\quad t_{1}=\alpha,\quad t_{2}=\beta.

By construction,

q=\Phi^{\epsilon}(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})=\Phi^{\epsilon}(t_{2},x_{2}^{\prime}).

Therefore, by Proposition
34
,
t_{1}=t_{2}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}=x_{2}^{\prime}
. In particular,
\alpha=\beta
. ∎

It only remains to prove Proposition
32
. Toward that end, recall that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\nu)
,
\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
is the Minkowski gauge of the hypograph
\mathcal{H}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
; see (
177
) and (
178
). In the proof, it remains to show that
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)
is convex for suitable choices of
r
, as asserted in the next proposition.

Proposition 37
.

If
r\in(0,r_{*}^{(1)})
, then
\overline{\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)}
is a closed convex cone.

The proof of convexity is tedious, hence it is deferred until after the proof of Proposition
32
.

Proof of Proposition
32
.

Let
r_{*}=r_{*}^{(2)}
and
\epsilon_{*}=\epsilon_{*}^{(2)}
, where
r_{*}^{(2)}
and
\epsilon_{*}^{(2)}
are the parameters identified in Proposition
36
.

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{*})
. Suppose that
x\in\mathcal{U}_{p}(r_{*})
and
\alpha>0
.

Since
x\in\mathcal{U}_{p}(r_{*})
, Lemma
19
implies there is a unique
\beta>0
such that

\text{dist}(\beta^{-1}x^{\prime},G_{p})<2r_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\langle x,e\rangle}{\beta}=g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{\beta}\right).

On the one hand, this says that
\beta^{-1}x\in\partial\mathcal{H}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, hence, as a consequence of the definition (
178
) of
\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, one deduces that
\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)\leq\beta
. On the other hand, the definition (
178
) implies that
\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)^{-1}x\in\mathcal{H}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
. Consider the normal vector
n
to
\mathcal{H}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
at
\beta^{-1}x
. By the convexity of
\mathcal{H}^{\epsilon}_{\nu}
,

\langle\beta^{-1}x,n\rangle=\max\left\{\langle q,n\rangle\,\mid\,q\in\mathcal{H}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\right\}\geq\langle\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)^{-1}x,n\rangle.

At the same time, by (
182
),
\langle\beta^{-1}x,n\rangle>0
holds. Hence the previous inequality directly implies that
\beta\leq\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)
. This proves
\beta=\underline{\psi}_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)
. In particular,
\underline{\psi}_{\nu}(x)=1
if and only if
\beta=1
, completing the proof. ∎

Finally, here is the proof that
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)
is convex. While convexity (for small
r
) is very intuitive, the proof is admittedly somewhat cumbersome.

Proof of Proposition
37
.

The definition immediately implies that
\overline{\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)}
is a cone, hence it only remains to show it is convex. Toward that end, since
\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)
is open, it suffices to prove that, for any
x_{*}\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)
, there is a
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(187)

\displaystyle\langle v,x_{*}\rangle=\max\left\{\langle v,x\rangle\,\mid\,x\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)}\right\}.

Replacing
x_{*}
above by
\frac{1}{\alpha}x_{*}
for some
\alpha>0
does not change (
187
) since
\overline{\mathcal{U}_{p}(r)}
is a cone.
41
41

41

Indeed, the maximum is zero if it is finite.
Thus, up to replacing
x_{*}
by
\frac{1}{\alpha}x_{*}
for some suitable
\alpha>0
, it is enough to show that if
q_{*}^{\prime}\in\partial U_{p}(r)
, then there is a
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(188)

\displaystyle\langle v,q_{*}^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})e\rangle=\max\left\{\langle v,q^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})e\rangle\,\mid\,q^{\prime}\in\overline{U_{p}(r)}\right\}.

Toward that end, fix
q_{*}^{\prime}\in\partial U_{p}(r)
. Recall that since
G_{p}
is convex, the set
U_{p}(r)
is convex. Therefore, there is an
n\in\{e\}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(189)

\displaystyle\langle n,q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle=\max\left\{\langle n,q^{\prime}\rangle\,\mid\,q^{\prime}\in\partial U_{p}(r)\right\}.

In fact, in view of the formula (
181
) for
g_{\nu}
in terms of
\text{dist}(\cdot,G_{p})
, (
189
) holds if and only if
n=-\alpha Dg_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})
for some
\alpha>0
. Hence, for definiteness, let
\alpha=1
and
n=-Dg_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})
from now on.

Let
v=n+ue
, where
u
is chosen such that

0=\langle v,q_{*}^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})e\rangle=\langle n,q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle+ug_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime}),\quad\text{hence}\quad u=-\frac{\langle n,q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle}{g_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})}.

(In view of (
182
), there is no division by zero in the definition of
u
.) As a first step towards (
188
), define
\ell_{v}:\overline{U_{p}(r)}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\ell_{v}(q^{\prime})=\langle v,q^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})e\rangle
so that
\ell_{v}
is precisely the function being maximized in (
188
). Observe that, by the definition of
v
,

(190)

\ell_{v}(q^{\prime})=\langle n,q^{\prime}\rangle+g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})\langle v,e\rangle=\langle n,q^{\prime}\rangle+ug_{\nu}(q^{\prime}).

Further, if
q^{\prime}\in\partial U_{p}(r)
, then the definition of
g_{\nu}
implies
g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})=g_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})
, hence

\displaystyle\ell_{v}(q^{\prime})=\langle n,q^{\prime}\rangle+ug_{\nu}(q^{\prime})\leq\langle n,q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle+ug_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})=\ell_{v}(q_{*}^{\prime}).

This proves

\ell_{v}(q_{*}^{\prime})=\max\left\{\ell_{v}(q^{\prime})\,\mid\,q^{\prime}\in\partial U_{p}(r)\right\}.

Finally, it remains to show that
\ell_{v}
achieves its maximum in
\overline{U_{p}(r)}
on the boundary. To see this, first note that if
u\leq 0
, then (
190
) implies that
\ell_{v}
is convex, and then, by convexity,
\ell_{v}
necessarily attains its maximum on the boundary.

On the other hand, if
u>0
, then
\ell_{v}
is concave. To see that
\ell_{v}
is nonetheless still maximized at
q_{*}^{\prime}
, fix
q^{\prime}\in U_{p}(r)
and consider the function
H_{q^{\prime}}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
given by

H_{q^{\prime}}(t)=\ell_{v}((1-t)q_{*}^{\prime}+tq^{\prime}).

The goal now is to show that
H_{q^{\prime}}
is maximized at
t=0
. Toward that end, observe that since
\ell_{v}
is concave in
U_{p}(r)
, the function
H_{q^{\prime}}
is concave in
[0,1]
. Thus, since the derivative of a concave function is decreasing, it suffices to show that the derivative of
H_{q^{\prime}}
is negative at zero.

Indeed, differentiating
H_{q^{\prime}}
at zero leads to

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\{H_{q^{\prime}}(t)\right\}\restriction_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\langle n,q^{\prime}-q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle+u\langle Dg_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime}),q^{\prime}-q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle n,q^{\prime}-q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle\left(1-u\right).

Observe that, by (
182
),

\displaystyle u=-\frac{\langle n,q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle}{g_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})}=\frac{\langle Dg_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime}),q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle}{g_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})}<1

so
1-u>0
.
At the same time, by the choice of
n
,

\displaystyle U_{p}(r)\subseteq\{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,\mid\,\langle n,x^{\prime}\rangle<\langle n,q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle\}.

In particular,
\langle n,q^{\prime}-q_{*}^{\prime}\rangle<0
and then the previous computations imply that one has
\frac{d}{dt}\{H_{q^{\prime}}(t)\}\restriction_{t=0}<0
, as claimed. ∎

B.3.
Flatness in the Perturbation Argument

This appendix establishes that, in passing from
\psi_{\nu}
to the perturbation
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, some flatness of the face
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
is preserved. The precise result is stated next.

Proposition 38
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
be a Finsler norm satisfying the
\zeta
-interior ball condition (
60
) for some
\zeta>0
. Given a
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and a
\nu\in(0,\zeta)
, let
e=\|p\|^{-1}p
;
\psi_{\nu}=\psi_{e,\partial\varphi^{*}(p),\nu}
be the associated conical test function; and let
(\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
be an admissible perturbation.

Given any
\mu>0
, if
[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}
is the set of points in
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
of distance at least
\mu
from the (relative) boundary, that is,

[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}=\{q\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)\,\mid\,\text{dist}(q,\text{bdry}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p)))\geq\mu\},

then, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\mu)
,

\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q)=e\quad\text{for each}\quad q\in[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}.

In the proof of Proposition
38
, it will be useful to note that if one defines the set
[G_{p}]^{(\mu)}
via
[G_{p}]^{(\mu)}=\{q^{\prime}\in G_{p}\,\mid\,\text{dist}(q^{\prime},\text{bdry}(G_{p}))\geq\mu\}
, then

[\partial\varphi^{*}(p)]^{(\mu)}=[G_{p}]^{(\mu)}+\varphi^{*}(e)e.

Thus, in view of the identity (
103
) relating the normal vector
\|D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\|^{-1}D\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
to the gradient
Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, Proposition
38
follows immediately from the next lemma.

Lemma 20
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
38
, if
g_{\nu}
is defined by (
70
) and
(g^{\epsilon}_{\nu})_{\epsilon\in(0,\nu)}
are the mollified functions defined by (
76
), then

Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})=0\quad\text{for each}\quad q^{\prime}\in[G_{p}]^{(\mu)}\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon<\mu.

To get a sense of why the lemma is true, consider the case when
\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
is
(d-1)
-dimensional, hence
G_{p}
can be regarded as a convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
with nonempty interior. In this case,
q^{\prime}\in[G_{p}]^{(\mu)}
if and only if
\overline{B}_{\mu}(q^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\subseteq G_{p}
. Thus,

\text{dist}(\cdot,G_{p})\equiv 0\quad\text{in}\,\,\overline{B}_{\mu}(q^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d-1},

which implies that

Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})=\int_{B_{1}}Dg_{\nu}(q^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy=0\quad\text{if}\,\,\epsilon<\mu.

Proof of Lemma
20
.

Fix
q^{\prime}\in[G_{p}]^{(\mu)}
and let
k=\text{dim}(G_{p})
. After an affine change of coordinates, there is no loss of generality assuming that
q^{\prime}=0
and
G_{p}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.

In what follows, for a point
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, write
x^{\prime}=(\check{x}^{\prime},\underline{x}^{\prime})
with
\check{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\}
and
\underline{x}^{\prime}\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1-k}
.

Since
G_{p}
has dimension
k
, the inequality
\text{dist}(0,\text{bdry}(G))\geq\mu
is equivalent to the requirement that the closed
k
-dimensional ball
\overline{B}_{\mu}(0)\cap(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})
is contained in
G_{p}
. Thus, by comparing to
\text{dist}(\cdot,\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})
, one deduces that

\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})=\text{dist}(x^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})=\|\underline{x}^{\prime}\|\quad\text{for each}\quad x^{\prime}\in B_{\mu}(0).

After differentiating, this becomes, for
k<d-1
,

D\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})=D\text{dist}(x^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})=\frac{\underline{x}^{\prime}}{\|\underline{x}^{\prime}\|}\quad\text{for each}\quad x^{\prime}\in B_{\mu}(0)\setminus(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})

and, for
k=d-1
,

D\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})=0\quad\text{for each}\quad x^{\prime}\in B_{\mu}(0).

Applying these identities in the formulas (
70
) and (
76
), changing variables via the map
T(\check{x}^{\prime},\underline{x}^{\prime})=(\check{x}^{\prime},-\underline{x}^{\prime})
, and recalling that
\epsilon<\mu
and (
75
) holds, one finds

\displaystyle Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(0)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{B_{\epsilon}(0)}\frac{\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)}{\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)^{2}}}D\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G)\rho(\epsilon^{-1}x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{B_{\epsilon}(0)}\frac{\text{dist}(T(x^{\prime}),\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})}{\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(T(x^{\prime}),\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})^{2}}}D\text{dist}(T(x^{\prime}),\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})\rho(\epsilon^{-1}x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-(d-1)}\int_{B_{\epsilon}(0)}\frac{\text{dist}(x^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})}{\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(x^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})^{2}}}Dd(x^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\})\rho(\epsilon^{-1}x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(0).

This proves
Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(0)=0
. ∎

B.4.
Verifying the Smallness Condition

This subsection proves Proposition
24
. The proof proceeds as follows. First, recall that
M+\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\geq M+\psi_{\nu}\geq u
in
V
. Hence one proceeds to lower
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, subtracting a constant until it first touches
u
at some point
x_{\epsilon}
. That is,
x_{\epsilon}
is nothing more than a point where
M+\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}-C_{\epsilon}
touches
u
from above, where
C_{\epsilon}
is defined by

C_{\epsilon}=\inf\left\{C>0\,\mid\,M+\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)-C<u(x)\,\,\text{for some}\,\,x\in\overline{B}_{R}(x_{0})\right\}.

Let
q_{\epsilon}=\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{\epsilon})^{-1}x_{\epsilon}
be the associated point on the surface
\{\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}=1\}
.

To establish that the projection
q_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
is, in fact, a distance
O(\epsilon)
away from
G_{p}
, the strategy is to show that
C_{\epsilon}=O(\epsilon^{2})
, while such a bound would fail if
q_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
were too far away. More precisely, the proof involves the following two lemmas:

Lemma 21
.

If
x_{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))\setminus\{0\}
and
\nu\in(0,\zeta]
, then there is a constant
c(\nu,x_{*})>0
such that, for all small enough
\epsilon>0
,

(191)

\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{*})\leq\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})[1+c(\nu,x_{*})\epsilon^{2}].

Proposition 39
.

Fix a
\nu\in(0,\zeta)
. There are constants
A(\nu,\zeta)>0
and
\delta(\nu,\zeta)\in(0,\nu)
such that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\nu)
, if
x\in\{\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}<+\infty\}\cap\{\psi_{\zeta}<+\infty\}
and the points
q_{\nu}
and
q_{\zeta}
defined by
q_{\nu}=\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x)^{-1}x
and
q_{\zeta}=\psi_{\zeta}(x)^{-1}x
satisfy

(192)

\max\left\{\text{dist}(q^{\prime}_{\nu},G_{p}),\text{dist}(q^{\prime}_{\zeta},G_{p})\right\}\leq\delta(\nu,\zeta),

then

(193)

\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)\geq\psi_{\zeta}(x)[1+A(\nu,\zeta)\text{dist}(q^{\prime}_{\nu},G_{p})^{2}].

Before going into the proofs of Lemma
21
and Proposition
39
, here is how these two results combine to yield an estimate on
\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon}^{\prime},G_{p})
.

Proof of Proposition
24
.

To begin the proof, fix a sequence
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,+\infty)
such that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G_{p})=\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon}^{\prime},G_{p}),\quad\lim_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}=0.

Since
\overline{V}
is compact, there is no loss of generality in assuming that the limit
x_{*}=\lim_{j\to\infty}x_{\epsilon_{j}}
exists. In particular, since
u
is upper semicontinuous, a classical argument shows that

u(x_{*})-\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})=\max\left\{u(y)-\psi_{\nu}(y)\,\mid\,y\in\overline{V}\right\}.

Since
\psi_{\nu}\geq\psi_{\zeta}
and
\psi_{\nu}(x_{0})=\psi_{\zeta}(x_{0})
by Proposition
12
,

u(x_{*})=M+\psi_{\zeta}(x_{*})=M+\psi_{\nu}(x_{*}).

Thus, by (
96
),
x_{*}\in V\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
and
0<\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})<+\infty
. Further, since
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\to\psi_{\nu}
locally uniformly in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
, the limit
q_{*}=\lim_{j\to\infty}q_{\epsilon_{j}}
exists. Since
x_{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))
, there holds
q_{*}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
.

The considerations of the previous paragraph show that
q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime}\to q_{*}^{\prime}
as
j\to+\infty
and
q^{\prime}_{*}\in G_{p}
. In particular,
\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G_{p})\to 0
as
j\to+\infty
, and, up to passing to another subsequence, there is no loss of generality in assuming that

\sup\left\{\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G_{p})\,\mid\,j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\leq\delta,

where
\delta=\delta(\nu,\zeta)>0
is the constant in Proposition
39
.

Similarly, if
\tilde{q}_{\epsilon_{j}}=\psi_{\zeta}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})^{-1}x_{\epsilon_{j}}
, then Proposition
12
implies that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\tilde{q}_{\epsilon_{j}}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{x_{\epsilon_{j}}}{\psi_{\zeta}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})}=\frac{x_{*}}{\psi_{\zeta}(x_{*})}=\frac{x_{*}}{\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})}=q_{*}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p),

In particular,
\text{dist}(\tilde{q}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G_{p})\to 0
as
j\to+\infty
, and, thus, there is no loss of generality in assuming once more that

\sup\left\{\text{dist}(\tilde{q}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G_{p})\,\mid\,j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\leq\delta.

In particular,
x_{\epsilon_{j}}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
39
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

On the one hand,
x_{\epsilon_{j}}
is a point where
M+\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}-u
is minimized in
\overline{V}
, that is,

M+\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})-u(x_{\epsilon_{j}})=\min\left\{M+\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x)-u(x)\,\mid\,x\in\overline{V}\right\}.

Using Lemma
21
and (
95
), this implies

M+\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})-u(x_{\epsilon_{j}})\leq M+\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{0})-u(x_{0})\leq c(\nu,x_{0})\epsilon^{2}_{j}\psi_{\nu}(x_{0}).

On the other hand, by Proposition
39
and (
95
),

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})

\displaystyle\geq\psi_{\zeta}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})\left[1+A(\nu,\zeta)\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq u(x_{\epsilon_{j}})-M+A(\nu,\zeta)\psi_{\zeta}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G)^{2}.

In particular, combining the two estimates leads to the bound

A(\nu,\zeta)\psi_{\zeta}(x_{\epsilon_{j}})\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G)^{2}\leq c(\nu,x_{0})\epsilon_{j}^{2}\psi_{\nu}(x_{0})

Since
x_{\epsilon_{j}}\to x_{*}
and
x_{0},x_{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))\setminus\{0\}
, Proposition
12
implies
\psi_{\nu}(x_{0})=\varphi(x_{0})
and
\psi_{\zeta}(x_{*})=\varphi(x_{*})>0
, hence this last inequality leads to

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{-1}\text{dist}(q^{\prime}_{\epsilon},G_{p})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\sqrt{\frac{c(\nu,x_{0})\varphi(x_{0})}{A(\nu,\zeta)\varphi(x_{*})}}<+\infty.

∎

Proof of Lemma
21
.

Fix
x_{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))\setminus\{0\}
. Define
q_{*}=\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})^{-1}x_{*}
. Since
q_{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\varphi^{*}(p))\cap\{\psi_{\nu}=1\}
, the inclusion
q_{*}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
holds and, thus,

q_{*}=q_{*}^{\prime}+g_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})e=q_{*}^{\prime}+\varphi^{*}(e)e.

In the proof, there are two cases to consider: (i)
q_{*}^{\prime}\in\text{int}(G_{p})
and (ii)
q_{*}^{\prime}\in\partial G_{p}
. (Here
\text{int}(G_{p})
and
\partial G_{p}
are the interior and boundary of
G_{p}
relative to the topology of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.)

In case (i), since
q_{*}^{\prime}\in\text{int}(G_{p})
, there is a
\mu>0
such that
\overline{B}_{\mu}(q_{*}^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\subseteq G_{p}
. Thus, if
\epsilon<\mu/2
, then
g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(q^{\prime})=\varphi^{*}(e)
for each
q^{\prime}\in\overline{B}_{\mu/2}(q_{*}^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. In particular, for any
\epsilon<\mu/2
,

\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q_{*})=1=\psi_{\nu}(q_{*}),

which implies
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(x_{*})=\psi_{\nu}(x_{*})
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\mu/2)
, stronger than needed in (
191
).

To complete the proof, it remains to consider case (ii). In this case,
q_{*}^{\prime}\in\partial G_{p}
so the convexity of
G_{p}
implies the existence of a
p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that

(194)

\langle p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\rangle\leq\langle p^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{*}\rangle\quad\text{for each}\quad q^{\prime}\in G_{p}.

Considering
g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
and Taylor expanding the function
r\mapsto\sqrt{\nu^{2}-r}
near
r=0
, one finds, for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
,

\displaystyle g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q_{*}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\varphi^{*}(e)-\nu+\int_{B_{1}}\sqrt{\nu^{2}-\text{dist}(q_{*}^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})^{2}}\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq\varphi^{*}(e)-\frac{1}{4}\nu^{-1}\int_{B_{1}}\text{dist}(q_{*}^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})^{2}\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\varphi^{*}(e)-\frac{1}{4}\nu^{-1}\epsilon^{2}\int_{B_{1}}\left[\epsilon^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{*}^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})\right]^{2}\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}.

At this stage, it is useful to note that

(195)

\displaystyle B(q_{*}^{\prime}):=\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{B_{1}}\left[\epsilon^{-1}\text{dist}(q_{*}^{\prime}+\epsilon y^{\prime},G_{p})\right]^{2}\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}>0.

Assuming (
195
) for the moment, one deduces that, for any
\epsilon>0
small enough,

g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q_{*}^{\prime})\geq\varphi^{*}(e)-\frac{1}{2}\nu^{-1}B(q_{*}^{\prime})\epsilon^{2}.

Since
q_{*}\in\partial\varphi^{*}(p)
, the duality identity (
20
) implies
\langle q_{*},e\rangle=\varphi^{*}(e)
, hence

g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(q_{*}^{\prime}\right)\geq\varphi^{*}(e)\left(1-\frac{B(q_{*}^{\prime})\epsilon^{2}}{2\nu\varphi^{*}(e)}\right)=\langle q_{*},e\rangle\left(1-\frac{B(q_{*}^{\prime})\epsilon^{2}}{2\nu\varphi^{*}(e)}\right)

Since
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}Dg_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q_{*}^{\prime})=Dg_{\nu}(q_{*}^{\prime})=0
by Proposition
33
, it follows that one can fix an
\eta>0
such that, for all
\epsilon>0
small enough,

\displaystyle g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{q_{*}^{\prime}}{1+\eta\epsilon^{2}}\right)\geq\frac{\langle q_{*},e\rangle}{1+\eta\epsilon^{2}}.

In particular, as soon as
\epsilon
is small enough, one can invoke Proposition
36
to deduce that
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q_{*})\leq 1+\eta\epsilon^{2}
, yielding the desired estimate (
191
) by homogeneity.

To check (
195
), let
H_{p^{\prime}}=\{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,\mid\,\langle p^{\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle\geq 0\}
and use (
194
) to obtain

\text{dist}(x^{\prime},G_{p})\geq\text{dist}(x^{\prime},q_{*}^{\prime}-H_{p^{\prime}}).

By homogeneity and (
75
), this leads to

\displaystyle B(q_{*}^{\prime})\geq\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{B_{1}}\left[\epsilon^{-1}\text{dist}(\epsilon y^{\prime},-H_{p^{\prime}})\right]^{2}\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}=\int_{B_{1}}\text{dist}(y^{\prime},-H_{p^{\prime}})^{2}\rho(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}>0.

∎

It only remains to prove Proposition
39
. Before delving into the proof, it will be convenient to introduce constants
M_{\zeta}(\delta)
and
R(\delta)
defined by

(196)

\displaystyle M_{\zeta}(\delta)

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\frac{g_{\zeta}(x^{\prime})-g_{\zeta}(y^{\prime})}{\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|}\,\mid\,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\{\text{dist}(\cdot,G)\leq\delta\},\,\,x^{\prime}\neq y^{\prime}\right\},

(197)

\displaystyle R(\delta)

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\|q^{\prime}\|\,\mid\,\text{dist}(q^{\prime},G)\leq\delta\right\}.

Proof of Proposition
39
.

Suppose that
x\in\{\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}<+\infty\}\cap\{\psi_{\zeta}<+\infty\}
and assume that (
192
) holds for some
\delta>0
to be determined below. As in Section
6
, write
x=\langle x,e\rangle e+x^{\prime}
, where
x^{\prime}\in\{e\}^{\perp}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.

To lighten the notation, define
C,\gamma,\ell>0
by

C=\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x),\quad\gamma=\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)^{-1}\psi_{\zeta}(x),\quad\ell=\text{dist}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)},G_{p}\right).

Note, in particular, that
\psi_{\zeta}(x)=\gamma C
. By Definition
8
,
\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}\geq\psi_{\zeta}
and, thus,
\gamma\leq 1
.

By Taylor expanding the function
r\mapsto\sqrt{\nu^{2}-r}
at
r=0
, we have, for
\delta>0
small enough,

(198)

\displaystyle g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(y^{\prime})\leq g_{\nu}(y^{\prime})\leq g_{\zeta}(y^{\prime})-\frac{1}{4}(\nu^{-1}-\zeta^{-1})\text{dist}(y^{\prime},G_{p})^{2}\quad\text{if}\quad\text{dist}(y^{\prime},G_{p})\leq\delta.

This condition fixes
\delta
from now on.

In view of (
192
), the key property (
78
) of
\psi_{\nu}^{\epsilon}
, and the definition of
C
and
\ell
, one can write

(199)

\displaystyle\frac{x_{d}}{C}=g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right)\leq g_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right)-\frac{1}{4}(\nu^{-1}-\zeta^{-1})\ell^{2}.

At the same time, by definition of
\gamma
and
M_{\zeta}(\delta)
,

\displaystyle\frac{x_{d}}{\gamma C}=g_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{\gamma C}\right)\geq g_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right)-M_{\zeta}(\delta)\left\|\frac{x^{\prime}}{\gamma C}-\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right\|=g_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right)-M_{\zeta}(\delta)\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}-1\right)\left\|\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right\|.

Combining this with the bound (
199
), one deduces that

\displaystyle\frac{x_{d}}{\gamma C}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{x_{d}}{C}+\frac{1}{4}(\nu^{-1}-\zeta^{-1})\ell^{2}-M_{\zeta}(\delta)\left\|\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right\|\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}-1\right).

After rearrangement, this becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(\nu^{-1}-\zeta^{-1})\ell^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}-1\right)\left(\frac{x_{d}}{C}+M_{\zeta}(\delta)\left\|\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right\|\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}-1\right)\left(g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right)+M_{\zeta}(\delta)\left\|\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right\|\right).

Reinterpreting this last inequality as a lower bound on
\gamma^{-1}-1
, one arrives at the following lower bound on
\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)
:

\displaystyle\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)=C

\displaystyle=\psi_{\zeta}(x)+(1-\gamma)C

\displaystyle\geq\psi_{\zeta}(x)+\frac{1}{4}(\nu^{-1}-\zeta^{-1})\gamma\psi^{\epsilon}_{\nu}(x)\ell^{2}\left(g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right)+M_{\zeta}(\delta)\left\|\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right\|\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\zeta}(x)\left[1+\frac{1}{4}(\nu^{-1}-\zeta^{-1})\ell^{2}\left(g^{\epsilon}_{\nu}\left(\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right)+M_{\zeta}(\delta)\left\|\frac{x^{\prime}}{C}\right\|\right)^{-1}\right]

Finally, observe that
C^{-1}\|x^{\prime}\|\leq R(\delta)
and

\displaystyle\max\left\{g_{\nu}^{\epsilon}(q^{\prime})\,\mid\,\text{dist}(q^{\prime},G)\leq\delta\right\}\leq\max\left\{g_{\nu}(q^{\prime})\,\mid\,\text{dist}(q^{\prime},G)\leq\delta\right\}

\displaystyle=\varphi^{*}(e),

hence (
193
) holds with the constant
A
given by

\displaystyle A=\frac{1}{4}(\nu^{-1}-\zeta^{-1})\left(\varphi^{*}(e)+M_{\zeta}(\delta)R(\delta)\right)^{-1}.

∎
